# fernjo – Decisions

Log of architecture and technology decisions. Newest entries on top.

**When an entry is due:** choice of framework, library or version, architecture and interface decisions, deliberately rejected approaches, workarounds with consequences – anything you would otherwise have to discuss again in six months.

**Rules:** Do not rewrite entries after the fact. If a decision is revised, add a new entry and append "Superseded by: <title>" to the old one.

<!-- Template:

## YYYY-MM-DD – <Title>
**Context:** <Which problem, which constraints?>
**Decision:** <What was decided?>
**Rationale:** <Why?>
**Rejected alternatives:** <What else was considered and why not>
-->

## 2026-11-27 – M14: owner decisions on the security review

**Context:** The v0.4.0 review left rows without a fix, two fixes that change behaviour, and open owner checks (`Docs/release-checklist.md`, "v0.4.0 security review" and "v0.4.0 owner checks"). The owner was asked in one batch and adopted the recommendations (2026-11-27).
**Decision:**
- **Kept:** row 83 (fernjo fetches the update feed itself, with a size cap; risk: if that fetch failed on a platform, installed copies there could not find updates; checked against GitHub on Linux and Windows, macOS only after the release) and row 87 (with sessions connected, a finished download waits for "Restart to update" instead of opening the dialog).
- **Accepted, with the remaining risk:** row 86 (a feed holder can serve another platform's signed file; the app may then need a manual reinstall), row 91 (a damaged `settings.json` or a downgrade can turn update checks back on), row 97 (a hostile build dependency would be signed), row 98 (the publish job does not re-check what it attaches; accepted for v0.4.0, fix on the backlog), row 102 (anyone with write access, today one person, can push a `v*` tag; the key stays behind the environment's reviewer), row 103 (the download cap overshoots by a read slice), row 104 (rare races around the restart on macOS and Linux), row 106 (a withheld or stalled update goes unnoticed until a restart), row 107 (power loss during a Linux update can leave the AppImage missing), row 108 (with nothing connected the app restarts when the download ends, cutting off unsaved work).
- **Row 100:** the Windows job becomes a required check, on `dev` now and on `main` with the release. A GitHub setting; not yet made.
- **Row 105 (macOS Cmd+Q):** not decided; it waits for the owner's check.
**Rationale:** Each accepted row needs control the update signature already guards against, local access, or a fix riskier than the finding.
**Rejected alternatives:** Reverting rows 83 and 87; changing the publish job before its first run.

## 2026-11-27 – M14: fixes from the v0.4.0 security review

**Context:** The M14 reviews of `v0.3.1..HEAD` (findings from row 81 in `Docs/release-checklist.md`). This entry records the fixes that involve a decision; the table names the commit and test of each.
**Decision:**
- **Signing step (row 81).** `sign` starts the Tauri CLI as `node node_modules/@tauri-apps/cli/tauri.js signer sign`, never through `pnpm`: pnpm puts the project's `node_modules/.bin` first on `PATH`, so a direct dependency declaring a `node` or `sh` program ran with the signing key in its environment, and `pretauri`/`posttauri` scripts would have too. The same applies to any hand signing with the real key. The install stays `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile --ignore-scripts`, which runs before the key is present. Not taken: a separate signer-only `package.json` and lock file (it would also keep other packages off the job's disk, but nothing of theirs runs there). The whole step is pinned line by line, and the job may not change `PATH`, `GITHUB_ENV`, `BASH_ENV` or `NODE_OPTIONS` (`release_signing_step_is_exactly_the_reviewed_one`). A new key pair is generated the same way, `node node_modules/@tauri-apps/cli/tauri.js signer generate -w <folder-outside-repo>/fernjo-update.key`; this replaces the `pnpm tauri signer generate` command in "M13: update signing and the updater plugin".
- **Exit hook (row 82).** `feed::check` replaces the plugin's `on_before_exit` (`AppHandle::cleanup_before_exit`: hides every window, clears the resource tables, which hold the single-instance lock) with an empty hook. On success the plugin calls `std::process::exit(0)` right after starting the setup, so nothing needs cleaning; on a failed start the app stays visible, shows the failure and keeps its lock. Pinned in the source by `the_plugin_exit_hook_is_replaced_so_a_failed_setup_start_keeps_the_app_usable`; the behaviour itself is an owner check on Windows.
- **Feed fetch (row 83).** `feed` fetches `latest.json` itself, stops at 256 KiB (`MAX_FEED_BYTES`; a release's feed is a few tens of KiB, notes are cut at 16 KiB) and hands the parsed value to the plugin's `Updater::restore_update`, which applies the same target selection and comparator and always requires a signed version. The request stays the plugin's: same user agent and `Accept`, reqwest's redirect and system proxy defaults, the same TLS stack (ring provider). `reqwest` and `rustls` are direct dependencies at the versions the plugin already locks; the user agent constant names the pinned plugin version. This is the one place the project reads foreign JSON into a `serde_json::Value`, under that cap. Risk taken: the check, without which no later update can reach a device, now runs through our code; the fake feed covers the logic and the ignored test `the_real_release_host_is_reached_over_https` covers TLS and the redirects against GitHub (run on Linux and on Windows, recorded in `Docs/release-checklist.md` row 83; not on macOS). `write-latest-json.mjs --check` refuses a feed over 128 KiB, half the cap, which installed copies keep for good. Rejected: the plugin's `check` with its unbounded body; a preflight request (the second request could answer differently).
- **No cache on tag builds (row 96).** The release `build` jobs restore the pnpm and cargo caches only when no release tag is set (pull request and branch runs). Caches written by CI on the default branch are readable by a tag run, and what is restored into `target/` ends up in the files `sign` signs. A tag build therefore compiles everything (job timeout 60 minutes; cached builds took 11 to 17). Never run before the v0.4.0 tag in this form.
- **Fork pull requests (row 101).** A `pull_request` run uses the workflow file of the pull request's merge commit, so the upload guard of "M11" row 73 (no artifacts from a fork's build) holds only for a fork that leaves `release.yml` alone; no secret, write token or release is reachable either way. What stands in front is the repository's approval setting for fork runs (all external contributors, read 2026-11-27): keep it, and read a fork's changes under `.github/` before approving a run.

## 2026-11-27 – M14 threat model

**Context:** M14 (`Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-27-m14-security-gate-v0.4.0-design.md`, section 1) reviews what changed since v0.3.1 before v0.4.0, the first version that updates itself. This entry adds to "M11 threat model" what that version brings, and is the reviewers' frame. It describes the code at the start of M14 (`feature/m14-security-gate-v0.4.0`, before any M14 fix). **Open** marks a point with no protection or with one that no decision has accepted; nothing here is a review result or a fix.

**Decision:** the M11 model stays; the assets, assumptions and cells below are added to it.

**Assets.** What each one touches:

| Asset | Secrets | Data folder | Network | Webview |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Update feed (`latest.json`: version, notes, URL and signature per platform) | none | none | one HTTPS GET to the pinned `releases/latest/download/latest.json`, plus its redirects; after the start and every 24 h, and on "Check now" | sees version and notes (cleaned, cut to 16 KiB) in `update-status` |
| Signing key, its password, the pinned public key | the private key and password: the two secrets of the GitHub environment `release-signing` and the owner's own copy, never on a device that runs the app | none | the `sign` job only | none (the public key is in `tauri.conf.json`) |
| Downloaded update bytes (held in memory) | none | none | one GET of the URL the feed names, plus its redirects | sees progress counts only |
| Install handover (NSIS setup on Windows, replaced bundle on macOS, replaced AppImage on Linux) | none | must not change `connections.json`, the vault or `settings.json`; a last sync push runs before it | the sync push | starts it through `update_install` |
| `checkForUpdates`, `skippedUpdateVersion` | none | in `settings.json` | `checkForUpdates` off stops the automatic request | reads and sets both through commands |
| Webview capability (`core:event:allow-listen`, `core:event:allow-unlisten`) | none | none | none | the permissions the page holds besides the app's own commands |

**Trust assumptions (accepted, not defended).** M11 assumptions 1 and 4 apply unchanged (the device and its OS account are trusted; a script that runs in the webview holds the whole command set). Added:
1. The signature of the pinned key is the only thing that makes an update genuine. Whoever holds the private key and can change the feed controls every installed copy that checks.
2. The owner's GitHub account and the repository's administrators are trusted: they can bypass the environment rules (`can_admins_bypass`) and change repository settings.
3. GitHub delivers releases, runs the jobs as the workflow file at the tag says and keeps environment secrets to the jobs the environment admits.
4. The pinned build tooling (actions by commit SHA, `Cargo.lock`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, the Tauri CLI that signs) is trusted at the versions pinned.
5. The first install is by hand from the release page; its integrity rests on HTTPS and, if the person checks, on `SHA256SUMS` and the build provenance. Installers carry no OS code signature.
6. The device's TLS root certificates decide which server counts as GitHub.

**Cells.** One table per adversary; assets not listed are out of its reach.

*1. Hostile or compromised feed (whoever controls what the pinned URL answers)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Downloaded bytes, install | Announce any newer plain version with its own URL and notes, and pair it with a file and a signature. | The plugin verifies the bytes against the pinned key and, with `requireSignedVersion`, against the announced version, before it returns them. Only a strictly newer stable version is offered (`decide::is_offered`). The signature binds the bytes and the version; it names no platform and no file name that the app checks. | Accepted for a feed without the key (assumption 1). |
| Update feed | Answer with a large or slow body; answer nothing; answer an old manifest so that no update is seen. | A check is dropped after 15 s. An automatic check fails silently, a manual one shows a fixed text. | **Open:** the body is read without a size cap; a withheld or stale feed is not detected. |
| Memory of the app | Announce or send a download without end. | Cap of 256 MiB on the declared length and on the bytes received; dropped after 60 s without data. | **Open:** the cap is approximate (overshoot of a read slice); there is no bound on the whole download's duration. |
| Webview | Choose the notes and the version string shown. | Notes lose control characters, are cut to 16 KiB and rendered as text (`{@html` is forbidden by a test); the release page URL is built in Rust from a plain version only. | Accepted: Unicode format characters pass through in notes. |

*2. Network path (between the device and GitHub or the redirect target)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Update feed, downloaded bytes | Watch that a check happens (address, host name, timing); block it; with a TLS root the device trusts (an inspecting proxy), act as the feed. | HTTPS to the configured endpoint (a release build refuses a plain `http` endpoint at start); the request carries no identifier besides the User-Agent `tauri-plugin-updater/<version>`; the signature check as in cell 1. The system proxy is used. | Accepted: blocking and observing; a trusted root counts as the feed (assumption 6). |

*3. Redirect target (the host a redirect leads to; GitHub's asset hosts in normal operation)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Update feed, downloaded bytes | Serve anything for the feed or the file. Redirects are followed to any host, up to 10 hops (reqwest's default; the plugin adds no rule), and the plugin's HTTPS rule is about the configured endpoint. | The signature check, as in cell 1. | Accepted: no host is pinned (the signature, not the host, protects the install). |

*4. Pull request or fork*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Signing key | Run changed workflow and build code in the dry run. | `sign`, `verify-signatures` and `publish` are skipped on a pull request; a fork's run gets a read-only token and no secrets; the secrets exist only in the environment, whose policy admits `v*` tags and asks a reviewer. | |
| Release files | Produce installers in its own run. | A fork's artifacts are not uploaded; nothing is attached to a release. | **Open:** the check of that rule with a real fork pull request has not been run (M11 row 73). |
| Build caches of a later tag build | Write a cache. | GitHub scopes caches written by pull request runs to that pull request (platform behaviour, not tested here). | Accepted. |

*5. Build dependency (a hostile crate, npm package or action at a pinned or updated version)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Downloaded bytes (what gets signed) | Change the binary or installer during `build`; the changed file is then signed and published. | Pinned versions and lock files, `cargo deny`, `pnpm audit`, Dependabot updates merged only by the owner with a cooldown; the build jobs hold no secret and a read-only token. | **Open:** `sign` signs what `build` produced, caches included. |
| Signing key | Run code in the job that holds the key. | `sign` installs with `--ignore-scripts`, restores no cache, compiles nothing and names the secrets in one step's `env:`; what runs there is `pnpm`, the Tauri CLI and the actions pinned by SHA. | Accepted: the Tauri CLI and the runner image see the key (assumption 4). |
| Release files | Run code in `verify-signatures` (the dependency build scripts of the example) or `publish`. | `verify-signatures` has no secret and a read-only token; `publish` compiles nothing and runs the repository's scripts of the tag. | |

*6. Tag pusher (anyone or anything with write access who pushes a `v*` tag)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Signing key | Push a tag whose workflow file says something else. | The secrets are only in the environment `release-signing`: a job gets them only by naming it, from a `v*` tag ref, after a required reviewer approves (self-review allowed). | Accepted: the reviewer must read what they approve. |
| Release and feed | With a changed workflow, write a release with its own files and publish it as latest. | Installed copies take nothing without a valid signature (cell 1); published releases are immutable. The ruleset `release tags` blocks deleting and moving a `v*` tag; it has **no creation rule**, unlike what "M13: update feed, signing job, key lifecycle and scope changes" says (read back with `gh api` on 2026-11-27). | **Open:** who may create a `v*` tag is not restricted beyond write access. |

*7. Repository administrator (or a taken-over owner account)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| All of the above | Bypass the environment rules, change the ruleset, settings and workflows, replace the secrets, sign and publish any file. A published release takes no new files; a new release can. | Account security (outside the repository). A changed secret does not match the pinned key, so replacing it signs nothing installed copies accept. | Accepted (assumption 2). |

*8. Local process of the same user*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Install handover, settings, the app itself | Edit `settings.json` (stop checks, set a skipped version), replace the installed app (per-user install), change the environment the app starts with, swap the temporary setup file before it runs. | Nothing in fernjo. | Accepted (M11 assumption 1). |

*9. Webview script*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted or open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Install handover | Call `update_install`: downloads and installs the offered update; asks first only when sessions are connected. | Only a verified, strictly newer update can be installed; no argument chooses a file, URL or version. The capability grants no `updater:*` permission, so the plugin's own commands are closed. | Accepted (M11 assumption 4). |
| Settings, feed requests, release page | Run checks (manual ones ignore the skipped version), skip the offered version, switch automatic checks off, open the release page. | `update_skip` stores only the version on offer; `update_open_release_page` takes no argument and builds the URL in Rust. | Accepted (M11 assumption 4). |

## 2026-11-27 – M13: final review fixes (run slot, install window, test feed, release notes)

**Context:** The review of the whole M13 branch asked for fixes before the merge. This entry adds to the three M13 entries of 2026-11-26 below.
**Decision:**
- **Run slot.** A check that starts in `Ready` leaves the state at `Ready` and holds its own `checking` flag, so a confirmed dialog still installs the held download, and a late answer of that check never replaces `Installing`. A `SlotGuard` gives the slot back when a backend step panics (`Checking` and `Downloading` return to what they were, `Installing` ends in `Failed`), and `finish_install` clears `AppState.sync_closing` on every way out, a panic included.
- **Residual race.** A check that finds another version while the "Install update?" dialog is open (or that finished just before the confirmation) replaces the held download: the confirmation then ends the sessions and installs nothing, and the new version is offered. The same holds for any confirmation that arrives after the state left `Ready`.
- **Closing while installing.** The window close and an exit without an exit code are prevented while the state is `Installing` (`updater::install_blocks_exit`), and `sync_runner::finish_after_push` does nothing then (`finish_is_dropped`, added 2026-11-27), so neither "Restart now" of a data folder change nor a close dialog confirmed late can cut the replacement of an AppImage or a bundle. Such a request is dropped (a confirmed dialog has already ended the sessions): the install restarts the app itself, and after a failed one the window stays open and the request can be made again. Not covered: the process being killed by the system, and a quit on macOS that never raises `ExitRequested` (see "M10 quit confirmation on ExitRequested", update of 2026-11-27). `Update::install` still runs on the calling thread (no `spawn_blocking`).
- **Skip.** A skipped offer shows no footer marker and Settings words it as skipped; the Rust status keeps `state: available` with `skipped: true`.
- **Test feed.** The owner's update check uses `tools/update-test.conf.json`, merged with `pnpm tauri build --debug --config` into two local builds (endpoint on `127.0.0.1`, the published test key, `dangerousInsecureTransportProtocol`; the merge keeps `requireSignedVersion`), with a hand-written `latest.json` (steps in `Docs/release-checklist.md`). `tools/write-latest-json.mjs` got no override for the download URLs, so the release path has nothing to switch. `update_test_config_changes_only_the_updater_feed` and `update_test_config_is_never_used_by_a_release` pin the file, the merge, that the release workflow passes no extra configuration and that `src-tauri` has no second configuration file. A build made with the file trusts a key whose private half is public and must be removed after the check.
- **gitleaks.** The unused fixture `other-key.pub` was deleted, but its allowlist line stays: `gitleaks detect` scans the history and reports the file there without it.
- **Older-line hotfixes.** `publish` runs `gh release edit --draft=false` without `--latest`, so GitHub can mark the newest-created release as latest. A later hotfix on an older line would then become the update feed, and its older `latest.json` would offer nothing to newer installs until the next release: publish such a hotfix with `--latest=false`, and if the workflow has already published it, move the marker back at once (`gh release edit <newest tag> --latest`). The workflow is not changed now.
- **24 hour clock.** The interval between automatic checks counts awake time on macOS and Linux, because `Instant` excludes suspend there.
**Rationale:** Each fix closes a state in which the app said something untrue or could not be closed; the test feed makes the owner check runnable without adding any switch to a release build.
**Rejected alternatives:** A URL override variable in `write-latest-json.mjs` (a switch in the release path for a check that needs one hand-written file); a separate app identifier for the test build (more configuration to maintain; the steps ask for a backup and a reinstall instead); removing the gitleaks line and relying on a squash merge.

## 2026-11-26 – M13: update signing and the updater plugin
**Context:** Milestone 13 adds automatic updates through `tauri-plugin-updater`, driven only from Rust (spec `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-26-m13-auto-update-design.md`, sections 5 and 6). Before the plan commits to the design, four facts were checked in a scratch copy of the repository and a scratch cargo project (never in the repo); this entry records them with what showed them. The spike changed none of `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json`, `src-tauri/Cargo.toml` and the root `Cargo.toml` in the repository (Task 4 adds the dependency and the configuration).
**Decision:**
- **Key.** The owner generated the update signing key pair outside the repository and delivered only the public key: key id `15493CC4F35D089F`, base64 text `dW50cnVzdGVkIGNvbW1lbnQ6IG1pbmlzaWduIHB1YmxpYyBrZXk6IDE1NDkzQ0M0RjM1RDA4OUYKUldTZkNGM3p4RHhKRmZYQjlQaGNSZVRCNWdKejJvZTg0TWh1bEl3VWorRmJKWCtEM0I1VTlKVSsK`. It is pinned as `plugins.updater.pubkey` in `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` by Task 4. The private key and its password stay with the owner (later also in the GitHub Environment `release-signing`) and are never in the repository, a log or a reply. For key rotation (a new key only works for installed versions that already carry the new public key, so rotate with an overlap release) the command is `pnpm tauri signer generate -w <folder-outside-repo>\fernjo-update.key`; only the public key text is ever shared.
- **Pinned version.** `tauri-plugin-updater` `=2.14.1` (crates.io, 2026-10-28, rust-version 1.90, the repo pins 1.100.0; licence `Apache-2.0 OR MIT`): the newest stable 2.x. `3.0.0-alpha.0` to `3.0.0-alpha.3` are newer on the registry and are not used. It works with the repo's `tauri` 2.12 (locked 2.12.7): adding it to the scratch copy changed no existing crate version. Default features (`rustls-tls` with the `ring` provider, `system-proxy`, `zip`) stay on. Exact pin (`=`) because the installer code is security-relevant and a new release should be read before it is taken.
- **New crates** (`cargo tree --workspace --target all --prefix none` before and after, compared with `comm`): `tauri-plugin-updater 2.14.1`, `minisign-verify 0.3.0`, `zip 9.8.2`, `tar 0.4.46`, `filetime 0.2.30`, `xattr 1.7.0`, `typed-path 0.13.0`, `hyper-rustls 0.27.10`, `rustls-platform-verifier 0.7.1` (and `-android 0.2.0`), `webpki-root-certs 1.0.12`, `system-configuration 0.7.0` (and `-sys 0.6.0`), `core-foundation 0.9.4`, `constant_time_eq 0.6.2`, `simdutf8 0.1.5`, `simd_cesu8 1.2.0`, `jni 0.22.4`, `jni-macros 0.22.4`, `objc2-osa-kit 0.3.2`, `osakit 0.3.1`. Nothing was removed or changed in version. Several are target-specific (Android, macOS).
- **`cargo deny check` in the scratch copy:** advisories, bans and sources ok; **licenses failed on one licence**: `webpki-root-certs 1.0.12` is `CDLA-Permissive-2.0` (through `rustls-platform-verifier`, `reqwest`, `tauri`). Task 4 adds `"CDLA-Permissive-2.0"` to `allow` in `deny.toml` and to `accepted` in `about.toml` (the two lists stay in step); it is a permissive licence for the Mozilla root certificate data, no copyleft. The repository as it is passes (`advisories ok, bans ok, licenses ok, sources ok`).
- **Fact 1: the build needs no private key.** In the scratch copy `plugins.updater` has `pubkey` (first a throwaway key, then the owner's public key) and the one endpoint, `bundle.createUpdaterArtifacts` is unset, no `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY*` variable is set, and `tauri_plugin_updater::Builder::new().build()` is registered. `pnpm tauri build --bundles nsis` finished and wrote `target\release\bundle\nsis\fernjo_0.3.1_x64-setup.exe` (6.58 MiB), no `.sig`, no complaint about a key. That setup `.exe` is the file the plugin takes as is (fact 3): Windows shown; Linux per source, unbuilt. The Linux and macOS builds were not run here (CI or owner check).
- **Fact 2: `Update::download` verifies before it returns bytes.** A scratch program drives the plugin through a mock Tauri app (`tauri::test::mock_builder`, plugin configuration injected) against a local HTTP server, with files signed by `pnpm tauri signer sign --app-version 9.9.9` (throwaway key). Debug build results: correctly signed body `Ok` (300 000 bytes); one flipped byte `Err(Minisign(InvalidSignature))`; other file with this file's signature `Err(Minisign(InvalidSignature))`; signature by another key `Err(Minisign(UnexpectedKeyId))`; empty signature `Err(Minisign(InvalidEncoding))`; announced version `10.0.0` or `9.9.9-rc.1` against a signature bound to `9.9.9` `Err(SignedVersionMismatch { signed, announced })`. `download` buffers the **whole** body in a `Vec<u8>` without a size cap, calls `on_download_finish`, and only then verifies (source: `Update::download` ends with `self.verify(&buffer)?`), so a bad signature is reported after the full download and no bytes are returned. `Update::install` verifies again before it touches anything (`install` calls `verify` first; `download_and_install` relies on the check in `download`). Consequence: the size cap and the stall timeout must be ours; the callback has no way to abort, but dropping the future (for example under `tokio::time::timeout` or a `select!`) does.
- **Fact 3: `install` per OS, progress, builder, redirects** (plugin source 2.14.1, `src/updater.rs`). *Windows:* writes the bytes to a temp `.exe` (or extracts a zip that holds one), runs the `on_before_exit` hook (the plugin registers `cleanup_before_exit`), starts the NSIS setup with `ShellExecuteW` and the arguments `/UPDATE` plus the install-mode flags (ours is `currentUser`) and, with `restart_after_install` (default on), the NSIS restart arguments, then calls `std::process::exit(0)`; it returns only with an error. *Linux:* an AppImage is renamed into a temp directory on the same mount and the new file (a raw ELF or a `.tar.gz` that holds an `.AppImage`) is written to its path with the old permissions; it returns and Rust must restart the app. A deb or rpm install goes through a privileged helper. *macOS:* extracts the tarball (fact 4), swaps the bundle at the running app's path in one step where the file system allows it (two renames otherwise), asks for administrator rights through an AppleScript prompt when the folder is not writable, touches the app and returns; Rust must restart. *Progress:* `download(on_chunk: FnMut(usize, Option<u64>), on_download_finish: FnOnce())`; the first argument is the size of the chunk just received, the second the `Content-Length` of the response (`Some` when the server sends it, `None` otherwise; the spike saw `declared=Some(300000)` and `declared=None`); the callback cannot abort. *Builder:* from Rust `app.updater_builder()` (trait `UpdaterExt`) offers `.endpoints(Vec<Url>)` (a non-https URL fails with `InsecureTransportProtocol` in a release build and only warns in a debug build), `.pubkey(..)`, `.target(..)`, `.timeout(..)` (covers the whole request), `.header(..)`, `.version_comparator(..)`, `.no_proxy()`, `.proxy(..)`. `dangerousInsecureTransportProtocol`, `requireSignedVersion` and `allowDowngrades` are keys of the `plugins.updater` configuration only. In a release build a configuration with an `http://` endpoint and without `dangerousInsecureTransportProtocol` already fails at plugin initialisation (`The configured updater endpoint must use a secure protocol like https`, seen with the scratch program as `--release`). With that flag the plugin accepts HTTP in any build profile, and a debug build accepts it with a warning even without the flag (`validate_endpoints` in the plugin's `config.rs`); the cargo feature `update-test-hooks` has no part in this, it only lets `feed::check` take another endpoint. *Redirects:* the plugin uses `reqwest` with its default redirect policy (up to 10 hops, any host; the plugin has no redirect code of its own). The spike's server answered the feed URL with a 302 to the manifest and the download URL with a 302 to a second path, and both were followed (server log: `GET /latest.json`, `GET /feed/ok.json`, `GET /dl/good`, `GET /cdn/good.bin`), so GitHub's `latest/download` and asset redirects are fine. The User-Agent is `tauri-plugin-updater/2.14.1`; the requests carry nothing else.
- **Fact 3, what the design must respect.** (a) The version comparison is plain semver `>`: a manifest that announces `9.9.9-rc.1` against an installed `0.1.0` **is offered** by `check`, so "a pre-release is not offered" is our own check on `update.version` (or a `version_comparator`), as the spec plans; the same version offers nothing. (b) `tauri signer sign --app-version <version>` writes `version:<version>` into the signature's trusted comment, which the signature covers; the plugin compares it with the announced version whenever it is present, and with `requireSignedVersion: true` in `plugins.updater` a signature without a version is refused. The sign job always passes `--app-version`, and Task 4 sets `requireSignedVersion` to `true`, so a tampered feed cannot pair a new version number with an older signed release. (c) The content of a `.sig` file is already the base64 text that goes into the `signature` field of `latest.json`.
- **Fact 4: macOS tarball layout.** The macOS `install_inner` unpacks every tar entry below a temp directory at `entry.path().iter().skip(1)`, so **the first path component is dropped** and the temp directory becomes the new bundle. The archive must therefore hold exactly one top-level directory `fernjo.app/` with `Contents/...` below it and no `./` prefix (a leading `.` would be the component that is skipped). Pack with `tar -czf fernjo_<version>_<arch>.app.tar.gz -C <bundle folder>/macos fernjo.app` (symlinks and file modes of the bundle must be kept; tar does). This matches the layout in the plugin's own doc comment (`[AppName]_[version]_x64.app.tar.gz` holding `[AppName].app`). Tauri's own tarball was not inspected (no Mac here, `createUpdaterArtifacts` stays off): that the packed bundle installs, and the swap itself, are **owner checks on macOS** (build with `pnpm tauri build --bundles app`, pack as above, sign, update an older build from a local test feed).

**Mock runtime on Windows:** a test binary that hosts Tauri's mock runtime needs an embedded common-controls v6 manifest on Windows, otherwise it exits with `STATUS_ENTRYPOINT_NOT_FOUND` before `main`. `tauri_build` embeds it into the app binary only, so `src-tauri/build.rs` writes the same manifest into `OUT_DIR` and passes it through `cargo:rustc-link-arg-tests` (test targets only, the app binary is unchanged). `UpdaterBuilder::new` is `pub(crate)`, so the plugin cannot be driven without an app; Task 4 uses the mock app (`tauri` feature `test`, dev-dependency) and keeps `feed.rs` generic over the runtime.
**Rationale:** The signature is the only trust anchor (the feed is plain JSON, GitHub redirects to a CDN), so the spike checked that the plugin refuses a body whose signature or signed version does not match before `install` sees bytes, and that the installers we already ship are the update files. Points that differ from a first reading are written down so the plan does not rely on them: the unbounded buffer, the pre-release offer, the version binding, and plain HTTP (a release build refuses it unless the configuration sets `dangerousInsecureTransportProtocol`, which the shipped one never does).
**Rejected alternatives:** A 3.0 alpha of the plugin (not stable); `bundle.createUpdaterArtifacts` (needs the private key in the build job); a range pin for the plugin; a placeholder public key (the spike started on the assumption of a placeholder; the owner handed over the real key while Task 1 was running, so none was needed).

## 2026-11-26 – M13: feed client, download limits and test fixtures
**Context:** Task 4 of M13 adds `src-tauri/src/updater/feed.rs` on `tauri-plugin-updater` 2.14.1 (the facts are in "M13: update signing and the updater plugin").
**Decision:** `feed::check` builds the plugin's updater from the app (endpoint and public key come from `plugins.updater` in `tauri.conf.json`: the one GitHub `latest.json` URL, the owner's key, `requireSignedVersion: true`, nothing else; `requireSignedVersion` is a plain configuration key in 2.14.1) and runs it under `CHECK_TIMEOUT` (15 s); the plugin's own builder `timeout` is **not** set because `Update` inherits it and applies it to the whole download request. An update is returned only when the plugin offers it **and** `decide::is_offered` agrees (pre-release, same or older and skipped versions give `Ok(None)`). A 404 or network failure is `feed_unreachable`, a body that is not a manifest `feed_invalid`, a signature, key or signed-version failure `signature_invalid`; texts are fixed and carry no URL or body. `feed::download` runs the plugin's download future inside a `select!` loop: the callback only sends chunk size and declared length to a channel; the loop drops the future when the declared length or the received bytes pass the cap (`MAX_UPDATE_BYTES`, the check is `within_cap`, `decide::within_download_cap` with the cap as a parameter and a unit test that the two agree) or no chunk arrives for `STALL_TIMEOUT` (60 s); the plugin still verifies the bytes before returning them. The tests pass `Limits` with a low cap and short stall time, so they never send 256 MiB. The endpoint override of `check` exists only with the cargo feature `update-test-hooks` (off by default, enabled for this package's own tests through a self dev-dependency so plain `cargo test --workspace` runs `tests/updater_feed.rs`; `tauri_conf.rs` asserts it is not in `default`). The fake feed is plain HTTP on 127.0.0.1 and works in debug and release test runs because the test configuration sets `dangerousInsecureTransportProtocol` (the shipped configuration never does). **Fixtures:** `src-tauri/tests/fixtures/update-test-key/` holds a test-only minisign key pair (empty password, labelled in its README, not the shipped key; the test `updater_config_is_pinned` checks they differ) and one payload signed with `pnpm tauri signer sign --app-version 9.9.9`; tampered, wrong-key (the app pins the owner's real key) and version-mismatch cases are made at test time, so no `minisign` crate is needed. gitleaks 8.30.1 with the repository's configuration reports no leak for that folder. `CDLA-Permissive-2.0` (`webpki-root-certs`) is allowed in `deny.toml` and `about.toml`.
**Known residual limits:** the plugin reads the feed body with `res.json::<serde_json::Value>()` without a size cap; it is bounded only by our 15 s check timeout and by the pinned HTTPS GitHub endpoint. The download cap is approximate: the future is dropped when the loop sees the chunk that passes the cap, which can be up to a cooperative-budget slice of the plugin's read loop later (a few MiB), so the buffer can overshoot the cap by that much before it is freed.
**Rationale:** The plugin's signature check is the trust anchor; the module adds the bounds the plugin lacks (unbounded buffer, no stall detection, pre-releases offered).
**Rejected alternatives:** Setting the plugin's request timeout (it would cut large downloads); a `minisign` dev-dependency to sign at test time (the pre-signed payload covers the cases); a mock-free test (the plugin's builder cannot be constructed without an app).

## 2026-11-26 – M13: capability narrowing

**Context:** `src-tauri/capabilities/default.json` granted `core:default` (window, webview, app, path, image, resources, menu, tray, event). M13 adds an updater plugin, so the webview should hold only what it uses. The real-app check the plan asked for (`pnpm tauri build --no-bundle`, DevTools port) could not run on the development machine (application control blocks freshly built binaries), so the result below is a static proof.
**Decision:** The capability (`windows: ["main"]`, the only capability file) grants exactly `core:event:allow-listen` and `core:event:allow-unlisten`; no `updater:*`, no `core:default`. Pinned by `capability_has_no_core_default_and_no_updater_permission` and `capability_lists_exactly_the_pinned_permissions` in `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`; the vitest `ui_imports_only_the_tauri_modules_the_capability_covers` (`ui/src/lib/update.test.ts`) fails when a non-test UI file imports a `@tauri-apps` module other than `api/core` and `api/event` or mentions `__TAURI`, so a new window or plugin call is noticed.
**Proof (static):** every Tauri use in non-test `ui/src` is in `ui/src/lib/api.ts`: `invoke`, `Channel` (`@tauri-apps/api/core`) and `listen` (`@tauri-apps/api/event`); no other `@tauri-apps` import, no `__TAURI`, no `data-tauri-drag-region` (the window keeps native decorations), no window or webview API. Full screen of a remote desktop is `SessionStore.remoteFullscreen`, a CSS layout change (`App.svelte` `.shell.fullscreen`); no OS window call, so `core:window:allow-set-fullscreen` is not needed.
- `invoke` of an app command (`generate_handler!`): checked in tauri 2.12.7 `webview/mod.rs` (`on_message`): the ACL is enforced for plugin commands, for any command when the app defines an ACL manifest (`has_app_manifest`), or for a non-local origin. `build.rs` calls `tauri_build::build()` with no `AppManifest`, and the window loads the local app origin, so app commands need no permission.
- `Channel` (`open_session`, `open_quick_session`, `restore_session`): the data path is the command `plugin:__TAURI_CHANNEL__|fetch`, which `on_message` exempts from the ACL (`FETCH_CHANNEL_DATA_COMMAND`); the channel itself is an argument of an app command.
- `listen`: `@tauri-apps/api/event` calls `plugin:event|listen`, and `unlisten` calls `plugin:event|unlisten`; both are plugin commands and need `core:event:allow-listen` and `core:event:allow-unlisten`. `emit` and `emit_to` are never imported by the UI, so `core:event:allow-emit` is not granted. `plugin:resources|close` in `core.js` belongs to `Resource` objects the UI does not create.
- Removed with `core:default`: window, webview, app, path, image, resources, menu and tray permissions; none has a caller in the UI.

**Update 2026-11-27:** the real-app check ran on Windows (owner, installer of a release dry run): tree, SSH output, the `vault-status` event after the idle auto-lock and remote desktop full screen worked. Checking events through "Check now" would prove nothing, because the update screen also follows the command's reply.

- The Tauri build script checks permission identifiers at compile time; `cargo clippy` and `cargo test -p fernjo-app` (all targets) pass with the two identifiers, so both exist.
**Not run here (owner check, evidence goes to the M13 roadmap criterion):** in a `pnpm tauri build --no-bundle` binary with a scratch `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` and the DevTools-port recipe: the tree loads; an SSH session from `tools/ssh-test-server.sh` opens and terminal data arrives (Channel); `sync-status` and `update-status` events arrive; remote desktop full screen still toggles (CSS only, reasoned here, not run); the update window opens. If one of these fails with "not allowed by ACL", add that one permission and a line here naming the UI call.
**Rejected alternatives:** Keeping `core:default` (grants window, path and app APIs the UI never calls); adding `core:event:allow-emit` or window permissions "just in case".

## 2026-11-26 – M13: update feed, signing job, key lifecycle and scope changes
**Context:** Milestone 13 lets the app update itself (spec `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-26-m13-auto-update-design.md`). The facts about the plugin and the feed client are in "M13: update signing and the updater plugin" and "M13: feed client, download limits and test fixtures"; this entry records the remaining choices and what changed against the spec and the roadmap text while it was built.
**Decision:**
- **Feed.** One endpoint, pinned in `tauri.conf.json` (`plugins.updater.endpoints`, `https://github.com/benjo-code/fernjo/releases/latest/download/latest.json`) with the public key and `requireSignedVersion: true`; nothing else is allowed in that block (`updater_config_is_pinned` in `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`). The URL has no template variables (no version, OS or id), so every device asks for the same URL; GitHub sees an IP address and the User-Agent `tauri-plugin-updater/<version>`. GitHub answers the feed and the asset URLs with redirects to its CDN (reqwest's default of 10 hops); the signature, not the host, protects the install, so the "exactly one GET" check counts one request to the pinned URL plus its redirect hops (`check_sends_exactly_one_get_to_the_pinned_path`, `redirect_hops_are_counted_and_followed` in `src-tauri/tests/updater_feed.rs`). There is no runtime override of endpoint or key (no setting, no environment variable); the endpoint override of `feed::check` exists only behind the cargo feature `update-test-hooks`, which is never a default feature and never on in `pnpm tauri build`.
- **Signing job.** `sign` in `release.yml` is the only job that names the two signing secrets (`TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY`, `TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY_PASSWORD`), only in the `env:` of the one step that runs `pnpm tauri signer sign --app-version <version>` on the four update files (Windows setup `.exe`, both macOS `.app.tar.gz`, AppImage). It runs for tag pushes only, uses the GitHub Environment `release-signing`, has `contents: read`, installs with `--ignore-scripts` and restores no cache of its own. `verify-signatures` (no secret, no write token) checks every `.sig` with the example `verify_update_signature` (the plugin's checks, including the signed version), and `publish` (needs it) writes `latest.json` with `tools/write-latest-json.mjs`, re-checks it with `--check`, runs `verify-installers.sh --release` and attaches everything to the draft. A pull request dry run builds without a key and skips the three jobs. `bundle.createUpdaterArtifacts` is deliberately not used: it needs the private key inside the build job (which also runs the smoke test and builds fork pull requests); the installers the app already ships are the update files, and the macOS tarball is made by an explicit `tar` step (single top directory `fernjo.app/`).
- **Key lifecycle.** One trusted key (id `15493CC4F35D089F`); the private key and its password are the two secrets of the Environment `release-signing`, and the owner keeps a copy outside GitHub (reported 2026-11-27; GitHub never shows a stored secret, so that copy is the only way to set them again). They are never in the repository, a log or an artifact. A key lost in both places: installed copies cannot take an update signed with a new key, so users reinstall by hand once (that installer carries the new public key). A leaked key: delete the secrets, then either ship an update that carries a new `pubkey` and is signed with the old key (it works for everyone who updates before an attacker uses the key) or, when write access to the repository or its releases leaked too, ask for a manual reinstall. There is no silent rotation by design (one key, no second trusted key).
- **Owner-side GitHub requirements.** The workflow file a tag run uses comes from the tag itself, so whoever can push a `v*` tag can run a modified workflow; the real protection of the key is therefore the Environment `release-signing` (required reviewer, deployment tag policy `v*`) together with the `v*` tag ruleset that restricts tag creation. GitHub creates a missing environment automatically **without** any protection on first use, so the environment with its policy and the two secrets must exist **before** the first tag (v0.4.0); until then a tag run fails at `sign` and leaves a draft (intended). `sign` also trusts the output of `build` (the existing rust-cache and pnpm caches); that is existing behaviour of the pipeline, not new.
- **Install order.** "Update and restart" goes through the same close confirmation as the other restarts (`CloseThen::Install`, texts from `sessions::close_prompt`): dialog when sessions are connected, then `SessionManager::close_all`, the sync push of `sync_runner::finish_after_push`, then `Update::install`. On Windows the plugin starts the NSIS setup and exits the process; on macOS and Linux `install` returns and Rust calls `request_restart`. `AppState.sync_closing` is cleared whenever an install returns (an error or the macOS and Linux success path), so a failed install never leaves the window unclosable and a retry runs.
- **Checks and bounds.** The automatic check runs 60 s after start (`FIRST_CHECK_DELAY`) and then every 24 hours (`CHECK_INTERVAL`, `updater/schedule.rs`), never under `FERNJO_DATA_DIR`, never with an unusable `settings.json`; only a strictly newer stable version is offered; `checkForUpdates` (default on) and `skippedUpdateVersion` live in `settings.json`; an automatic check swallows every error silently, a manual one shows a fixed code text. The download is capped (`MAX_UPDATE_BYTES`, 256 MiB, declared length checked first) and abandoned after 60 s without data; the plugin buffers the whole body, which is why the cap is ours. Release notes are cut, cleaned of control characters and shown as plain text. The residual limits (uncapped feed body bounded by the 15 s timeout and the pinned HTTPS endpoint; the cap overshoots by a read slice) are in "M13: feed client, download limits and test fixtures".
- **Test support.** The `update-test-hooks` feature is switched on for the package's own tests through a self dev-dependency (`fernjo-app` in its own `[dev-dependencies]`), so plain `cargo test --workspace` runs `tests/updater_feed.rs`; `update_test_hooks_are_not_a_default_feature` guards it. `src-tauri/build.rs` embeds a common-controls v6 manifest into test targets on Windows (the mock runtime needs it). The test key pairs under `src-tauri/tests/fixtures/update-test-key/` and `crates/fernjo-core/tests/fixtures/update-sig/` are test-only and labelled; `.gitleaks.toml` has a narrow allowlist for two public key texts in the second folder.
- **Scope changes against the spec and the roadmap text:** six commands instead of three (`update_status`, `update_check`, `update_install`, `update_skip`, `set_check_for_updates`, `update_open_release_page`); `update_open_release_page` takes no argument and falls back to the generic releases page (`https://github.com/benjo-code/fernjo/releases/latest`, built in Rust) when no version is on offer, because every failed state offers "Open release page"; a separate `verify-signatures` job; `latest.json` is written by Node (`tools/write-latest-json.mjs`), not a shell script; `requireSignedVersion` and `--app-version` were added after the spike; the owner delivered the real public key, so there is no placeholder and no release guard for one; `ci.yml` also runs `cargo clippy -p fernjo-app --lib --bins -- -D warnings` (the shipped feature-off configuration, which no other step compiles).
**Rationale:** The signature is the only trust anchor and the signing key is the one secret worth stealing, so one small job holds it and nothing that runs before it executes with it; the checks that decide whether an update is genuine run in jobs without any secret. Fewer moving parts (one endpoint, one key, no runtime override) leave less to misconfigure.
**Rejected alternatives:** `createUpdaterArtifacts` in the build job; a signing step inside the build matrix; a feed URL with the version or platform in it (leaks them to GitHub for nothing); a runtime-configurable endpoint or key; two trusted keys; a placeholder public key with a release guard.

## 2026-11-16 – M5 restore semantics and backup container
**Context:** Milestone 5 adds import, export and restore (`Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-16-m05-import-export-design.md`; file formats in `Docs/backup-format.md`). The rules for what a restore does to existing data, how a half-applied state is avoided and which container the backup uses are not obvious from the code.
**Decision:** **Replace vs merge:** a full backup (`.fjbk`) **replaces** the tree and all vault entries after a confirmation that names what is lost; the master password and data key stay; the `.bak` files are kept and afterwards hold the pre-restore state. The connections-only JSON and the mRemoteNG import **merge** into a new top-level folder with fresh ids (`Imported from fernjo export (date)`, `Imported from mRemoteNG (date)`; a second one on the same day gets " (2)", " (3)"). A vault that exists must be unlocked even for a backup without entries (replacing it then empties the vault); without a vault file a backup with entries asks for a vault first (`vault_needed`); an unreadable vault is never overwritten (`vault_unusable`). The restore never creates a vault and never reuses the backup password as a master password. **Plan, then apply:** `fernjo-import` does no I/O on app data; it returns a plan (folder with fresh ids, vault entries under the ids the tree references, report) or the decrypted backup. The decrypted plan waits in one pending slot in Rust (`AppState.pending`, `Pending` in `src-tauri/src/import_commands.rs`), so secrets never reach the webview; a new pick replaces it, a successful apply, cancel and every main-window page load clear it. A wrong key or password keeps the picked file so the UI can ask again. **Order and rollback:** import adds all entries with ONE vault save (`Vault::add_entries_with_ids`: ids and names are validated first, so a refused batch changes nothing), then `Store::graft`; if the graft fails, the entries are removed again with one more save (`Vault::remove_entries`). Restore closes all sessions, snapshots the current entries, runs `Vault::replace_entries`, then `Store::replace_tree`; if the tree step fails the snapshot is put back. When the rollback itself fails the errors are distinct and say what state is left: `import_incomplete` (N imported entries could not be removed, remove them in the vault) and `restore_incomplete` (the vault may hold the backup's entries while the tree is the old one; the previous vault content may still be in `vault.fernjo.bak`, unless the failed rollback save got as far as replacing it, so the message says "may"). **Vault `.bak` after an import:** the entries are added with one save, so `vault.fernjo.bak` holds the vault as it was before the import (and, as after a restore, the state from before it). After a rolled-back import the rollback is a second save, so the `.bak` then holds the vault with the imported entries still in it. (Until 2026-11-17 the import saved once per entry, which left N−1 entries in the `.bak`, cost O(N²) bytes and did the rollback with N more saves; see "M5 final review fixes".) **Backup container:** an own format `FJBK` (magic `FERNJOBK`, version 1) built on `fernjo_vault::seal_with_password` / `open_with_password` (Argon2id with the vault's default cost, XChaCha20-Poly1305, the whole header as associated data, KDF parameters bounded like the vault's load bounds, password NFC-normalised), body is versioned JSON. The export password is separate from the master password, at least 8 characters, never stored; a wrong password cannot be told from a damaged file (`wrong_password`). **Dates:** folder names use the local date (`chrono::Local`, `today_local`), timestamps inside files are UTC RFC 3339 with `Z`. **Debug-only dialog override:** the native pick and save dialogs run in Rust (the webview never passes a path); in debug builds only, `FERNJO_DIALOG_FILE` replaces both dialogs with a fixed path (one path serves pick and save), so a DevTools-driven session can run the flows without OS input. Release builds do not read the variable.
**Rationale:** A restore that merged would change ids and could not give an identical round trip; replacing from the JSON export would restore references that mean nothing on another device. Plan-then-apply keeps parser tests free of a data folder and makes a failure leave tree and vault as they were. Reusing the vault file format would couple two version numbers and invite a backup password that is secretly another vault's master password; the small `seal_with_password` reuses the audited key derivation and AEAD code instead.
**Rejected alternatives:** Merging a full backup; replacing from the connections JSON; reusing the vault file format for backups; sending the decrypted plan to the UI and back; a path chosen by the webview; one vault save per imported entry (replaced on 2026-11-17 by `add_entries_with_ids`, see "M5 final review fixes").

## 2026-11-16 – M5 mRemoteNG format facts and mapping
**Context:** The importer (`crates/fernjo-import/src/mremoteng/`) targets the owner's stable mRemoteNG 1.76. The format facts were read from the mRemoteNG v1.76.20 source (`AeadCryptographyProvider`, `Pkcs5S2KeyGenerator`, `XmlConnectionsDecryptor`); mRemoteNG is GPL, so its source was read to learn the format and **never copied**.
**Decision:** **Supported:** `ConfVersion` 2.6 only (`EncryptionEngine` `AES`, `BlockCipherMode` `GCM`); anything else is `unsupported_format` with a message naming what was found. **Out:** 2.7 and 2.8 are pre-release formats with a separate credential repository and no `Password` attribute (the importer says so and stops); legacy files below 2.6 (AES-CBC, possibly whole-file encrypted and not XML) are on the backlog. **Secret layout:** `base64(salt[16] ‖ nonce[16] ‖ ciphertext ‖ tag[16])`, AES-256-GCM with the 16-byte nonce and the 16-byte salt as associated data; key = PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA1 over the UTF-8 password bytes, the salt and `KdfIterations`, 32 bytes. `KdfIterations` (root attribute, default 1000 when absent) must be within 1000..=1 000 000 (mRemoteNG enforces the lower bound; the upper is ours so a hostile file cannot spin the importer), otherwise `damaged`. **Which key:** the default key is `mR3m`; the root attribute `Protected` decrypts to `ThisIsNotProtected` with the right key. Without a typed key `mR3m` is tried and a failure is `key_needed`; a typed key that fails is `wrong_key`. A decryption error on a single node's `Password` is not fatal: the node imports without a password and the report names it. **Full-file encryption:** with `FullFileEncryption="true"` the root's text is one secret whose plaintext is the inner `Node` elements; it is decrypted, wrapped in a `Connections` element and parsed as usual. **Limits:** 32 MiB file (also the read cap of the command layer, which checks the size from the metadata first; a connections export or backup is read up to 64 MiB, `MAX_OTHER_BYTES`, error `too_large`, so an encrypted backup above 64 MiB never reaches `open`, and the connections importer's own limit of 32 MiB gives `invalid` for a file between 32 and 64 MiB), UTF-8 with optional BOM (UTF-16 is refused with a hint), 59 container levels (the core's tree depth limit of 60 minus the new import folder, see "M5 final review fixes"), 100 000 nodes (`too_large`, `too_complex`), and a work budget of 20 000 000 PBKDF2 iterations in all (`too_complex`, see the same entry). **roxmltree 0.21.2:** DTDs are refused (`allow_dtd: false`, so there are no entity expansions) and a DOM node limit of 2 000 000 is set. The parser's duplicate-attribute check is quadratic in the number of attributes of one element; this was first accepted ("the owner picks the file") and is superseded by the pre-parse scan in "M6 security review: import limits". **Mapping (as implemented):** the root is not imported, its children go into one new top-level folder `Imported from mRemoteNG (YYYY-MM-DD)`; `Type="Container"` is a folder (kept even if every child is skipped); `Connection` (also a missing `Type`) with `Protocol` `SSH2` or `RDP` (case-insensitive) is an SSH or RDP connection; `Name` (empty becomes "Unnamed" and a problem line), `Descr`, `Hostname` (empty is a problem line) and document order are kept, `Id`, `Expanded`, icons and panels are not. Inheritance is mapped faithfully per setting, folders included: `Port` is unset when `InheritPort` is true or the value is missing, and a port that is 0, above 65535 or not a number is a problem line and unset (`port_invalid` is the reader's field for the not-a-number case); `Username` is unset when `InheritUsername` is true or the value is empty. A password becomes a vault entry only when `InheritPassword` is false and the decrypted password is not empty; the entry carries the node's username and domain when their inherit flag is false and the value is not empty, and the node references it as `settings.credential`. **Deduplication:** entries with the same (username, domain, password) are one shared entry (the lookup uses a per-process keyed hash only to narrow candidates; a hit is confirmed by comparing the real passwords). **Empty-own-password rule:** `InheritPassword="false"` with an empty password means "no password" in mRemoteNG, but an unset fernjo setting inherits; the connection is therefore listed under partly mapped when a folder above sets a credential ("now uses the credential of folder X"), and likewise for an own empty username; no empty-secret entries are invented. A domain without a password to attach it to is partly mapped (fernjo has no domain setting before M8). Entry names are `username` or `username@domain`, with " (2)" and so on when taken; an entry without a username is named after the first node that uses it. **Report:** counts (folders, SSH, RDP, credentials, shared credentials, skipped, partial, problems) and three lists, each capped at 200 lines plus an "and N more" line, each line cut to 200 characters; names, paths and protocols only, never a password. **Skipped** (listed with path and protocol or type): protocols other than SSH2 and RDP (`SSH1`, `VNC`, `Telnet`, `Rlogin`, `RAW`, `HTTP(S)`, `ICA`, `IntApp`, or none) and nodes with an `Unknown` `Type` including everything below them. **Partly mapped:** a non-empty `PuttySession` other than `Default Settings`, one aggregate line that RDP display, redirection and gateway settings are not carried (when there are RDP connections), the empty-own-value cases and the domain case above. **Problems:** a port that is invalid or out of range, a missing name or host, a password that could not be decrypted (the line says "imported without one", or, when a folder above sets a credential and the node therefore inherits it, that it now uses that credential). **Import without passwords** has its own report, worked out with the plan before the line caps: no credentials, no "now uses the credential of folder X" lines, one line (first in the list, so the cap never cuts it) saying how many stored password references were left out. **Wording:** the mRemoteNG file errors say "key" (`key_needed`, `wrong_key`: the field is labelled "Custom key"); the backup errors keep "password". **Independent vectors:** the known-answer ciphertexts and the whole-file fixtures (`crates/fernjo-import/tests/fixtures/`) are produced by `tools/mremoteng-vectors.cs`, a file-based .NET script using BouncyCastle.Cryptography (not the RustCrypto code under test), and are committed, so CI never needs dotnet. **Open (not verified):** the fixtures' attribute set is written from the documented format; the optional validation against mRemoteNG's `mremoteng_confcons_v2_6.xsd` was **not** done (the script has no `--xsd` option) and no real exported `confCons.xml` from the owner has been read by the importer. Verify when the XSD or a real file is available: validate the committed fixtures against the XSD (for example `xmllint --noout --schema mremoteng_confcons_v2_6.xsd crates/fernjo-import/tests/fixtures/default-key.xml`; the full-file fixture has no node elements to validate against the XSD) and import the real file through the Import & backup dialog.
**Rationale:** The owner runs the stable 1.76 line, which writes 2.6; supporting only what was verified against the source keeps the importer small and its errors honest. A faithful per-setting mapping gives every child exactly what it got in mRemoteNG; the report lists everything that could not be carried, so nothing is dropped silently.
**Rejected alternatives:** Supporting 2.7/2.8 (pre-release, different credential model, nothing to test against); legacy CBC (backlog); simplifying inheritance (a child would silently change its username or port); inventing empty-secret entries to express "no password"; copying mRemoteNG source or its fixtures (GPL); a hand-written XML scanner.

## 2026-11-17 – M5 final review fixes
**Context:** The review of the whole M5 branch found seven real problems (dead tabs after a restore, a depth limit looser than what `connections.json` loads, a decrypted plan outliving the dialog, a hostile `confCons.xml` hanging the importer, an import that blocked the main thread and saved the vault once per entry, a newer backup reported as damaged, an unproven round-trip criterion) and a handful of smaller ones. This entry records the decisions behind the fixes.
**Decision:** **Tree depth:** `fernjo_core::MAX_TREE_DEPTH` = 60 levels (top level is 1). `serde_json` reads `connections.json` with a recursion limit of 128 nested JSON levels; each node costs two (object, `children` array) and a connection's `settings` one more, so a connection at level 62 still loads, both from the file and from a backup body (`the_depth_limit_leaves_a_margin_below_what_the_loader_accepts` in `store.rs` finds the edge by parsing, so a change of the format is noticed). 60 keeps a margin of two levels. `Tree::graft` and `Store::replace_tree` refuse more with `TreeError::TooDeep` (code `too_deep`) and write nothing; `backup::open` calls a body tree deeper than that `damaged` (deeper than the JSON parser accepts is `damaged` anyway); the mRemoteNG reader limits containers to 59 levels (`too_complex`) and the connections importer the file's nodes to 59 (`invalid`), so that the new top-level import folder fits. **Not covered at the time, follow-up (closed in "M6 security review: import limits"):** creating or moving nodes by hand (M2) was not checked against the limit, so a tree built by hand beyond 60 levels still saved, could fail to load past about level 62, and its backup would be refused at restore as damaged. `Tree::graft` collects the existing ids in one `HashSet` (O(tree + subtree) instead of a tree search per grafted node). **Work budget:** every `Password` attribute of a confCons file has its own salt and costs one PBKDF2 derivation of `KdfIterations` iterations, so a file of 100 000 nodes at 1 000 000 iterations would occupy a worker for hours (`import_cancel` cannot stop a running derivation). Before any password is decrypted the reader counts the `Password` attributes (a cheap pass that over-counts) and refuses a file whose total, `(key check + full-file body + passwords) x KdfIterations`, exceeds 20 000 000 iterations (`MAX_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS`) as `too_complex` with a message naming the numbers; a full-file body is counted again after it is decrypted. Hundreds of passwords at 1000 iterations (the normal case) use about 2 % of it. **Dev profile:** a debug build of the PBKDF2 loop took minutes for a million iterations; the generic hash code is compiled into the crate that uses it, so `[profile.dev.package.fernjo-import]` and `sha1` are set to `opt-level = 3` in the root `Cargo.toml` (a million iterations then take about 0.3 s; only these two were needed, the other RustCrypto crates the review named add nothing measurable). **Many entries, one save:** `Vault::add_entries_with_ids` adds a batch with one save and `Vault::remove_entries` removes one with one save (rollback); the import path uses both, and `import_apply` is a plain `#[tauri::command]` on an `async fn` that moves the work into `spawn_blocking` (state from the `AppHandle`, like `export_backup`), so a big import does not freeze the window. **Newer backup:** `backup::open` maps `DocumentError::NewerSchema` of the `connections` document to `NewerVersion { found }` (the found number, capped at `u16::MAX`) instead of `damaged`. **Pending plan:** the dialog's `close()` always calls `import_cancel` (harmless when nothing is pending), and `ImportStore` calls it again when a pick, inspect or failed apply finishes after a cancel and nothing newer is going on (a late result must not cancel a newer import). **Restore and tabs:** Rust closes every session without an event, so the UI's snapshot callback gets a `restored` flag and the app calls `sessions.closeAll()` for a restore. **Export:** the password is wrapped in a `SecretString` before the dialog; the password rule, the vault state and the store are checked before the save dialog opens (`export_backup_precheck`); a target inside the data folder (symbolic links and `..` resolved, a not yet existing sub folder included) is refused as `export_target_not_allowed`, so an export can never replace `vault.fernjo` or `connections.json`. **Secret guards:** the no-secret tests now also search for the two other imported fixture passwords (the one with XML and shell metacharacters and the Unicode one, each whole and as the part no JSON or `Debug` escaping changes) and use the sentinel wrong key of the table entry.
**Rationale:** Each rule sits where the thing it protects is decided: depth in the core, so no importer, restore or future caller can write a tree that cannot be read again; the budget in the reader, before the expensive step; one vault save per batch because a vault save is a full serialise, encrypt, write and copy.
**Rejected alternatives:** Making hand-made trees obey the depth limit in this wave (changes M2 behaviour); a time limit or a cancel flag for the PBKDF2 loop (the count is known in advance, so refusing is simpler and cheaper); a lower `KdfIterations` cap (legitimate files use up to a few hundred thousand); `opt-level = 3` for the whole workspace in dev builds.

## 2026-11-16 – M5 dependencies
**Context:** The new crate `fernjo-import` and the import commands need XML parsing, the mRemoteNG crypto primitives and a date source. `cargo deny check` must stay green.
**Decision:** Workspace dependencies in `Cargo.toml` (versions as in `Cargo.lock`): `roxmltree` 0.21.2 (read-only DOM, no external entities), `aes-gcm` 0.11.1 (feature `zeroize`; AES-256-GCM with a 16-byte nonce through `AesGcm<Aes256, U16>`), `pbkdf2` 0.13.0 (feature `hmac`), `sha1` 0.11.0 (PBKDF2's PRF), `base64` 0.23.1 (an older 0.21.7 is also in the lock through other dependencies). `unicode-normalization` 0.1.26 was already a workspace dependency (NFC of the backup password and its length check). `chrono` 0.4.45 was already in the lock (through russh-util) and is declared in `src-tauri/Cargo.toml` with default features off and the `clock` feature, for the local date of the folder names (`today_local`) and the UTC timestamps. The .NET script `tools/mremoteng-vectors.cs` uses `BouncyCastle.Cryptography` 2.6.2 (script only; not part of the build or CI).
**Rationale:** RustCrypto crates match the vault's choice and need no new vetting category. `chrono` was already compiled into the tree.
**Rejected alternatives:** `time` for the local date (`OffsetDateTime::now_local` fails on Unix once the process is multithreaded); a hand-written XML scanner; BouncyCastle-style bindings in Rust (the .NET script is the independent implementation precisely because it is not our code).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 SSH behaviour: known_hosts, timeouts, keepalive, agent limit
**Context:** Details of the SSH layer (`crates/fernjo-ssh`) that are not obvious from the code and that the owner would otherwise ask about again.
**Decision:** **`known_hosts`:** fernjo has its own file `<data folder>/known_hosts` (OpenSSH format, so `ssh-keygen -F` and `-R` work on it; never `~/.ssh/known_hosts`, which fernjo neither reads nor writes). Matching is a byte-based matcher in `known_hosts.rs`, not `russh::keys::known_hosts::known_host_keys_path` (russh 0.65.1): that helper fails the whole lookup on one unparsable key line, does not count comment lines in the line numbers it reports and mishandles CRLF and a leading BOM, and the file is shared with OpenSSH where all of that occurs. Supported patterns are exact names (`host`, `[host]:port`) and hashed `|1|` entries; the host name is lowercased before matching and writing. A rewrite only touches the lines fernjo owns, every other byte stays, and a `.bak` is kept. **Limitation:** `@revoked` and `@cert-authority` lines (and wildcard or negated patterns) are never honoured and never touched: a revoked key in a hand-edited file is not rejected by fernjo. **Connect timeout:** `ConnectParams.connect_timeout` (default 15 s) bounds each wait for the server separately (TCP connect and handshake, each authentication round trip, the PTY request), and the clock is paused while a user prompt is open; it is deliberately not one total budget, because a person typing a password or comparing a fingerprint must not burn the budget of the network. **Agent sign step:** it shares that timeout, so an agent that needs a touch or a confirmation for each signature (hardware key, `ssh-add -c`) times out after 15 s of waiting; recorded as a known limitation and in the backlog. **Keepalive:** `keepalive_interval` is the connection's value (default 30 s, 0 turns it off) with russh's `keepalive_max: 3`; russh ends the connection on the 4th unanswered tick (it counts after sending), so a dead peer is noticed after about 4 intervals (about 120 s by default), not 3.
**Rationale:** An own, tolerant matcher keeps a user-edited file usable and keeps unknown lines intact; per-stage timeouts match what the user perceives as "the server is not answering".
**Rejected alternatives:** The russh helper (see above); reading `~/.ssh/known_hosts` (fernjo's data folder is meant to be self-contained and syncable, and a rewrite of the shared file is risky); one total connect budget (breaks with slow typists); honouring `@revoked` now (needs a policy for what fernjo does with a revoked key it must not delete).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 flow control numbers
**Context:** `yes` or `cat` of a big file must not freeze the UI or make Ctrl+C wait behind megabytes of queued output. Complements the flow-control paragraph of "M4 session IPC". Requirement: after `yes` has run for 5 s, Ctrl+C returns the prompt within 1 s.
**The mechanism (corrected):** the echo of Ctrl+C is ordered behind every byte of output the server has already sent, so all of it must drain through xterm.js's parser before the prompt can appear; the latency is (data in flight between the server and the parser) divided by the parser's speed, plus the round trip. The data in flight is the sum of every buffer in the pipe: the SSH window (the server may send this much before russh sends a window adjust; russh adjusts when it *receives* a packet, not when the consumer has read it), russh's per-channel packet buffer (`channel_buffer_size`, 32 KiB packets), the event queue of `fernjo_ssh` (`EVENT_QUEUE`), the unacknowledged bytes in the webview (`HIGH_WATER`) and one more batch (`MAX_BATCH`). The first version did not look at the first two and documented only the last three.
**Before (russh defaults plus the first numbers):** window 2 MiB + channel buffer 100 x 32 KiB = 3.1 MiB + `EVENT_QUEUE` 64 x 32 KiB = 2 MiB + `HIGH_WATER` 256 KiB + one 64 KiB batch, about 7.4 MiB in flight (`LOW_WATER` 64 KiB, `MAX_BATCH` 64 KiB).
**Decision (after):** `client_config` in `crates/fernjo-ssh/src/session.rs` sets `window_size` 512 KiB and `channel_buffer_size` 4 (packet size stays 32 KiB); `EVENT_QUEUE` 4 (also in `session.rs`); `session_flow.rs`: `HIGH_WATER` 128 KiB, `LOW_WATER` 32 KiB (the forwarder pauses above the first and resumes at or below the second); `sessions.rs`: `MAX_BATCH` 32 KiB (one SSH packet), batches every 16 ms. Data in flight: 512 + 4 x 32 + 4 x 32 + 128 + 32 KiB, about 0.9 MiB, a tenth of before.
**Measured** (Windows 11, release binary, ten tabs, `htop` in one, `yes` in another for 5 s, Ctrl+C in the `yes` tab, WebGL renderer, time from the key event to the first compositor frame that shows the new screen, six rounds per run, fresh test container per run): single tab, earlier tasks: 80 to 165 ms. Ten tabs, before: 259 to 1386 ms over eight runs of six rounds on the old build (8 of 48 rounds above 1 s; the first measurement gave 296 to 1670 ms). Ten tabs, after, two runs of six rounds: 487, 476, 471, 457, 489, 379 ms and 412, 368, 545, 281, 479, 331 ms (all twelve under 1 s, the worst 545 ms). Smaller pipes tried with the same method (one run each) gave 148 to 257 ms (window 128 KiB, one buffered packet, one queued event, `HIGH_WATER` 128 KiB) and 198 to 260 ms (the same with `HIGH_WATER` 32 KiB), so the in-flight bound is what the latency follows; the chosen numbers keep more headroom for throughput. (Measurement note: an early series of these runs accidentally drove the old binary, because the copies of the app were named differently from the process the script stopped and the single-instance plugin let the old one keep running; the numbers above for "after" and the smaller pipes are from runs where the new binary's process was confirmed.)
**Trade-off (throughput):** in-app throughput is limited by `HIGH_WATER` divided by the round trip of the acknowledgement (xterm parse, `session_ack` invoke, wake-up of the forwarder) and, at the top, by xterm.js itself. `yes` with 92-byte lines (50 MB, release binary, WebGL): 2.4 MB/s before (`HIGH_WATER` 256 KiB), 1.3 to 1.4 MB/s after (128 KiB), 0.8 MB/s with 32 KiB. A raw 30 MB transfer through `fernjo-ssh` alone (test `bulk_output_throughput_stays_high_with_the_small_window`) runs at 3 to 6 MB/s on this machine with the old and the new window alike, so the Docker loopback and the cipher are the limit there and the window is not; the figure of 15 MB/s that was proposed as a floor is not reachable on this setup with either configuration. The SSH window also bounds throughput on a slow link (window divided by the round trip: 512 KiB at 50 ms is about 10 MB/s, at 200 ms about 2.5 MB/s), enough for a terminal. If big `cat`s on fast links matter more than Ctrl+C under `yes`, raise `HIGH_WATER` first (the in-app limit) and re-measure.
**Rationale:** The requirement is about interactivity, which the bound on data in flight decides; bulk throughput of a terminal is bounded by xterm.js anyway.
**Rejected alternatives:** Dropping output when behind (corrupts escape sequences); no flow control (memory grows with the slowest renderer); discarding queued output when Ctrl+C is typed (the UI cannot know what the program will still print).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 clipboard path
**Context:** The terminal needs copy and paste in a Tauri webview on three engines. `navigator.clipboard.readText()` in WebView2 shows a permission prompt and the promise stays pending until the user answers, so a naive paste hangs.
**Decision:** Copy uses `navigator.clipboard.writeText` from the user gesture (works without a prompt). Keyboard paste (Ctrl+Shift+V, Cmd+V) is not intercepted: the native `paste` event delivers the text and is passed to xterm.js, so no permission is involved. The context-menu "Paste" has no paste event and uses `readText()` with a 2.5 s timeout; on timeout it gives up silently and the keyboard shortcut remains the dependable path. **Not done:** Rust clipboard commands. The usual crate `arboard` pulls `clipboard-win` on Windows, which is BSL-1.0, and `deny.toml` does not allow BSL-1.0; allowing a new licence is a policy decision for the owner (backlog: "Native clipboard commands").
**Rationale:** The browser path covers the daily cases without new dependencies, permissions or licences.
**Superseded in part (M10):** the context-menu Paste now reads through the native `clipboard_read_text` command, see "M10 native clipboard: BSL-1.0 allowed"; copy and keyboard paste are unchanged.
**Rejected alternatives:** `tauri-plugin-clipboard-manager` (needs a capability for the webview and the same crate family); calling `readText()` without a timeout (hangs the menu item on WebView2).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 terminal: xterm.js versions, WebGL policy, no lazy import
**Context:** M4 adds the terminal to the UI.
**Decision:** `@xterm/xterm` 6.0.0 with the addons `@xterm/addon-fit` 0.11.0, `@xterm/addon-webgl` 0.19.0, `@xterm/addon-search` 0.16.0 and `@xterm/addon-web-links` 0.12.0, pinned exactly in `package.json`. **WebGL** is attached only to the terminal of the visible tab and disposed when the tab goes to the background (browsers cap the number of WebGL contexts at about 16 per page, and ten or more tabs are expected); it also falls back to the DOM renderer when a context is lost or cannot be created (WebKitGTK). **No lazy import:** the terminal is part of the main chunk, which grew from 123 kB to 632 kB (173 kB gzip). This is a local app that loads the bundle from disk, so the cost is parse time, not download; a lazy chunk would add a loading state to the first tab for no measurable gain. `build.chunkSizeWarningLimit` in `ui/vite.config.ts` is raised so the build does not warn about this known size.
**Rationale:** One WebGL context at a time stays far below every limit and costs nothing for hidden tabs.
**Rejected alternatives:** WebGL in every tab (context limit); `import()` of the terminal (see above); the canvas addon (removed in xterm.js 6).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 session IPC: raw output channel, raw input body, flow control, opener plugin
**Context:** Terminal sessions stream bytes both ways and ask the user questions while connecting. The Tauri 2 API (`tauri` 2.12.5 as in `Cargo.lock`, source read in the cargo registry before use) decides what the wire can look like.
**Decision:** **Output:** one `tauri::ipc::Channel<InvokeResponseBody>` per session carries both kinds of messages: terminal output as `InvokeResponseBody::Raw` (the JS handler receives an `ArrayBuffer`, via `new Uint8Array(...).buffer` below 1 KiB or the fetch path above it) and events as `InvokeResponseBody::Json` (the handler receives the parsed object). The UI tells them apart with `instanceof ArrayBuffer`; the JS `Channel` re-orders by the message index Tauri adds, so output and events stay in order. A typed `Channel<SomeEnum>` would force output through JSON (a number array), which is why the untyped body is used.
