The wire contract is documented at the top of `src-tauri/src/sessions.rs`; the Rust event type is `UiEvent` (the name `SessionEvent` is taken by `fernjo_ssh`). **Input:** `session_input` takes `tauri::ipc::Request` and reads `InvokeBody::Raw` (the webview invokes with a `Uint8Array`), the session id in the header `x-fernjo-session`; a JSON body or a missing header is `invalid_request`. Input, resizes and close go through one ordered queue per session (an unbounded `mpsc`, drained by a writer task) because separate async commands may overtake each other. **Flow control:** output is batched (16 ms or 32 KiB, in messages of at most 32 KiB); the forwarder counts sent bytes, the UI reports written bytes with `session_ack`; above 128 KiB unacknowledged it stops reading from the SSH layer until the count falls to 32 KiB (hysteresis). The SSH layer's event queue (4 events) and russh's channel buffer (4 packets) are bounded, and the SSH window is 512 KiB, so the server is held back and memory stays bounded; the full budget, the measurements and the trade-offs are in "M4 flow control numbers" (the numbers first written here, 256 KiB / 64 KiB / 64 KiB and the 2 MiB window and 100-packet buffer of russh's defaults, were too large to meet the Ctrl+C requirement with ten tabs). **Closing:** `close_session` is silent (the channel is muted first), answers every open prompt with the safe answer (reject, no secret), aborts the connect task and drops the entry. **Plan resolution** happens synchronously inside `open_session`, `resume_session` and `reconnect_session` (the spawned task needs no `AppState`); reconnect resolves again from the tree as it is, a deleted node ends as `disconnected` with "the connection no longer exists". **Opener plugin:** `tauri-plugin-opener` 2 (2.7.3 as resolved in `Cargo.lock`), registered after the dialog plugin and used only through its Rust API (`OpenerExt::opener().open_url`) in the command `open_external_url`, which accepts only absolute `http`/`https` URLs with a host (else `invalid_url`). `capabilities/default.json` stays `["core:default"]` (guarded by `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`), so the webview cannot call the plugin's own commands or open arbitrary paths and schemes.
**Rationale:** Raw binary in both directions avoids JSON-encoding every byte; one channel keeps events and output ordered; routing the link opening through a validated Rust command keeps the webview's permissions at the minimum, like the dialog plugin.
**Rejected alternatives:** Two channels (events and output; ordering between them is lost); a typed JSON channel for output (large and slow); `Vec<u8>` as a JSON array for input (same cost, and one invoke per keystroke batch); `@tauri-apps/plugin-opener` from the webview (needs a capability and accepts any URL the UI sends); an `AppHandle`/`AppState` clone inside the connect task to resolve plans there (the plan is only needed at start, and resolving in the command reports errors straight to the caller).

## 2026-11-16 – M4 dependencies: russh, ring, RSA advisory
**Context:** M4 adds SSH sessions in the new Tauri-free crate `fernjo-ssh`. The SSH client library and its crypto backend had to be chosen, and `cargo deny check` reports `RUSTSEC-2023-0071` (Marvin Attack, RSA timing side channel) for the `rsa` crate russh uses.
**Decision:** `russh` 0.65.1 (as resolved in `Cargo.lock`) with `default-features = false` and features `flate2`, `ring`, `rsa`; `tokio` 1 with features `rt`, `net`, `time`, `sync`, `macros`, `io-util`. `russh` pins `rsa` 0.10.0-rc.18, which carries the advisory (fixed in `rsa` 0.10.0). The advisory is ignored in `deny.toml` with a reason, time-boxed by an owner decision of 2026-11-16: the ignore is removed as soon as russh depends on `rsa` >= 0.10.0 (re-check with `cargo deny check` after every russh update). `rsa` stays enabled because RSA host keys and RSA user keys are still common on real servers. **Crypto backend:** `ring`, because it builds on Windows without a C toolchain. "Pure Rust" no longer holds for the crypto backend: `ring` contains C and assembly.
**Rationale:** Dropping RSA would make fernjo unusable against many servers; the exposure is a timing side channel on RSA private-key operations (client-side signing with an RSA user key), which is accepted for now and bounded in time by the removal condition.
**Resolved 2026-11-18:** russh 0.65.2 resolves to `rsa` 0.10.0 and the ignore is gone (see "M6 security review: RUSTSEC-2023-0071 ignore removed").
**Rejected alternatives:** `aws-lc-rs` as backend (needs a C toolchain / CMake on Windows); disabling the `rsa` feature (breaks RSA keys); a permanent, unconditioned ignore.

## 2026-11-15 – Vault: lost-update guard, password change and private key updates
**Context:** An unlocked vault keeps its entries in memory and writes the whole file on every change. A `vault.fernjo` delivered by a sync tool after the unlock (another device added an entry, changed the password, or a newer format arrived) would be silently overwritten, which also broke "a newer format version is refused and never overwritten".
**Decision:** `Vault::save` re-reads the file first and only writes when the file is missing (recreate) or starts with exactly the header bytes this handle last read or wrote (the body nonce changes on every save, so the header identifies the version). Anything else (another header, garbage, empty, shorter than a header, newer format) fails with the new error code `changed_on_disk` ("The vault file was changed by another device or a sync tool. Lock and unlock the vault to load the changes, then repeat your change."); entries and header in memory stay as they were. **Limit:** this is check-then-write, not a lock; a sync tool can still win the race between the check and the rename. **Password change:** the data key never rotates (keychain items must survive), so changing the master password does not re-encrypt or delete existing copies; `vault.fernjo.bak` from just before the change, backups and sync history still open with the old password and yield the data key that decrypts every later version too. This is documented in `Docs/vault-format.md` and in the change-password form; the `.bak` is not refreshed after a change. Real re-keying is not part of M3. **Private key updates:** `update_credential` with a new `privateKey` and no `passphrase` keeps the stored passphrase; `passphrase` sets or replaces it, `clearPassphrase: true` removes it (alone it keeps the key; together with a typed `passphrase` it is refused). The UI offers "Remove stored passphrase" only when editing a private key. **Threads:** the commands that run an Argon2 derivation (`vault_create`, `vault_unlock`, `vault_change_password`) are `#[tauri::command(async)]` so they run off the main thread.
**Rationale:** The guard is cheap, needs no file locking (which sync folders do not honour anyway) and turns a silent data loss into an explicit, recoverable error. Not rotating the key keeps the wrapped-key design of "Vault: wrapped data key, Argon2id, XChaCha20-Poly1305".
**Rejected alternatives:** File locks (not honoured across devices); merging two versions of the vault (needs the key and conflict rules, out of scope); refreshing `.bak` after a password change (it only hides the old copy, the sync history and backups remain); making the new key replace the passphrase silently (data loss the UI field "unchanged" did not announce).

## 2026-11-15 – Vault: secrets and known limitations of zeroisation
**Context:** Secrets must not outlive their use in memory, but the stack between the webview and the keychain contains code fernjo does not own.
**Decision:** Best-effort zeroisation only. Master passwords and entry secrets are `SecretString`/`Zeroizing` inside `fernjo-vault` and the command wrappers; the data key and derived keys are wiped after use. **Known limitations:** secrets pass through unzeroised intermediate `String`s in serde deserialisation (the internally tagged `Secret` enum buffers its content), serde_json buffer reallocations when the vault body is encoded, the Tauri IPC JSON buffer in both directions (the webview process holds the typed secret and, on reveal, the revealed one for as long as the browser engine keeps it), and the `keyring` crate and the OS keychain service. The explicit "reveal" action is the only way a secret reaches the webview, and the UI hides it again after 10 seconds or when the vault locks.
**Rationale:** A guarantee that cannot hold across serde, an IPC layer and a browser engine would be false; the goal is to keep secrets out of files, logs, snapshots and `Debug` output (enforced by tests) and to shorten their lifetime where fernjo controls the memory.
**Rejected alternatives:** A custom deserialiser that parses secrets into zeroising buffers (large code surface for a partial gain); claiming memory-safety guarantees in the docs.

## 2026-11-15 – Data folder change: restart guard
**Context:** The data folder can be changed in the app (M3). Open files, the vault mutex and the keychain id all refer to the running folder, so switching live would risk writing to two folders.
**Decision:** `apply_data_folder` validates the target (absolute, usable, not equal to or nested inside the running folder), copies `connections.json` and `vault.fernjo` only if the target has neither, writes `settings.json` last and then sets `restart_required`; the running app keeps using its old folder until `restart_app`. While the restart is pending, tree and vault commands that write refuse with `restart_required` (`ensure_writable`), so nothing is saved to the old folder after the choice; lock and unlock are allowed (they write no file). Choosing again while pending is allowed (last wins); choosing the running folder cancels the change. If the target already holds data files they are kept untouched and reported in `DataFolderInfo.existingFiles` (names, in the order `connections.json`, `vault.fernjo`); the dialog warns that this folder's data will be used instead of the current data. A failing copy rolls back the files already placed (`copy_failed`). The choice is refused with `data_dir_locked_by_env` when `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` is set, with `settings_unusable` when `settings.json` is unreadable or from a newer schema (the file is never saved over) and with `no_config_dir` when the OS has no config directory. The old folder stays intact as a backup.
**Update 2026-11-15 (M3 review):** `apply_data_folder` is a plain Rust function, not an IPC command. Only `choose_data_folder` (native picker in Rust) calls it, so the webview can never point the data folder at an arbitrary path.
**Rationale:** Copy-then-restart keeps every failure recoverable and never merges two data sets.
**Rejected alternatives:** Switching live (two data sets in flight); merging a target's existing data with the current data; moving instead of copying.

## 2026-11-15 – Master password: NFC normalisation
**Context:** The same typed password must unlock the vault on Windows, macOS and Linux, where input methods and file systems produce composed or decomposed Unicode.
**Decision:** The master password is normalised to NFC and otherwise used byte-exact (no trimming, no case folding); the minimum length of 8 counts characters after normalisation. Entry secrets (passwords, keys, passphrases) are not normalised; they are stored byte-exact. Details in `Docs/vault-format.md`.
**Rationale:** macOS keyboards and sync tools may deliver decomposed forms; NFC makes the derived key independent of that. Trimming would silently change what the user typed.
**Rejected alternatives:** NFKC (folds characters users consider distinct); no normalisation (a vault created on one OS might not open on another).

## 2026-11-15 – Vault file format
**Context:** The vault file is synced between devices by the user and must stay readable by other tools and later versions.
**Decision:** The file format (version 1: header with vault id, Argon2id parameters, salt, wrapped data key; body encrypted with the data key, whole header as AAD) is specified in `Docs/vault-format.md`, which is enough to write a reader. Parameters (128 MiB, 6 iterations, 4 lanes), load bounds, the NFC rule and the dependency versions are recorded in "M3 dependencies and vault parameters". A newer format version is reported as an unusable vault and never overwritten; saves are atomic (temp file + rename) and keep `vault.fernjo.bak`.
**Rationale:** One normative document next to the code avoids drift; decisions about why live here.
**Update 2026-11-15 (M3 review):** a save refuses to overwrite a file changed on disk since it was read (`changed_on_disk`), and changing the master password does not re-encrypt or delete older copies of the file; both are described in "Vault: lost-update guard, password change and private key updates" and in `Docs/vault-format.md`.
**Rejected alternatives:** A self-describing container such as age or JWE (larger dependency, no wrapped-data-key semantics for the keychain).

## 2026-11-15 – `settings.json` and data folder precedence
**Context:** M3 lets the owner move the data folder, so the app needs a place that says where the data folder is. That place cannot be the data folder.
**Decision:** `settings.json` (`{ "schemaVersion": 1, "dataDir"?: string }`) lives in `<OS config dir>/fernjo/` (`Location::config_dir`); `connections.json` and the vault live in `Location::data_dir`. Precedence: `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` (non-empty) > absolute `dataDir` in `settings.json` > `<OS config dir>/fernjo`. While the variable is set, `settings.json` is neither read nor written, so manual runs never touch the real config. A relative or empty `dataDir` counts as unset. If the variable is set but the OS has no config directory, `config_dir` equals `data_dir` (the override stays usable; nothing is read from it). An unreadable `settings.json` (no usable `.bak`) or one with a newer schema does not stop the app: `locate` falls back to the default folder and puts the `LoadError` in `Location::settings_error` for the app to show; the file is left untouched and callers must not save over it. A corrupt file that is recovered from `.bak` is moved to `settings.json.corrupt-<secs>` so the next save cannot copy it over the good backup. Sync-conflict copies (`sync-conflict`, `conflicted copy`, `_conflict-`) in the data folder are reported as `Notice::SyncConflicts`. The atomic write (`write_atomic`) is shared by `connections.json` and `settings.json`.
**Rejected alternatives:** Keeping `settings.json` in the data folder (circular); failing startup on a bad `settings.json` (the app would be unusable for a recoverable file).

## 2026-11-15 – M3 dependencies and vault parameters
**Context:** Milestone 3 adds `crates/fernjo-vault` (no Tauri, no `fernjo-core`). It needs a password KDF, an AEAD, a secret type, memory wiping, Unicode normalisation and a CSPRNG, all from audited crates, never home-made.
**Decision:** Dependencies (versions as resolved on 2026-11-15, see `Cargo.lock`): `argon2` 0.6.0 (Argon2id), `chacha20poly1305` 0.11.0 (XChaCha20-Poly1305, `XChaCha20Poly1305` with 24-byte nonces), `secrecy` 0.10.3 (`SecretString` for passwords), `zeroize` 1.9.1 (wiping the data key, derived keys and decrypted bodies; the `zeroize` feature is enabled on `argon2` and `chacha20poly1305`, otherwise they do not wipe the Argon2 initial hash and memory blocks or their cipher key copies), `unicode-normalization` 0.1.26 (NFC for master passwords), `getrandom` 0.4.3 (OS randomness for key, salt and nonces; called directly so no `rand_core` trait versions have to line up with the RustCrypto crates). Reused: `serde`, `serde_json`, `uuid`, `thiserror`, dev `tempfile`. `cargo deny check` stays green without new ignores. **Format:** `Docs/vault-format.md`; a random 256-bit data key is wrapped by the Argon2id-derived key, the body is encrypted with the data key and fresh 24-byte nonces on every save; the key wrap authenticates the header up to the wrapped key, the body authenticates the whole header (so a password change, which verifies the old password, picks a new salt and wrap nonce, re-wraps the unchanged data key and saves once, re-seals the small body under the same data key with a fresh nonce; the data key and the entries stay unchanged). **Master password:** at least 8 characters counted after NFC normalisation, no composition rules; NFC-normalised, otherwise used byte-exact (no trimming), so the same typed password unlocks on every OS and keyboard input method. The length rule is enforced when creating or changing a password, not when unlocking. **KDF parameters:** Argon2id with 128 MiB memory, 6 iterations, 4 lanes. Measured 0.52 s per derivation on a 6-core desktop (release build; `cargo test -p fernjo-vault --release -- --ignored --nocapture`), the "about 0.5 s" target. The values are stored in the header, so they can be raised later. The memory-heavy, few-pass choice follows the Argon2 guidance (memory is what slows GPU attackers); 128 MiB is well within every supported machine. Load bounds, applied before deriving: parallelism 1..16, iterations 1..20, memory `8 * parallelism` KiB..1 GiB. `KdfParams::TEST` (8 KiB, 1 pass, 1 lane) is the smallest accepted value and exists for tests only. The workspace `Cargo.toml` compiles `argon2` and `blake2` with `opt-level = 3` in the dev profile; unoptimised Argon2 is about 30 times slower and would make unlock take several seconds in `pnpm tauri dev` and in tests that use the default parameters.
**Also in M3:** `keyring` 4.2.0 and its OS stores (see "Vault: keychain behaviour") and `tauri-plugin-dialog` 2.8.4 (see "Data folder dialog").
**Rationale:** RustCrypto crates are the standard pure-Rust, permissively licensed implementations, with no system library on any target. XChaCha20-Poly1305 has a nonce large enough to pick at random on every save without a counter, which matters for a file that is synced between devices.
**Rejected alternatives:** `rand` as the randomness source (an extra dependency and trait-version coupling for filling a few byte arrays); AES-GCM (12-byte nonces make random nonces on a long-lived synced file less comfortable); scrypt or PBKDF2 (Argon2id is the current recommendation and resists GPUs better); encrypting the body directly with the password-derived key (a password change would re-encrypt everything and the keychain could not hold a password-independent key).

## 2026-11-15 – M2 dependencies
**Context:** The connection tree needs serialisation, ids, errors, a config directory, a single-instance guard and the Tauri JS API.
**Decision:** Rust (`fernjo-core`): `serde` 1.0.229 and `serde_json` 1.0.154 (JSON), `uuid` 1.29.2 (v4 ids), `thiserror` 2.0.21 (error types), `directories` 6.0.0 (OS config directory), dev-only `tempfile` 3.27.0. `src-tauri`: `fernjo-core` (path) and `tauri-plugin-single-instance` 2.5.5, which on Linux pulls in `zbus` (D-Bus) and with it three "unmaintained" advisories (`RUSTSEC-2026-0370`, `-0371`, `-0372`: `zvariant`, `zbus_names`, `zvariant_derive`); they are listed in `deny.toml` with a reason because no upgrade exists. UI: `@tauri-apps/api` 2.12.4. Versions are the ones `Cargo.lock` / `pnpm-lock.yaml` resolved on 2026-11-15; `cargo deny check` is green.
**Rationale:** All are the standard crates for their job, permissively licensed, and already in Tauri's own dependency tree or widely used. The single-instance plugin is the supported Tauri way to focus the running window.
**Rejected alternatives:** A home-made lock file or named pipe for single instance (platform-specific code we would have to test three times); `confy`/`config` crates (we write one fixed file with our own atomic-write rules); `directories`' `ProjectDirs` (adds a `config` subfolder on Windows, see the data-folder entry).

## 2026-11-15 – IPC content security policy
**Context:** M0 left a follow-up (entry "Webview hardening from day one"): the CSP had no `connect-src` for Tauri IPC, and the config test forbade the substring `http:`. M2 adds the first Tauri commands.
**Decision:** The CSP gains `connect-src ipc: http://ipc.localhost` (everything else unchanged: `default-src 'self'`, no `unsafe-eval`). `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs` now requires that exact `connect-src`, rejects the token `*` and `unsafe-eval`, and allows no `http:`/`https:` token other than `http://ipc.localhost`. Capabilities stay `core:default`. Verified: with this CSP the running app loads the tree through the commands (no IPC blocked).
**Rationale:** Tauri 2 sends commands over `ipc:` (and `http://ipc.localhost` on Windows/Android); without the entry a strict `default-src` blocks them. The relaxed test still forbids every external origin.
**Rejected alternatives:** Dropping the CSP or using `connect-src *`; the custom-protocol-only workaround (not available on all platforms).

## 2026-11-15 – Pointer-based drag and drop in the tree
**Context:** M2 needs moving nodes by drag and drop in a nested tree with three drop zones per folder (before, inside, after). The owner's Linux setup is Wayland with WebKitGTK.
**Decision:** The tree is dragged with pointer events (`pointerdown`/`pointermove`/`pointerup`, pointer capture once a 4 px threshold is crossed). A pure function (`ui/src/lib/drop-target.ts`, `dropPosition` and `resolveDrop`) maps the pointer to a drop (new parent and index, counted after the dragged node is removed, the same convention as the Rust `move_node`); a DOM-free `DragController` holds the state machine. Rust validates every move; the only call is `move_node`. Dragging is off while a filter is active or a rename is open; Escape cancels.
**Rationale:** Native HTML5 drag and drop needs `dragDropEnabled: false` in the Tauri window and is the least reliable on WebKitGTK/Wayland; the pointer approach behaves the same in all three webviews and is unit-testable without a browser.
**Rejected alternatives:** Native HTML5 drag and drop; a Svelte drag-and-drop library (not built for nested trees with three drop zones, extra dependency).

## 2026-11-15 – `connections.json`: format, location, write strategy
**Context:** M2 persists the connection tree; later milestones add settings (credential reference in M3, RDP in M8) and import (M5). The file must survive crashes and newer app versions, and must never hold a secret.
**Decision:** The tree lives in Rust (`fernjo-core`, a pure `Tree` plus a `Store` that saves after every mutation); every mutating Tauri command returns the full new snapshot and the UI renders snapshots (the UI keeps only selection, expansion, filter and drag state). Delete asks for confirmation and is final; `connections.json.bak` is the only safety net. **Format:** `connections.json`, pretty-printed UTF-8 JSON, `{ "schemaVersion": 1, "children": [ … ] }`, nodes nested: `{ "id" (UUID v4), "type": "folder"|"connection", "name", "description", "settings": { "username", "port", "keepaliveSecs" } (omitted keys = inherit), and either "children": [ … ] or "protocol": "ssh"|"rdp", "host" }`. Order is the position in `children`. No credential field exists. Inheritable in M2: username, port, keepalive; effective value = nearest value from the node up through its folders, then the default (SSH 22, RDP 3389, keepalive 30 s with 0 = off, no username; a folder's port has no default). **Location:** `<OS config dir>/fernjo/` (`%APPDATA%\fernjo`, `~/Library/Application Support/fernjo`, `~/.config/fernjo`), overridden by the environment variable `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` (tests, the sample-tree generator). **Write:** temp file in the same folder, `sync_all`, copy the current file to `connections.json.bak`, rename the temp file over the target; a failed save leaves memory unchanged (the mutation is applied to a copy first). **Load:** a missing file is an empty tree (created by the first mutation); a `schemaVersion` newer than the app is refused and never overwritten; an unreadable file falls back to `.bak` after being renamed to `connections.json.corrupt-<unix seconds>` (so a later save cannot replace the good `.bak` with garbage) and the UI shows a notice; if both are unreadable the app starts in an error state and writes nothing. Migration is a chain of version N → N+1 functions (empty for now, tested). **Update 2026-11-15 (M3, schema 2):** `schemaVersion` is now 2 and `settings` gains an optional, inheritable `credential` (vault entry UUID, omitted = inherit; resolved like `port`, no default; the core never knows the vault, so the id may dangle). The 1 → 2 migration step is a no-op that exists only so older builds refuse the file (they would drop `credential` on their next save); a version-1 file loads and is written as version 2 on the next save (the version-1 bytes stay in `.bak`), version 3 is refused as a newer schema. The "No credential field exists" sentence above applies to schema 1 only.
**Rationale:** Rust ownership keeps validity and inheritance in one tested place, and a snapshot per mutation (~170 KB at 500 connections) is simple and cannot drift. The write sequence never leaves a half-written main file; the load rules never destroy data the user might still want.
**Rejected alternatives:** UI-owned tree pushed to Rust (logic would drift into TypeScript); diff or event streams (complexity with no benefit at this size); instant delete with an undo toast (needs an undo history in the core); `directories`' `ProjectDirs` layout (adds a `config` subfolder on Windows); renaming the temp file over the old one without a `.bak` copy.

## 2026-11-15 – Visual direction: "Ulm, revised"
**Context:** M1 had to fix fernjo's look before any real UI is built. Four directions were built as switchable mockups (main window, unlock dialog, light and dark) in one artifact: Fernverkehr (DB timetable, the name taken literally), Ulm (Braun / HfG Ulm functionalism), Gate (airport signage) and Workbench (the category standard). The directions came from impeccable's `concept-seed` (seed:1c7386560d20e2dc); Ulm was impeccable's own pick, Fernverkehr the assigned roll.
**Decision:** "Ulm, revised", approved by the owner on 2026-11-15 ("ulm revised is approved") after one feedback round: warm grey housing, off-white surfaces, charcoal ink, Hanken Grotesk and Red Hat Mono, 24 px tree rows, a lamp column that lights up only for running sessions (shape carries state: pulsing ring, filled, struck), yellow (#f0b400) only for the one action that matters and for Reconnect on dropped tabs, non-modal vault unlock (note in the sidebar, password asked in the tab of the connection that needs it), optional host column, collapsible properties panel, terminal minimum contrast 4.5:1. Record: `DESIGN.md`, `ui/src/styles/tokens.css`, the surface brief `.impeccable/surfaces/ui-src-app-svelte.md` and the mockups `Docs/design/m01-concepts.html` (the artifact itself is private to the owner).
**Rationale:** Calm enough for all-day terminal work and eye comfort, and still recognisable through the lamp column and the yellow key; it keeps the mRemoteNG structure the owner wants (stacked properties, dense tree, protocol icons, classic tabs) and avoids the owner's anti-references.
**Rejected alternatives:** Fernverkehr (large blue sidebar is a strong colour all day, condensed face risky at 125 % scaling; its host column was adopted as an option); Gate (bright yellow over long dark sessions, close to Fernverkehr's world); Workbench (no look of its own); five catalog challengers declined during the roll (spectrogram plate, pocket airline timetable, reference edition, convention catalog, Metro tiles).

## 2026-11-15 – UI tokens, theming and bundled fonts
**Context:** M2–M6 build the real UI; it needs a stable token interface that renders in WebView2, WKWebView and WebKitGTK 4.1 (Ubuntu 22.04) under a strict CSP.
**Decision:** `ui/src/styles/tokens.css` is the token interface (names only grow; renames need a new entry here). Light values in `:root`; dark values in `:root[data-theme="dark"]` and in `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { :root:not([data-theme="light"]) }` with identical contents, so M6's theme setting only sets `data-theme` on `<html>`. Colours are `#rrggbb`/`#rrggbbaa`; modern colour functions, nesting, `@layer`, `:has()` and container queries are not used. `ui/src/styles/tokens.test.ts` guards names, parity, hex format, banned CSS and WCAG contrast pairs (Vitest runs with `css: true` so `?raw` imports work). Fonts are bundled from npm, pinned: `@fontsource-variable/hanken-grotesk` 5.3.0 and `@fontsource-variable/red-hat-mono` 5.3.0 (both OFL-1.1, about 99 KB woff2 in total, normal style only). Vite asset inlining is off (`assetsInlineLimit: 0`) because the CSP blocks `data:` fonts. The terminal's minimum contrast (4.5, `--terminal-min-contrast`) is applied by xterm.js `minimumContrastRatio` in M4 instead of bending the palette.
**Rationale:** The webview with the oldest engine sets the rules; tests keep the rules from eroding; bundled fonts keep the CSP strict and work offline.
**Rejected alternatives:** `oklch`/`light-dark()` (unsupported in WebKitGTK 4.1); Google Fonts at runtime (blocked by the CSP and a privacy leak); loosening the CSP for `data:` fonts; system font stacks only (would make the look differ per OS).

## 2026-11-15 – App icon
**Context:** M0 shipped a placeholder icon ("f" on a plain tile) and planned its replacement in M1 (entry "Project layout: one root pnpm package, Tauri identifier").
**Decision:** The icon comes from the chosen direction: charcoal housing, a bone ring (local end) joined to the lit yellow key (remote end). Source `src-tauri/icons/icon-source.svg`; the desktop set in `src-tauri/icons/` is generated with `pnpm tauri icon src-tauri/icons/icon-source.svg` (the generated `android/` and `ios/` folders are not kept). `icon.icns` alone comes from `src-tauri/icons/icon-source-macos.svg`, the same artwork scaled to Apple's 824 px icon grid; regenerate it with `pnpm tauri icon src-tauri/icons/icon-source-macos.svg -o <temp dir>` and copy only `icon.icns`. Approved by the owner on 2026-11-15.
**Rationale:** A plain signifier of a connection that stays legible at 16–32 px on light and dark taskbars and avoids a plant reference (the name means *Fernverbindung*).
**Rejected alternatives:** A lettermark (says nothing about the product); a fern motif (misreads the name).

## 2026-11-15 – CI toolchain: plain rustup, UI build first, hosted Ubuntu only; gitleaks CLI for the history scan
**Context:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` must install the pinned Rust toolchain, build a Tauri crate that embeds the frontend; separately, the full git history must be scanned for secrets once before the repo goes public; the cargo-deny job runs with the default advisory scope.
**Decision:** The pinned toolchain is installed with `rustup toolchain install` (rustup 1.28+ no longer installs on `rustup show`), not a third-party toolchain action. The `rust` CI job needs no UI build: plain cargo builds are Tauri dev builds and do not embed `ui/dist`; only `pnpm tauri build` embeds it and runs `pnpm ui:build` itself (`beforeBuildCommand`), which the `tauri-build` job covers. CI runs on `ubuntu-latest` only; the Ubuntu 22.04 minimum is checked when packaging (M6). The history scan is a one-off run of the gitleaks CLI, done locally before the repo goes public (M0 release task, "Scan the full git history for secrets"), not a CI job: `ci.yml` has no gitleaks step. The CLI is used instead of `gitleaks-action`, which needs a licence for organisations. Known gap: cargo-deny's unsound/unmaintained checks default to workspace direct dependencies only, so `RUSTSEC-2024-0429` (`glib` 0.18.5 unsound, via Tauri's GTK3 stack) is only reported with `unsound = "all"`; revisit when Tauri leaves GTK3.
**Rationale:** Fewer third-party actions to pin and trust; the `rust` job stays free of Node tooling; hosted minutes are free for a public repo and one Linux job covers the code paths until packaging.
**Rejected alternatives:** A third-party toolchain action (extra supply-chain surface); `gitleaks-action` (licence); an OS matrix in M0 (cost without a packaging step to test); enabling `unsound = "all"` now (would fail on a Tauri-internal advisory we cannot fix).

## 2026-11-15 – Webview hardening from day one
**Context:** The webview must never become an easy path to secrets, which stay in Rust.
**Decision:** `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` sets a non-null strict CSP (`default-src 'self'`, no `unsafe-eval`), and `src-tauri/capabilities/default.json` grants `core:default` only. `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs` guards both. Known follow-up: the CSP has no `connect-src` for Tauri IPC (`ipc:` / `http://ipc.localhost`) and the test forbids the substring `http:`. The milestone that adds the first Tauri command (M2) must add the `connect-src` entry, relax the test to forbid external origins and `*` instead of the substring `http:`, and verify there is no CSP warning in the webview console.
Follow-up closed in: IPC content security policy (M2).
**Rationale:** Tightening a CSP and capability set later is harder than starting strict; the tests keep them from drifting.
**Rejected alternatives:** `csp: null` until needed; broad default capabilities.
**Update 2026-11-18 (M6, navigation guard):** The main webview may navigate only to the app's own origin: `src-tauri/src/nav_guard.rs` (`is_app_url`, installed as the plugin `nav_guard::plugin()` right after the single-instance plugin, because `Builder::on_navigation` exists on `tauri::plugin::Builder` but not on the app builder in Tauri 2.12) allows only the platform's own release origin (`tauri://localhost` on macOS and Linux, `http(s)://tauri.localhost` on Windows and Android, as `tauri::protocol::origin` and `is_local_url` define it in Tauri 2.12; no non-default port, no userinfo; an explicit default port is normalised away by the URL parser and is accepted) and, in a dev build, exactly scheme, host and port of `build.devUrl`; everything else (other schemes, other ports, look-alike hosts, userinfo tricks, `about:blank`) is refused. Terminal links are unaffected (`open_external_url` opens them in Rust). `window.open` stays blocked by wry. Verified in the Windows release binary: a script `location.href`, `location.assign`, an `<a>` click, `form.submit()`, an iframe and `window.open` to `http://127.0.0.1:8099/` leave the page unchanged; reload, the UI and `open_external_url` still work. Known limit (WebView2): for top-level GET navigations (`location.href`, link click) the cancelled navigation still reaches the remote server as one request whose response is discarded; form posts, iframes and `window.open` send nothing. The guard keeps the remote page, its content and the IPC out of the window; it cannot stop that single blind GET (a CSP cannot restrict navigations). The dev allow rule (exactly the origin of `build.devUrl`) is covered by unit tests only, not by a real-app check. macOS and Linux are not verified.

## 2026-11-15 – Project layout: one root pnpm package, Tauri identifier
**Context:** The scaffold needs a frontend layout and an app identifier.
**Decision:** A single root pnpm package (no pnpm workspace) with the frontend sources and Vite config in `ui/`. Tauri identifier `io.github.benjo-code.fernjo`; changing it later moves the app data paths. The icon is a placeholder, replaced in M1. `version` in `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` duplicates the workspace version; follow-up: drop it if the release flow does not bump both.
**Rationale:** There is only one JS package, so a workspace adds nothing; the identifier follows the GitHub namespace.
**Rejected alternatives:** A pnpm workspace; a frontend directory outside `ui/`.

## 2026-11-15 – TypeScript pinned to 6.0.x
**Context:** `typescript-eslint` declares peer `typescript <6.1.0` and `svelte-check` declares `^5 || ^6`, so neither supports TypeScript 7.
**Decision:** `package.json` pins `typescript` to `~6.0.3`; `.github/dependabot.yml` ignores TypeScript major bumps. Revisit when both tools support 7. Follow-up: `ui/vite.config.ts` is covered by ESLint and Prettier but not type-checked by `svelte-check` (`ui/tsconfig.json` only includes `src/**`); a `tsconfig.node.json` with `@types/node` would close the gap.
**Rationale:** Keeps `pnpm lint` working without peer-dependency overrides.
**Rejected alternatives:** TypeScript 7 now (breaks the peer ranges); overriding the peer ranges.

## 2026-11-15 – Fixed versions of the scaffold
**Context:** M0 fixes the toolchain and framework lines so later milestones build on a known base.
**Decision:** Rust 1.100.0 (`rust-toolchain.toml`, not bumped by Dependabot); Tauri 2.12 line (`tauri` 2.12, `tauri-build` 2.7; 3.0 alphas exist (crates.io listing, 2026-11-15) and are deliberately not used); Svelte 5, Vite 8, Vitest 5, ESLint 10; pnpm 12.21.0 (`packageManager` in `package.json`); Node 24 in CI (`engines` `>=22.12.0`).
**Rationale:** Current stable lines; the pinned toolchain makes local and CI builds identical.
**Rejected alternatives:** Tauri 3.0 alphas (unstable); a floating `stable` toolchain (non-reproducible builds).

## 2026-11-15 – cargo-deny: licence allow-list and advisory ignores
**Context:** M0 adds a `cargo-deny` CI job (advisories, bans, licences, sources) that must be green on the workspace including Tauri's dependency tree (cargo-deny 0.20.2, run with `--all-features`; the graph covers all platforms, so Linux-only crates are checked too).
**Decision:** `deny.toml` allows only `MIT`, `Apache-2.0`, `Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception`, `BSD-2-Clause`, `BSD-3-Clause`, `ISC`, `Zlib`, `Unicode-3.0`, `CC0-1.0`, `MPL-2.0`; no licence was added beyond this starting list (the first run reported no licence violations). Sources are crates.io only (unknown registry and git denied); duplicate versions only warn. One advisory is ignored: `RUSTSEC-2024-0370` (`proc-macro-error` unmaintained, pulled in by `glib-macros` through Tauri's Linux GTK3 stack; no safe upgrade exists). Remove the ignore when Tauri moves off GTK3 (or `glib-macros` drops `proc-macro-error`); `cargo deny check` will then report the entry as unused, as a *warning* (not an error), so look for it in the output after Tauri upgrades. Wildcard dependencies are not allowed (default `warn` is not loosened) and the licence confidence threshold stays at its default.
**Rationale:** The allow-list is limited to permissive licences and MPL-2.0 (weak copyleft), all compatible with distribution under `MIT OR Apache-2.0`. The ignore is a single, id-specific entry with a reason: the crate is a build-time proc macro we cannot replace, and it is an unmaintained notice, not a vulnerability.
**Rejected alternatives:** Disabling the unmaintained check (`unmaintained = "none"`) or blanket-ignoring advisories (would hide future findings); allowing licences speculatively (every addition should be driven by a real finding); skipping crates in the licence check.

## 2026-11-15 – Licence MIT OR Apache-2.0
**Context:** The owner wants fernjo open source and left the choice of licence open.
**Decision:** Dual licence `MIT OR Apache-2.0`.
**Rationale:** Rust ecosystem convention, compatible with all planned dependencies (russh, IronRDP, Tauri, xterm.js), and Apache-2.0 adds a patent grant.
**Rejected alternatives:** GPL-3.0 (would keep forks open, but more friction and not needed for a personal tool); proprietary (the owner wants open source).

## 2026-11-15 – Public repo, CI only on GitHub-hosted runners
**Context:** Tauri on Linux needs WebKitGTK system packages; the org's self-hosted runner (`dotnet-linux`) has no root. Release builds need macOS and Windows machines. Hosted-runner minutes cost money on private repos.
**Decision:** The repo is made public in M0, and all CI, including the `roadmap` job, runs on GitHub-hosted runners. Repo security is switched on: secret scanning with push protection, Dependabot alerts and updates, CodeQL, private vulnerability reporting, `cargo-deny`, and approval for workflows from outside contributors.
**Rationale:** Free hosted minutes for public repos, all three OSes available. Self-hosted runners must never run workflows of a public repo, because fork PRs could execute code on them.
**Rejected alternatives:** Self-hosted runner with WebKitGTK added (no root, and unsafe once public); mixed self-hosted/hosted (more complexity, the same public-repo risk).

## 2026-11-15 – Vault: wrapped data key, Argon2id, XChaCha20-Poly1305
**Context:** Credentials must be stored encrypted, the vault file may be synced between devices by the user, and "unlock automatically on this device" uses the OS keychain.
**Decision:** A random 256-bit data key encrypts the whole vault file; the master password derives (Argon2id) a key that wraps the data key. The OS keychain stores the data key. RustCrypto crates only.
**Rationale:** Changing the master password only re-wraps the data key, so keychain entries on other synced devices stay valid; no home-made crypto.
**Update 2026-11-15 (M3):** The statement above is imprecise: a password change re-wraps the data key and re-saves the file, and the body is re-sealed with a fresh nonce because the whole header is its AAD (see "M3 dependencies and vault parameters"). The data key and the entries stay unchanged, so keychain entries stay valid.
**Rejected alternatives:** Storing the password-derived key in the keychain (invalidated on every password change); OS keychain as the only store (does not sync across OSes, no portable backup).

## 2026-11-15 – Vault: keychain behaviour
**Context:** "Unlock automatically on this device" needs the data key in the OS keychain, without a system C library on Linux.
**Decision:** `keyring` 4.2.0 (with `keyring-core` 1.0.1) with its default `v1` feature, which selects per OS: `windows-native-keyring-store` 1.1.0 (Credential Manager), `apple-native-keyring-store` (macOS Keychain) and `zbus-secret-service-keyring-store` 1.0.1 (Secret Service over `zbus`, no libdbus; `cargo tree -p fernjo-vault --target x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` shows no `dbus` crate). Item: service `fernjo`, user = vault id, secret = the 32 raw key bytes (`DataKey`, wiped after use). Auto-unlock (`try_auto_unlock`) never prompts and **never deletes the item**: a missing item gives `NoKey`, a keychain read error `KeychainUnavailable`, a file-level failure (unreadable, truncated, replaced or newer-format file) `Unavailable`, a key that fails authentication (`tampered`, which `open_body` also reports for a JSON failure) gives `KeyNotAccepted`, and a file older than the last version this device opened gives `RolledBack` (see "Vault: rollback protection"); the vault stays locked in all of these and the UI falls back to the master password. `AppState.auto_unlock_issue` keeps the startup failure (`key_not_accepted`, `keychain_unavailable`, `unavailable` or `rolled_back`) and `vault_status` reports it as `autoUnlockIssue` while the vault is locked; the lock note shows a line chosen by that code while the issue is set, and a successful unlock (or a new vault) clears the issue, which removes the note. `key_not_accepted` and `unavailable` (file-level failures: unreadable, truncated, replaced or newer-format file) say that automatic unlock did not work, that the file may be incomplete or damaged and that the stored key was kept; `keychain_unavailable` (the keychain `get` itself failed: no Secret Service, locked keyring, access denied) shows **no line** (final review ruling, 2026-11-18): on a machine without a keychain service it is reported at every start, so users who never turned automatic unlock on would see a note about a feature they do not use each time. The field stays in the status and the Rust enum and code are unchanged; the cost is that an opted-in user whose keyring is locked gets no explanation, as before M6. `rolled_back` has its own line (the file is older than the last version this device opened; enter the master password to review). Unknown codes fall back to a neutral line that never claims a key exists. An unlock with the box ticked overwrites the item with the real key, which heals a truly wrong item; unticking removes it (`vault_set_auto_unlock(false)`). A master password change keeps the data key, so stored keys stay valid. **Why it never deletes (M6 security fix):** the item holds only the 32-byte data key, which never changes for a vault id (not on a password change either), and the vault id is random per vault, so a replaced vault has another id and never touches the old item. A `tampered` result therefore says nothing about the item; it comes from a damaged, half-synced or planted same-id file, and deleting would silently switch auto-unlock off after a transient problem (the former behaviour, rejected). **Other readers of the keychain:** a second launch of the app (single-instance) also runs the auto-unlock attempt before it exits, so it reads the keychain and, on macOS, may trigger a keychain prompt; it deletes nothing. `lock` never touches the keychain (the lock lasts until the next start); `set_auto_unlock(true)` surfaces a keychain error (`keychain_unavailable`), while a keychain failure on unlock or create is ignored and only leaves `autoUnlock` false.
**Update 2026-11-15 (M3 review):** because the data key never rotates, changing the master password does not lock out the old password for older copies of the vault file (`vault.fernjo.bak`, backups, sync history): they still open with the old password and the key they yield decrypts every later version. Real re-keying is not in M3. Unticking "Unlock automatically on this device" in the lock note now removes the keychain item after a successful unlock (the UI calls `vault_set_auto_unlock(false)`; `vault_unlock` with `remember: false` itself never deletes the item).
**Threat model:** With auto-unlock on, the data key is readable by anything running as the same OS user, so such a process can open the vault. Auto-unlock is therefore an explicit opt-in per device; the master password protects the vault file when it is copied or synced elsewhere.
**Rejected alternatives:** `dbus-secret-service` / `linux-keyutils` stores (libdbus or kernel keyring without persistence); storing the password-derived key (see above).

## 2026-11-15 – SSH via russh, RDP via IronRDP
**Context:** The core must be Rust; SSH is used more than RDP.
**Decision:** russh (tokio) for SSH; IronRDP for RDP, rendered through a framebuffer streamed to a canvas. The RDP milestone starts with a measured spike; a native render surface is the fallback (its own milestone if needed).
**Rationale:** Both are pure Rust and actively maintained; IronRDP supports TLS, NLA/CredSSP and clipboard.
**Rejected alternatives:** FreeRDP via FFI (C dependency, kept as fallback if IronRDP fails for protocol reasons); launching the OS RDP client (none on macOS/Linux, breaks the one-window idea); libssh2 bindings (C dependency).

## 2026-11-15 – UI: Tauri 2 + Svelte 5 + xterm.js
**Context:** fernjo needs a high-quality terminal (main use is SSH), a modern UI (the reason for the project) and RDP rendering, on three OSes.
**Decision:** Tauri 2 shell, Svelte 5 + TypeScript frontend, xterm.js for the terminal. All logic stays in Rust crates that do not depend on Tauri.
**Rationale:** xterm.js is a mature terminal (used by VS Code); web UI makes design iteration cheap. RDP via canvas is the accepted risk, checked by a spike.
**Rejected alternatives:** egui/Iced + alacritty_terminal (all Rust, best RDP, but immature terminal widget and harder to make look modern); Slint (no terminal widget); Electron (heavy, non-Rust core); Qt bindings (awkward in Rust).

## 2026-11-15 – Data folder dialog: tauri-plugin-dialog (Rust API only)
**Context:** M3 lets the user pick the data folder; the native folder picker must open from Rust and the webview must gain no new permissions.
**Decision:** `tauri-plugin-dialog` 2.8.4 (`tauri-plugin-dialog = "2"`), registered after the single-instance plugin and used only through its Rust API (`DialogExt::file().blocking_pick_folder()` on a `spawn_blocking` worker). No JS permission is added: `capabilities/default.json` stays `["core:default"]` (guarded by `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`), so the webview cannot call the plugin's own commands. `apply_data_folder` copies `connections.json` and `vault.fernjo` only into a folder that has neither of them, writes `settings.json` last and then sets `restart_required`; the running app keeps its old folder until `restart_app`.
**Update 2026-11-15 (M3 review):** `apply_data_folder` is not registered as an IPC command; the path only ever comes from the native picker.
**Rationale:** The picker needs no frontend code, and the dialog result goes through the same validated Rust path as any other choice.
**Rejected alternatives:** `@tauri-apps/plugin-dialog` from the webview (needs a capability, and the path would come from the UI); a text field for the path (error-prone); moving files instead of copying them (the old folder stays intact as backup).

## 2026-11-17 – M6 packaging: targets, per-user NSIS, one version source, release profile
**Context:** M6 turns the app into installers for Windows, macOS and Linux that a user can install without developer tooling.
**Decision:** `bundle.targets` is exactly `nsis`, `dmg`, `appimage` and `deb` (guarded by `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`). The NSIS installer is per-user (`installMode: "currentUser"`, English only), so an unsigned tool needs no administrator rights. `tauri.conf.json` has no `version` key: Tauri falls back to the `fernjo-app` crate version, which inherits `[workspace.package].version`, so `Cargo.toml` is the single source for installer names and the app version. The release profile is `strip = "symbols"`, `lto = "thin"`, `codegen-units = 1` (smaller binary, reproducible enough, link time stays moderate). No updater and no signing material in the config (also guarded by the test). macOS minimum is 11.0.
**Rationale:** One version source cannot drift between the conf and the crates; per-user install avoids UAC for a tool that writes only to the user's profile.
**Rejected alternatives:** per-machine NSIS install (needs administrator rights, unnecessary here); MSI/WiX (extra toolchain, no benefit for a per-user tool); `tauri-action` (hides the build behind a third-party action; plain `pnpm tauri build` in our own pinned workflow stays auditable); `lto = "fat"` (much longer builds for little gain).

## 2026-11-17 – M6 release workflow: plain `pnpm tauri build`, split permissions, dry run on pull requests
**Context:** A published GitHub release has to produce the installers and attach them with checksums, without giving a build that runs third-party code a token that can write.
**Decision:** `.github/workflows/release.yml` has two jobs. `build` (matrix: Windows NSIS, macOS dmg for aarch64 and x86_64, Ubuntu 22.04 AppImage and deb) holds only `contents: read`, runs `pnpm tauri build --bundles <list> [--target <triple>]` and uploads the installers as artifacts; a glob that matches nothing fails the step and lists what the bundler produced. `publish` is the only job with `contents: write`: it downloads the artifacts, runs `tools/verify-installers.sh` (exactly the five expected files), writes `SHA256SUMS` and calls `gh release upload --clobber`. The tag, release text and inputs reach scripts only through `env:`, and `tools/check-release-version.sh` (accepts only `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` with an optional leading `v`, so a pre-release such as `v0.1.0-rc.1` or a suffix such as `v0.0.0-rehearsal` is rejected; compares `Cargo.toml` and `package.json`) runs before the build. The `workflows` job in `ci.yml` runs `tools/check-workflows.sh` (pinned SHAs, the literal word `self-hosted` anywhere in a workflow file, no `pull_request_target`, a top-level `permissions:` block present, only `matrix`/`runner`/`env`/`secrets` and a few safe `github.*` values inside `run:` expressions, everything else via `env:`) and the tests of the scripts. A `pull_request` trigger limited to the packaging inputs is the dry run: it builds and uploads artifacts but never publishes. `workflow_dispatch` (with an optional `tag` to re-attach installers to an existing release) only exists once the file is on `dev`, which is why the pull-request trigger, not the manual one, is the way to try the workflow before the merge.
**Known limits of the workflow check:** it matches text, it does not interpret the workflow. A custom runner label (a runner group or a label that is not the word `self-hosted`) is not caught, and neither is `permissions: write-all` or a broader grant than needed (it only requires that a top-level `permissions:` block exists); the per-job split of `release.yml` is by review, not by the script. The script is not changed for this; the limits are stated so the check is not read as more than it is.
**Rationale:** Splitting the permissions keeps the write token away from the build and its dependencies; the explicit file list catches a bundler naming change before an incomplete release goes out. The AppImage, deb and dmg names are Tauri's documented pattern and are confirmed by the first workflow run.
**Rejected alternatives:** `tauri-action` (hides the build behind a third-party action; plain `pnpm tauri build` in our own pinned workflow stays auditable); a single job with `contents: write` (the build would run with a token that can modify releases).
**M11: build provenance (findings table row 71, owner decision 2026-11-25):** `publish` runs `actions/attest-build-provenance` (v4.2.2, pinned to its SHA, a wrapper over `actions/attest`) with `subject-path: installers/*` after "Write SHA256SUMS" and before the upload, and its `permissions:` gain `id-token: write` and `attestations: write` next to `contents: write`; `build` stays `contents: read`. Why: `SHA256SUMS` sits in the same mutable release as the installers and `gh release upload --clobber` can replace both together, so it proves a download is intact, not where it came from; the Sigstore-signed attestation ties each installer's digest to a run of this repository's release workflow (and the ref it ran from), and is stored outside the release. The README tells users to check it with `gh attestation verify <installer file> --repo benjo-code/fernjo --signer-workflow benjo-code/fernjo/.github/workflows/release.yml --source-ref refs/tags/vX.Y.Z`, which proves a build by this repository's `release.yml` from that tag. Each option closes a gap the others leave: `--repo` alone accepts an attestation from any workflow of the repository on any ref; `--signer-workflow` checks only the workflow path (the certificate's `.../release.yml@<ref>`), so it stops workflows at other paths but not a token that can push workflows, which can put an edited `release.yml` on a branch and attest a swapped file from there; `--source-ref` with the tag stops that branch run, as long as tags cannot be moved (the tag ruleset below). Releases before v0.3.0 have none. Still open at that point, as repository settings the owner changes: a `v*` tag ruleset and immutable releases (both settled below).
**M11: immutable releases, draft then publish (rows 69 to 71, 2026-11-25):** the owner turned on "Require actions to be pinned to a full-length commit SHA" and a tag ruleset "release tags" (`refs/tags/v*`: no update, no deletion, no force push, no bypass; creating a tag stays allowed, because the release procedure creates it). Immutable releases (a published release's assets and tag can no longer change) were turned on by the owner on 2026-11-25, after this flow reached `dev` (PR #50). A published release takes no new files then, so the workflow cannot start from a published release any more: `release.yml` now runs on a pushed `v*` tag (and on `workflow_dispatch` run on the tag, which repeats a failed run), `RELEASE_TAG` is `github.ref_name` when the ref is a `v*` tag and empty otherwise, and `publish` first refuses a missing or already published release, then attests, attaches the installers and `SHA256SUMS` to the draft (`--clobber` only replaces files of an earlier failed run on the same draft) and publishes it as its last step. The owner pushes the tag and creates the draft with `gh release create vX.Y.Z --draft --verify-tag` (`CLAUDE.md` "Releases"); the build takes far longer than that, so the draft exists before `publish` checks for it; a run that does not find it is repeated with "Re-run failed jobs" (same ref, reuses the stored artifacts for 7 days; a dispatch on the tag builds everything again). Side effects: every run that can publish runs on the tag, so the provenance's source ref is always `refs/tags/vX.Y.Z` (the old caveat that a re-attach from `dev` fails `--source-ref` is gone); the old attach rehearsal with a throwaway `v0.0.0` tag is retired, because the ruleset forbids deleting that tag and the run would now publish the release, so the first run of `publish` in this form is the v0.3.0 release, and every step before "Publish the release" leaves only a draft that can be retried or deleted. A wrong tag needs the owner to switch the ruleset off. CodeQL for workflow files is the `actions` language in `.github/workflows/codeql.yml` (the repository uses this advanced setup, so GitHub's default setup stays off). Rejected: leaving the draft for the owner to publish by hand (one more manual step, and a release published by hand before the run finishes would take no installers).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 NSIS installer icon set explicitly
**Context:** The owner found on the Windows test PC that `fernjo_0.0.0_x64-setup.exe` showed no fernjo icon.
**Decision:** `bundle.windows.nsis.installerIcon` is `icons/icon.ico` (guarded by `nsis_installer_uses_the_app_icon` in `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`, which also checks that the file exists and is one of `bundle.icon`). Without the key the bundler uses its default NSIS icon: `ExtractAssociatedIcon` on the installer built before the change returned the generic NSIS icon, after the change (`pnpm tauri build --bundles nsis`, built, not run) the fernjo icon (charcoal tile, ring, yellow dot). `icons/icon.ico` holds 16, 24, 32, 48, 64 and 256 px. `headerImage` and `sidebarImage` stay unset on purpose (no branded installer pages wanted).
**Rationale:** The icon of the app executable and of the installer are separate settings; only the first follows `bundle.icon`.
**Rejected alternatives:** a branded header and sidebar image (not wanted, extra assets to keep in step with the design).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 installer smoke tests: what they prove and where they run
**Context:** The milestone needs "install, start, uninstall leaves the data folder alone" for every installer, without touching the owner's machine.
**Decision:** `tools/smoke-installed.ps1` (Windows) and `tools/smoke-installed.sh` (Linux, macOS) run as steps of the `build` job in `release.yml`, on the hosted runner, after the installers are collected. Both refuse to run outside `GITHUB_ACTIONS`. Windows: put a sentinel file into `%APPDATA%\fernjo`, install silently (`/S`), require the per-user install under `%LOCALAPPDATA%`, start the app with a scratch `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` and wait for its main window, uninstall silently and wait until the install directory, the `HKCU` uninstall entry and the process are gone (the NSIS uninstaller relaunches itself, so its own exit code proves nothing), then check that the sentinel is unchanged. Linux: install the deb, check its dependencies and `ldd`, start the binary under Xvfb, extract the AppImage. macOS: mount the dmg, check the app binary and its architecture with `lipo`; the dmgs are not started.
**Rationale:** Hosted runners are the only clean machines available; checking the end state of the uninstall (not an exit code) avoids a false pass.
**Limits:** This is not a test on a real desktop: macOS images are never started, the Linux start runs under Xvfb, and no Wayland or FUSE AppImage run happens. These stay owner checks (`Docs/release-checklist.md`, known gaps 12 and 16).

## 2026-11-17 – M6 dependencies: cargo-about, and third-party notices in the installers
**Context:** The installers redistribute Rust crates, npm packages and two OFL-1.1 fonts, whose licences require the notices to travel with the binary.
**Decision:** `node tools/third-party-notices.mjs <out-file>` writes one Markdown file: the Rust part from `cargo about generate --workspace --locked --fail -o <tmp file> tools/notices/about.hbs` (`about.toml`: `accepted` mirrors the `deny.toml` allow-list, targets are the Windows, macOS and Linux triples, dev dependencies ignored) and the UI part from `pnpm licenses list --json`, limited to `@tauri-apps/api`, `@xterm/*`, `svelte` and the two `@fontsource-variable/*` fonts, each with its licence file text. It exits 1 when one of them has no licence text or is not installed (pure function `renderUiNotices`, tested through `--fixture <json>` in `tools/test-release-scripts.sh`); cargo-about itself fails on a licence outside `accepted`. cargo-about is pinned to **0.9.3** (published 2026-10-26, three weeks before use; needs `--features cli` to build its binary): the `build` job runs `cargo install --locked cargo-about@0.9.3 --features cli`, then generates `src-tauri/resources/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` before `pnpm tauri build`; `bundle.resources` ships it (guarded by `notices_are_a_bundle_resource` in `src-tauri/tests/tauri_conf.rs`). `cargo about` fetches licence files of badly packaged crates from the network, so a run takes about a minute; it hangs when its stdout is redirected on Windows (Git Bash) and refuses in PowerShell, hence `-o`.
**Committed placeholder:** `tauri-build` checks `bundle.resources` paths at compile time (a missing file makes plain `cargo check -p fernjo-app` fail with "resource path ... doesn't exist"), so `src-tauri/resources/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` is committed as a one-line placeholder and not gitignored. A local run of the script overwrites it; restore with `git checkout -- src-tauri/resources/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` and never commit the generated output.
**Rationale:** Generating at release time keeps the notices in step with `Cargo.lock` and `pnpm-lock.yaml` without a 100+ KB generated file in the history.
**Rejected alternatives:** a gitignored generated file (breaks every plain cargo build); committing the generated file (goes stale, noisy diffs); `cargo-license` or `cargo-bundle-licenses` (no per-licence template with full texts as flexible as cargo-about).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 performance measurements: definitions and results
**Context:** Spec section 6 sets manual targets: cold start to a usable window under 2 s, idle memory under about 150 MB, 500 connections filter without visible lag. M6 must show them as numbers.
**Decision:** `tools/measure-performance.ps1` (Windows PowerShell, helper `tools/perf/cdp.mjs` over the WebView2 DevTools port, no OS input) measures the release binary on a scratch `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` with the generated 500-connection tree. Cold start runs from `Start-Process` to the tree area being populated; idle memory is the sum of private working sets 30 s after usable, reported for `fernjo.exe` alone and for the whole process tree with the WebView2 children; filter is M2's method (second painted frame, 20 alternating worst-case updates). Definitions and raw output are in `Docs/performance.md`.
**Results (Ryzen 7 3800X, 15.9 GB, Windows 11 build 26200, release binary of commit `2965077`, three sessions of 10 runs):** cold start medians 932, 954 and 992 ms (worst run 1092 ms); idle memory 3.1-3.7 MiB fernjo only and 60.8-69.5 MiB with WebView; filter median 29.5-32.7 ms, worst 70.9 ms, first cold update 125-159 ms (accepted as in M2, whose cold first keystroke was 163 ms). All three targets met, no fix needed. Linux and macOS are owner-run (commands in `Docs/performance.md`).
**Probe correction:** the first probe accepted any `.empty` element, which the main area and the properties panel render as soon as the shell mounts, so it fired about 35 ms before the tree snapshot was loaded (880/858/852 ms medians). It now accepts only rows in `[role=tree]` or the tree pane's "No connections yet" offer; the numbers above come from that probe.
**Rationale:** Private working set is the figure that does not double-count WebView2's shared pages; both readings are recorded so the definition cannot flatter the result unseen.
**Rejected alternatives:** a Rust-side timing hook in the app (adds measurement code to the shipped binary); measuring "usable" by window appearance (the window shows before the tree is populated).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 security review: SSH algorithm policy
**Context:** The v0.1.0 security review compares what `fernjo-ssh` offers with OpenSSH's current defaults (`ssh -G localhost` of OpenSSH 10.3p1 on the review machine) and flags SHA-1 signatures, CBC ciphers, 3DES and the SHA-1 or group1 key exchanges.
**Decision:** `fernjo-ssh` keeps russh's `Preferred` lists (`russh` 0.65.2, `client::Config::default()`) and changes only the host key list and, for a host with a stored key, restricts it further (`restrict_host_key_algorithms`). What a connection offers:
- **Key exchange:** `mlkem768x25519-sha256`, `curve25519-sha256`, `curve25519-sha256@libssh.org`, `diffie-hellman-group-exchange-sha256`, `diffie-hellman-group18-sha512`, `group17-sha512`, `group16-sha512`, `group15-sha512`, `group14-sha256` (plus the extension markers for ext-info and strict kex). Not offered: `diffie-hellman-group1-sha1`, `group14-sha1`, `group-exchange-sha1` (russh implements them but keeps them out of its defaults). Checked on 2026-11-18 against `ssh -G localhost` of OpenSSH_10.3p1 (its `kexalgorithms` line): all of these are in OpenSSH's default list, including the libssh alias `curve25519-sha256@libssh.org`, except `diffie-hellman-group15-sha512` and `diffie-hellman-group17-sha512`, which OpenSSH 10.3 does not list by default (they are stronger than the listed `group14-sha256`, so offering them weakens nothing). OpenSSH's own extras (`sntrup761x25519-sha512`, `ecdh-sha2-nistp*`) are not offered by fernjo.
- **Ciphers:** `chacha20-poly1305@openssh.com`, `aes256-gcm@openssh.com`, `aes256-ctr`, `aes192-ctr`, `aes128-ctr`. No CBC, no 3DES, no arcfour. All in OpenSSH's default list.
- **MACs:** `hmac-sha2-512-etm@openssh.com`, `hmac-sha2-256-etm@openssh.com`, `hmac-sha2-512`, `hmac-sha2-256`. No SHA-1 MAC (OpenSSH still lists `hmac-sha1`; fernjo does not).
- **Host keys:** `ssh-ed25519`, `ecdsa-sha2-nistp256/384/521`, `rsa-sha2-512`, `rsa-sha2-256`. **Changed in M6:** russh's default list also ends with plain `ssh-rsa` (RSA signature over SHA-1), which OpenSSH stopped offering in 8.8; `client_config` now removes it. A host whose only key is an old RSA host key signed with SHA-1 therefore fails the handshake, as it does with a current `ssh` unless the user re-enables `ssh-rsa`. With a stored key only the stored algorithm is offered (RSA: SHA-512, then SHA-256, never SHA-1).
- **User authentication with an RSA key:** the signature hash is what the server announces (`server-sig-algs`: SHA-512, then SHA-256), SHA-256 when it announces nothing; SHA-1 only when the server lists `ssh-rsa` and nothing better. Signature over the session id, so no MITM gains from it; accepted.
- **Compression:** `none` first, then `zlib` and `zlib@openssh.com` (negotiated with the server, never forced).

Two tests in `crates/fernjo-ssh/src/session.rs` guard this. `client_config_offers_exactly_the_reviewed_algorithms` asserts the exact lists above, in order, for key exchange (including the extension markers), ciphers, MACs, host key algorithms and compression, so any algorithm a russh upgrade adds, drops or reorders fails it and has to be reviewed (update the test and this entry together; shown to fail when an extra algorithm, even a strong one, is added to the config). `client_config_offers_no_weak_algorithms` is a second, independent deny-list (SHA-1 or group1 key exchange, SHA-1 or MD5 MAC, CBC, 3DES, arcfour, `none` ciphers, plain `ssh-rsa`) with its own messages, so a careless update of the exact lists still cannot slip a weak algorithm in.
**Rationale:** Matching OpenSSH's own removal decisions keeps the policy defensible without a home-made list; the weak entries that russh implements for legacy servers are simply not offered.
**Rejected alternatives:** offering `ssh-rsa` behind a per-connection switch (a setting nobody asked for; revisit if a real server needs it); a hand-written algorithm list (drifts from russh's tested defaults on every upgrade).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 security review: data files are owner-only on Unix
**Context:** `write_atomic` (`fernjo-core`), the vault writer and the `known_hosts` writer created their temp files with `File::create` and the folder with `create_dir_all`, so on Linux and macOS the default umask (usually 022) left `connections.json`, `settings.json`, `vault.fernjo`, `known_hosts` and the `.bak` copies readable by other local users (host names, user names, the encrypted vault).
**Decision:** On Unix the temp file is created with mode 0600 (and set to 0600 again, which also fixes a stale temp file), the `.bak` copy is set to 0600 after `fs::copy`, and every directory that fernjo creates (a new data folder and any missing parent of it, through `DirBuilder` with mode 0700 and `recursive`) gets 0700; an existing folder keeps its mode, a file written by an older build is tightened by its next save. Windows keeps inherited ACLs. Each of the three crates carries its own 30-line helper (`create_private_file`, `create_private_dir_all`, `owner_only`) because they do not share a crate. `write_exact` (exports to a path the user picked) and `copy_atomic` of the data-folder move (`fs::copy` keeps the source file's mode) are unchanged on purpose. Unix-only tests: `data_files_are_owner_only_and_a_new_folder_is_private` (core), `vault_files_are_owner_only_and_a_new_folder_is_private` (vault), `known_hosts_is_owner_only_and_a_new_folder_is_private` (ssh).
**Trade-off:** a data folder that is synced or shared (the README advertises syncing it) and that another OS user is meant to read now yields files that only the owner can read once fernjo has written them; a sync tool that restores the files with its own modes can undo this, and fernjo tightens them again at the next save. That is intended (host names, user names and the vault are personal), but anyone who relied on group-readable files has to loosen the modes themselves, knowing the next save resets them.
**Rejected alternatives:** a umask call at startup (process-wide, affects files the user's session creates, not thread-safe); a shared crate for three small functions.

## 2026-11-18 – M6 security review: RUSTSEC-2023-0071 ignore removed
**Decision:** `cargo update -p russh` (0.65.1 to 0.65.2) moves `rsa` to 0.10.0 (with `ssh-key` 0.7.0-rc.12 and `pkcs1` 0.8.0); `cargo deny check` reports no advisory for it, so the time-boxed ignore is gone from `deny.toml`. The other ignores each still match a crate in `Cargo.lock` (`cargo deny check advisories` lists no unused entry). `RUSTSEC-2024-0429` (`glib` 0.18.5, via Tauri's GTK3 stack, Linux only) stays a known gap, see "CI toolchain: plain rustup, UI build first, hosted Ubuntu only; gitleaks CLI for the history scan".

## 2026-11-18 – M6 security review: import limits
**Context:** An independent review of `fernjo-import` and the tree (inputs treated as hostile) found two High and one Medium problem. **H1:** `roxmltree` has no limit on attributes or namespace declarations and both are superlinear: one element with 100 000 attributes took 21 s, 2 500 nested elements each declaring `xmlns:pN` took 6 s and 5 000 took 55 s (about cubic, so 1 MB is effectively infinite). `import_cancel` cannot stop a running parse. **H2:** a name was copied into every report line, folder trail and "uses the credential of folder X" message before the 200-character clip, so one 16 MB container name with 10 000 children took 41 s (name length x node count). **M1:** `MAX_TREE_DEPTH` was enforced only in `graft` and `replace_tree`, so a legal depth-60 import plus a few hand-made folders produced a `connections.json` (and a `.bak` copy of the previous one) that `persist::load` refuses past level 62: the whole tree is inaccessible after the next start.
**Decision:** **H1, pre-parse scan** (`mremoteng/scan.rs`, run by `file::parse`, so also on the decrypted body of a full-file file): one linear pass over the bytes, no allocation, that finds the most attributes in one start tag and the number of `xmlns` names. A tag with more than 200 attributes (`MAX_ATTRIBUTES_PER_TAG`; a node of the owner's test export `owner-confCons.xml` (throwaway IPs and passwords, confirmed by the owner on 2026-11-18) carries 113 or 114) or a document with more than 8 `xmlns` names (`MAX_XMLNS_DECLARATIONS`; a real file has one, `xmlns:mrng` on the root) is `too_complex` before `roxmltree` sees it. The scan is quote-aware the way the parser is (a quoted value ends at the first quote of the same kind, an attribute value cannot contain `<`; comments end at the first `-->` after `<!--`, CDATA at `]]>`, processing instructions at `?>`; every attribute has a quoted value, so the opening quotes are counted), and it errs only towards counting more: a test generates random well-formed documents with hostile decoys in values, comments and CDATA and compares the scan with the parser. It is a counter, not a parser (the "hand-written XML scanner" rejected in "M5 mRemoteNG format facts and mapping" would have replaced `roxmltree`; this one only guards it). Worst case under the caps, a 32 MiB file of tags with 197 attributes each, reads in 1.6 s (release). This supersedes the earlier "accepted, the owner picks the file" note for the single-element attribute case. **H2, caps and no per-node copies:** attribute strings are cut when the file is read (`file.rs`): `Name` 256 characters, `Username`, `Domain` and `PuttySession` 256, `Descr` 4096, `Protocol` 64, `Hostname` 256 (a DNS name has at most 253), `Password` 16 KiB. A name, description, username or domain over its cap is cut and one report line on its node says so ("shortened to N"); a value at the cap is read unchanged. A host over the cap is dropped, never cut into a different host (empty host, one problem line); a protocol over the cap is never taken for SSH2 or RDP even if what is left reads like one (skipped, listed); a password attribute over the cap counts as not decryptable without deriving a key (the work budget still counts it). `RawNode.clipped` (`Clipped`) carries the flags to the mapper. The mapper shows at most the first 200 characters of a name wherever it goes into a trail or message (`display`, the same 200 the report line keeps, so short names read exactly as before), builds a trail path no longer than a line can show, and shares the credential and username folder names as `Rc<str>`, so the cost no longer scales with name length x node count (the scaled review inputs now take well under 0.1 s instead of 7 and 16 s). **M1, the limit on every path:** `Tree::insert` (behind `create_folder`, `create_connection`, `duplicate` and `graft`) and `Tree::move_node` refuse a node whose subtree would reach past `MAX_TREE_DEPTH` with `too_deep` (`TreeError::TooDeep`, a normal command error the UI shows); a move checks the height of the whole moved subtree before anything is taken out; reordering inside a parent is unaffected. `Store::mutate` additionally refuses to save a mutation that left the tree deeper than the limit and deeper than before (defence in depth; a deeper tree written by an older version can still be edited, just not deepened). This closes the follow-up "hand-made nodes are not checked" of "M5 final review fixes".
**Rationale:** The caps sit where the value is read and where it is first copied, not in the UI, and refuse or cut instead of reducing precision silently. Limits of 200 attributes and 8 namespaces leave a margin of about 1.75 times over the owner's test export (throwaway IPs and passwords, confirmed by the owner on 2026-11-18; 114 attributes on its largest node, counted in `owner-confCons.xml`) for attributes and 8 times for namespaces (a real file declares one); a future mRemoteNG version that adds many node attributes would have to raise `MAX_ATTRIBUTES_PER_TAG`. The depth rule belongs in the tree so no caller (command, import, restore, duplicate) can write what `persist::load` cannot read.
**Rejected alternatives:** switching to a streaming parser such as `quick-xml` with its own limits (a new parser and a rewrite of the reader for two counters); a time limit or cancel flag for the parse (the cost is known from the bytes, so refusing is simpler); refusing the whole file for a long name or description (a legitimate long description would then lose the whole import); making `persist::load` independent of serde_json's recursion limit (a stacker dependency, and `backup::open` would need the same).

## 2026-11-18 – M6 security review: vault file size cap and known_hosts host validation
**Context:** Two Medium/Low findings of the independent reviews. **Vault M1:** `Vault::open`, `unlock` and `unlock_with_key` read `vault.fernjo` with `fs::read`, and `AppState::build` runs `Vault::open` (and the keychain auto-unlock) at every start, also in a second, intercepted launch. A huge synced or planted file (valid magic and version, KDF fields in range) therefore stalled or broke every start with no password needed. **SSH L1:** the host string went unchecked into `known_hosts`: after `add("evil,victim.corp", 22, K)`, `check("victim.corp", 22, K)` returned `Known` (the comma makes an alias), and a newline in the host wrote a second, attacker-shaped line (probed by the reviewer).
**Decision, vault:** `fernjo_vault::MAX_FILE_LEN` (`format.rs`, `pub const`) is 64 MiB. Every read of the vault file goes through `read_capped(path, max)`: it refuses when `metadata().len()` is over the cap, so an oversized file is not allocated, and it also reads through `take(max + 1)` and refuses when more than `max` bytes came back, so a file that grows between the check and the read cannot get past it. The error is `VaultError::Unreadable("the file is larger than 64 MiB")` (code `unreadable`), so `AppState` classifies it like any other unreadable file: `VaultState::Unusable`, never overwritten, truncated or deleted (`vault_create` answers `already_exists`), and the app starts normally. `open` uses the same bounded read as `unlock` (it needs only the 166-byte header, but the cap check keeps "an oversized file is unusable" one rule instead of a state where a vault opens and then refuses every unlock). The save path already reads only the header (`check_unchanged_on_disk`), and `.bak` is only ever written (`fs::copy`), never read by the vault. **Why 64 MiB:** a vault entry is a password or a private key, a few KB at most (an RSA 4096 PEM is about 3.4 KB), so a vault with several hundred entries is about 1 MB; 64 MiB is more than 50 times that and still fits tens of thousands of entries (the body is held several times over while it is decrypted and parsed, so the peak memory stays in the low hundreds of MiB). Tests: `read_capped_accepts_up_to_the_cap_and_refuses_one_byte_more` (boundary with a small cap), `an_oversized_vault_file_is_refused_by_open_unlock_and_unlock_with_key` (a sparse file of cap + 1 bytes made with `set_len`) in `vault.rs`, and `an_oversized_vault_file_is_unusable_and_stays_untouched` (`AppState` level, `vault_commands.rs`). Not covered on purpose: `connections.json` and `settings.json` are read with `fs::read` as well (a same-kind problem, outside this finding; backlog).
