## 2026-11-22 – M9 follow-up candidate: one set of hardened file helpers

**Context:** the atomic write with a random `create_new` temp name, fsync and directory fsync, and the capped read (the hub has the write only) now exist in five places: `fernjo-vault` (`write_atomic`, `read_capped`), `fernjo-ssh` (`known_hosts`), `fernjo-rdp` (`CertStore`), `fernjo-sync` (`files.rs`, no `.bak`) and `fernjo-hub` (`store.rs`), next to `fernjo_core::persist` (which keeps the `.bak`). Each is a deliberate copy because those crates must not depend on one another.
**Decision:** no change in M9. Consolidating them into `fernjo_core::persist` (or a tiny shared crate) is a backlog candidate; a fix to one copy must be checked against the other four copies until then.
Superseded by: M11: one set of hardened file helpers (`fernjo-fs`)

## 2026-11-23 – M10 session targets and quick connect

**Context:** quick connect (`[ssh://|rdp://]user@host[:port]`, nothing saved) needs the session manager to open a session that has no tree node. The webview is untrusted, so whatever it sends is checked in Rust. Spec: `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-23-m10-daily-use-features-design.md`.
**Decision:** (1) A session's target is `Target::Node(NodeId)` or `Target::Quick(QuickTarget)` (`connect_plan.rs`; `Entry.target` in `sessions.rs`), so Reconnect works for a quick session and nothing is written to `connections.json` or the vault. `QuickTarget` and the parser `parse_quick` live in `fernjo-core` (`quick.rs`); no scheme means SSH, an SSH target needs a user, an RDP user is optional, an IPv6 host needs brackets, and the `quick_*` error codes carry a plain sentence for the inline error. (2) One checker, `validate_quick`, runs inside `parse_quick` and again in `resolve_quick`, so a hand-built `QuickTarget` sent to `open_quick_session` gets the same rules as typed text (port 0, control characters, `@`, a missing SSH user, over-long fields), and the host also passes `fernjo_ssh::validate_host` (`invalid_host`). `quick_parse` runs `resolve_quick` as well, so `invalid_host` shows inline under the field and no tab appears for a host that would be refused. (3) A quick SSH session has `CredentialPlan::None`: the agent is tried, then keyboard-interactive and the typed-password prompt in the tab. A quick RDP session asks for the password (and the user name when none was typed) in the tab, has no domain, fit-to-tab resolution, the default colour depth and **the clipboard switched off**. (4) Host key confirmations and RDP certificate decisions are the same as for a saved connection.
**Rationale:** one sum type keeps every path that used to take a node id honest (`resolve_target`), and validating in Rust costs one function. A quick target is a host typed ad hoc, often not trusted yet, so the saved-connection default of RDP clipboard on would copy the local clipboard to an unknown machine; save the host as a connection to get it.
**Rejected alternatives:** a hidden temporary node in the tree (writes `connections.json` and syncs a connection nobody saved); trusting the webview's parse (the `known_hosts` name rules must not depend on it); parsing in TypeScript (all logic is Rust); clipboard on by default for quick RDP.
**Layout:** the field adds a 36 px row to the sidebar (`SIDEBAR_CHROME` is head 44, quick connect 36, foot 37), so `TREE_RESERVE` in `layout.svelte.ts` went from 120 to 156 and `TREE_ROWS_DEFAULT` from 23 to 21 (the `.tree-slot` minimum in `Sidebar.svelte` uses the same numbers; a test pins both). At 1280x800 the panel keeps 179 px against 167 px before M10. The constants are declared, not measured in the real app (owner check).
**Limits:** trusting a host key from a quick connect adds that host to `known_hosts` in the data folder, as any trusted host does, so a synced folder learns the host name; the title of a quick tab is `user@host` without the port.

## 2026-11-23 – M10 restore tabs: open-tabs file, disconnected restore, Reconnect all

**Context:** the owner wants yesterday's tabs back after a restart without a prompt wall or network traffic nobody asked for.
**Decision:** (1) The UI reports the ordered tab list and the active tab (`save_open_tabs`); Rust validates it (at most `MAX_TABS`, 50; exactly one of `node` and `quick` per entry; quick targets pass `validate_quick`) and writes `open-tabs.json` (schema 1) in the **device folder**, not the synced data folder: tabs are per device. The file holds targets only (a node id, or protocol, host, port and user of a quick connect), never a name, a credential or a copy of settings; title, protocol and whether the vault is involved come from the tree at restore time (`TargetView` is the one wire type for a target, `{node}` xor `{quick}`). (2) On start `get_open_tabs` returns the tabs that still resolve, and the UI creates **disconnected** tabs with the detail `RESTORED_TEXT` ("Restored from your last session. Not connected.") through `restore_session` (`SessionManager::restore`); nothing connects by itself. Each tab has the normal Reconnect, and the tab strip has "Reconnect all": tabs in tab order, at most three connecting at a time (a tab waiting on a prompt does not count), the vault is asked for once and only if a pending tab uses a vault credential. (3) The setting "Reopen tabs on start" (`reopenTabs` in `settings.json`, default on; Settings, "Tabs"); with it off `get_open_tabs` returns no tabs and nothing is saved. (4) The reader is a private bounded read with a 1 MiB cap in `open_tabs.rs`, because `fernjo_core`'s `read_capped` is `pub(crate)`; this is a third private copy (see "M9 follow-up candidate: one set of hardened file helpers") and an exception to the rule that a reader uses the capped helper (the file is not in the data folder). Writes use a random `open-tabs.json.<uuid>.tmp` with `create_new` (0600 on Unix) and rename. A missing, damaged, oversized or newer-schema file reads as empty and is replaced by the next save, so it can never stop the app.
**Pitfalls handled (each has a test):** the UI saves only once `ready` (after the first load) and only when the list differs from the last saved or restored one, so becoming ready never writes back what was just read; `SessionStore.closeAll` (window unload, a sync join, an import restore) bumps `bulkClosed`, and the save logic then skips and cancels instead of storing an empty list; a failed load leaves `ready` false, so a failed read never overwrites the file; the active index is remapped when entries are dropped.
A restored tab that is still flagged `restored` shows no lamp in the tab strip (an empty mark keeps the alignment) and none in the tree (`SessionStore.lampFor`), because the red "dropped" lamp means a lost connection; the flag is cleared when the user connects, and every later drop is a normal "dropped".
**Rationale:** disconnected-first answers "does reconnecting ask first": nothing connects, prompts or touches the network without a click, and one click reconnects everything. A device-folder file keeps the layout out of sync and out of backups.
**Rejected alternatives:** auto-reconnect on start (prompt wall, the anti-goal in the spec); deriving the list in Rust from running sessions (loses order and the active tab, which are view state); storing tab titles or protocol (go stale when the tree is edited); keeping the file in the data folder (would carry one device's tabs to the others).
**Known limits:** a change made in the last 300 ms before the window unloads is not saved (accepted); a scratch run with `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` restores its own tabs at the next start (the file is in that run's `.device` folder); after a sync join or an import restore the file keeps the earlier list until the next user change (Rust drops ids that no longer exist); "Reconnect all" does nothing visible when the vault status is unloaded or unusable and skips a tab whose restore call has not returned yet (a second click works); the vault wait for "Reconnect all" ends only when the vault is unlocked or the button is pressed again; a restored tab that is not visible may attach at the default 80x24 size (owner check).

## 2026-11-23 – M10 vault auto-lock: Rust clock and capture-phase heartbeat

**Context:** the vault should lock after a configurable idle time. A hung or reloaded page must not silently stop the lock.
**Decision:** Rust owns the clock: `IdleTracker` (`idle_lock.rs`) holds the time of the last activity on two injected clocks, an `Instant` and a `SystemTime`, and the idle time is the larger of the two elapsed values (`Instant` stops while the machine sleeps on macOS and Linux, so a laptop closed for hours would otherwise show only seconds of idle time on wake and the first mouse move would reset it; the wall clock keeps running, so the vault locks on the first tick after wake); a thread started in `setup` calls `tick` every 5 s, which locks through the same path as `vault_lock` and emits the event `vault-status` (payload `VaultStatus`) so the UI follows. The UI only sends a heartbeat, the command `note_activity` (returns nothing), at most once per 5 s while `keydown`, `pointerdown`, `pointermove` or `wheel` arrive; the listeners (`ui/src/lib/activity.ts`) are passive and in the **capture phase** on the window, so the terminal and the remote desktop, which stop propagation, still count. The limit is `vaultAutoLockMinutes` in `settings.json` (absent = off, the default; 1 to 1440, `AUTO_LOCK_MINUTES_RANGE`; `invalid_auto_lock` otherwise; a stored value outside the range is ignored at start); commands `get_vault_auto_lock` and `set_vault_auto_lock`; Settings, "Vault" section, saves on change, an empty field means off. Under `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` the choice is memory-only. `IdleTracker::note` never moves the steady clock backwards, a wall clock that went back counts as zero idle time (the steady value decides, so it can never delay the lock) and the next activity takes the earlier wall time over, and a lowered limit applies at once because it is read at every check. Connected sessions keep running; only new credential lookups need the unlock again.
**Rationale:** a Rust timer fed by input evidence is the variant where "the page is stuck" still ends in a lock. Capture phase is needed because terminal and RDP views handle keys and pointer events themselves.
**Rejected alternatives:** a UI timer that calls `vault_lock` (a reloaded or hung page stops locking); locking on OS sleep or screen lock (a non-goal of the spec, and platform code per OS).
**Known limits:** a wall clock set by hand forward by hours (or a large clock correction) can lock early, which is the safe direction; startup auto-unlock ("Unlock automatically on this device") is startup-only, so with it on and an idle lock the vault stays locked until the next restart or until the master password is typed; number input text that does not parse (for example "1e") is refused with a message and the previous value is kept (`validity.badInput` in `SettingsDialog.svelte`); the field stays disabled without a message when the stored value cannot be read; a successful unlock does not count as activity by itself (the next input does); the idle thread has no `catch_unwind`; not run on a real idle machine (owner check).

## 2026-11-23 – M10 Pageant: pick one source

**Context:** on Windows the agent was only the OpenSSH named pipe; the owner uses Pageant.
**Decision:** on Windows `agent::connect()` tries sources in order: the OpenSSH pipe, then Pageant (`AgentClient::connect_pageant`). A source counts only if it answers `request_identities` with at least one key; the first one that does is used for the whole sign-in (`first_with_identities` over boxed lazy sources, so Pageant is not even contacted when the pipe has keys). If both hold keys, only the OpenSSH agent is used. Linux and macOS are unchanged (`SSH_AUTH_SOCK`). No setting: Pageant is tried automatically. The pipe and Pageant share the caller's one connect timeout (a busy pipe can use it up; the result fails safe, without Pageant).
**Rationale:** a signature must go to the agent that holds the identity; one connection, one identity list and one sign step keep the existing agent code (including `agent_sign_timeout`) unchanged.
**Rejected alternatives:** merging the identities of both agents (needs a proxy that routes each signature to the right agent); Pageant first (the OpenSSH agent is the Windows default); a "use Pageant" setting (the spec says no new settings).
**Limits:** not tried with a real Pageant (owner check, see the roadmap); the Linux and macOS builds are compiled by CI only.

## 2026-11-23 – M10 native clipboard: BSL-1.0 allowed

**Context:** the context-menu Paste used `navigator.clipboard.readText`, which waits for a WebView2 permission prompt; a 2.5 s timeout gave up. The owner decided on 2026-11-23 that the BSL-1.0 licence (`clipboard-win`, pulled by `arboard`) may go into the allow lists. This supersedes the paste part of "M4 clipboard path" (copy still uses the browser clipboard; the RDP clipboard of "M8 clipboard path" is unchanged).
**Decision:** `arboard` 3.7.0 with `default-features = false, features = ["wayland-data-control"]` (text only); `BSL-1.0` is in `deny.toml` (`allow`) and `about.toml` (`accepted`), which stay in step so the third-party notices list `clipboard-win`. `clipboard_read_text` (`src-tauri/src/clipboard.rs`) is an `async` command that moves the read into `spawn_blocking` and creates the `arboard` clipboard per call; it refuses text above `MAX_PASTE_BYTES` (1 MiB, `clipboard_too_large`) and its errors carry only fixed text (`clipboard_unavailable`), never the clipboard content or the OS error. A `ClipboardText` trait lets the guard table and unit tests use a fake. The terminal's menu Paste (`TerminalDeps.readClipboard`) calls it; a failure shows nothing and does not hang; the old timeout and its tests are gone.
**Exposure, by design:** the command returns clipboard text, and a clipboard can hold a password, so `clipboard_read_text` joins vault reveal and `sync_pairing_code` as a response that may carry a secret. The command checks no user gesture: any script running in the webview can read the clipboard silently. The webview only loads fernjo's own bundle under a CSP and the navigation guard (see "Webview hardening from day one"), so this equals the trust placed in the rest of the page. The no-secret guard (`no_response_contains_a_secret`) has a narrow carve-out: the response must contain the sentinel clipboard text and no other sentinel.
**Rejected alternatives:** keeping the keyboard paste path only (the menu Paste stays broken); a Tauri clipboard plugin (another plugin and permission surface for one text read).
**Limits:** the `arboard` read has no timeout of its own; a real paste in WebView2 and the compile of the Wayland data-control feature on Linux and macOS are open (the latter by CI, the former an owner check).

## 2026-11-23 – M10 quit confirmation on ExitRequested

**Context:** the M7 confirmation intercepts `CloseRequested` of the main window only; Cmd+Q on macOS leaves through `RunEvent::ExitRequested` and ended connected sessions without a question.
**Decision:** `run()` builds the app and runs it with a callback; on `RunEvent::ExitRequested` the pure function `exit_action(code, connected, prompt_open)` (`sessions.rs`) decides: `Allow` for an explicit exit code (`Some(_)`: restart, update, `app.exit(n)`) or when no session is connected, `Prevent` when the dialog is already open, otherwise `Ask`. `Ask` calls `prevent_exit` and shows the same dialog through the shared `ask_to_close` that the `CloseRequested` path uses (one `close_prompt_open` flag). "Close" runs `close_all` and destroys the window; the destruction raises `ExitRequested` without a code, which sees no connected session and is allowed, so there is no loop.
**Rationale:** one dialog function for both paths cannot drift, and the pure decision is testable on every OS.
**Rejected alternatives:** a separate dialog for the quit path (two code paths to keep equal).
**Limits (owner checks on macOS):** real Cmd+Q with a connected session; logout or shutdown is also held until the dialog is answered; when no main window exists `Ask` prevents the exit and shows nothing (a hardening not done).

**Update 2026-11-27 (read in the dependency sources, not run on a Mac):** a real Cmd+Q most likely never raises `ExitRequested`. The app sets no menu, so Tauri 2.12 installs its default macOS menu, whose Quit item (`PredefinedMenuItem::quit`) sends `terminate:` (muda 0.20.0); the app delegate of tao 0.37.2 implements `applicationWillTerminate:` but not `applicationShouldTerminate:`, so nothing can hold that quit, and tauri-runtime-wry 2.12.5 raises `ExitRequested` only when the last window was destroyed and for an exit the app itself asks for. Cmd+Q (and the Dock's Quit) would then end connected sessions without a question, and the M13 hold of an exit during an install would not apply to it. The macOS owner check decides; a fix is in the roadmap backlog.

## 2026-11-23 – M10 M4 follow-ups: what was done, what stayed in the backlog

**Context:** the roadmap item "M4 review follow-ups" listed many leftovers; the spec took only the real bugs.
**Done:** (1) *Host key write order* (`TrustAlways` and `Replace` wrote `known_hosts` inside `check_server_key`): the decision is parked (`PendingTrust`; `TrustAction` is `Add`, `Replace` or `Confirm`, the last for a key already in `known_hosts` that only this device confirms) and `Session::connect` writes it through `apply_pending_trust` after the handshake succeeded; a failed handshake leaves both files unchanged. **Correction to the original finding:** russh 0.65.2 verifies the key-exchange signature before it calls `check_server_key`, so the old order did not accept an unproved key; the change is defence in depth that no longer depends on that library detail, and the `handler.rs` doc comments that said otherwise were corrected. A failed `known_hosts` write is still an `SshError`; the confirmed file stays best effort. The real `TrustAlways` flow after the handshake is covered by unit tests, not by the Docker integration tests. (2) *Unbounded session input queue*: `InputBudget` (`session_flow.rs`, `MAX_QUEUED_INPUT` 8 MiB) is reserved before an input op is queued (`Op::weight`: data bytes, RDP events times the event size, clipboard bytes; resize and close weigh 0) and released by the writer task for every handled op, on the stop paths and for ops dropped with the queue; over the cap the call answers `input_queue_full` and the session is untouched. (3) *Chords on non-Latin layouts*: `routeKey` falls back to the letter of `e.code` when `e.key` is not a Latin letter, so Ctrl+Shift chords work on, for example, a Cyrillic layout; plain Ctrl+letter still belongs to the shell. (4) *Ctrl+Shift+W with the vault view open* no longer closes a hidden tab (`tabToClose`).
**Stayed in the backlog (block "Source: M4 review follow-ups", reduced to these):** `SshError` carries no source and the error mapping is coarse; `known_hosts` BOM, duplicate host in an entry and test gaps; the russh task and descriptor leak on a pre-key-exchange timeout; the `tauri_plugin_opener` init script; the agent test name; terminal `ResizeObserver` coalescing, incremental `findNext` and the 13 px search bar; the tab and prompt accessibility items; the cosmetic items. New from the M10 reviews: `Op::weight` counts payload only (a flood of 1-byte writes costs more memory than the cap suggests; a minimum weight would fix it) and `Resize` is unbounded; IME and dead keys (`key` "Dead" or "Process") with a letter `code` count as chord letters; with sync on a restore is pushed to the other devices (`note_local_edit`) and the restore confirmation does not say so; the unparseable auto-lock number input.
**Rationale:** the real bugs and the cheap robust fixes go in; cosmetic and speculative items cost review time in M11, which reviews the whole.

## 2026-11-24 – M11 threat model

**Context:** the model so far is spread over "Webview hardening from day one", the M6 security entries and the M9 and M10 entries, and no single entry covers sync and the M10 features (quick connect, open tabs, vault auto-lock, Pageant, native paste) next to SSH and RDP. M11 (`Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-24-m11-hardening-security-design.md`, step 1) reviews everything in 0.3.0, and this entry is the reviewers' frame. It describes the code as it stood when M11 started (branch `feature/m11-hardening-security` before the M11 fixes), checked against the source, not copied from earlier entries. **Weak** or **missing** marks a protection that is thin or absent today; "M11 step 2/3" means the spec already plans a fix. Nothing here is a fix.
**Decision:** the assets, adversaries, cells and trust assumptions below are the model for M11 and for later reviews. A change that moves a cell is recorded in a new entry.

**Assets.**
- *Vault secrets:* passwords, private keys and passphrases in `vault.fernjo` (and `.bak`), the master password, the data key and the decrypted entries in memory while unlocked, the auto-unlock keychain item.
- *Sync key and hub secret:* one keychain item (`fernjo` / `sync-<group id>`), both inside the pairing code.
- *`connections.json`* (and `.bak`): hosts, user names, credential references (vault ids), RDP settings including the clipboard switch. No secret, but its integrity decides where a credential is sent.
- *`known_hosts`* (data folder, so it travels with a synced folder) and *`known_hosts.confirmed`* (device folder).
- *RDP pins:* `rdp_certs.confirmed` (device folder).
- *Device folder files:* `vault-marks.json` (rollback floor), `sync.json` (`lastSeq`), `sync-base.json` (the base snapshot: the tree in plain text and the vault file bytes), the two confirmed files, `appmenu-never`.
- *`open-tabs.json`* (device folder): targets only (node ids; protocol, host, port and user of quick targets).
- *The webview:* the page, its IPC access to every command, and `localStorage` (layout, selected node ids, theme).

**Trust assumptions (accepted, not defended):**
1. The device and the OS account fernjo runs under are trusted. Any process of the same user can read the keychain items, the device folder and the data folder, write `settings.json` (which names the data folder), delete `vault-marks.json` or `sync.json` (which removes the vault and hub rollback floors), and read process memory. fernjo defends against *other* local users and against the network, not against malware running as the user.
2. A peer device in the sync group holds the sync key and the hub secret and is **trusted with the data**: it can read and rewrite the whole tree, and it holds the vault file, which only the master password protects from it.
3. The hub is **not trusted with content**: it sees only the 34-byte header (group id, `seq`), sizes, times and client addresses. It can withhold, refuse, lose data, roll back (detected against `lastSeq`) and show different devices different histories (not detected). It cannot read or forge a head.
4. The webview runs only fernjo's own bundle. A script that does run there holds the whole command set (it can answer prompts, connect, delete, re-seed, leave sync); the CSP, the navigation guard, text-only rendering and Rust-side validation are the barrier, not per-command checks.
5. Three responses carry a secret by design: vault reveal, `sync_pairing_code`, and `clipboard_read_text` (the clipboard text, read without a user gesture).
6. The pairing code is a secret and its integrity is the user's job: its checksum catches typos, not tampering.
7. Trust on first use: the first SSH host key and the first RDP certificate of a host are decided by the person, and a server the person accepted receives the password of a password, keyboard-interactive or CredSSP login by protocol.
8. Import files, backups and the data folder are chosen by the person; a backup restored with its password is trusted content.

**Cells.** One table per adversary; assets the adversary cannot reach are listed under the table.

*1. Hostile SSH server (or a machine in the path before its key is trusted)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vault secrets | Receive the password of a password or keyboard-interactive login; get a signature bound to its own session from a key or the agent; ask for up to 5 keyboard-interactive rounds. | Host key decided before any authentication (`check_server_key` in `crates/fernjo-ssh/src/handler.rs`: unknown asks, changed asks with the old fingerprints, a key found only in the synced `known_hosts` asks through `HostKeySynced`); russh verifies the key-exchange signature first; algorithm policy (M6); at most 6 authentication attempts (`auth_plan.rs`); `MAX_KI_ROUNDS` 5 and capped prompt text (`cap_ki_round` in `sessions.rs`); no agent, X11 or port forwarding is requested. | Accepted: trust on first use, and a password sent to an accepted server. **Weak, found while writing:** fernjo's `client::Handler` does not override russh 0.65.1's `server_channel_open_*` defaults, which *accept* server-opened `session`, `x11`, `agent_forward`, `forwarded_tcpip` and `direct_tcpip` channels; nothing is wired to them (the agent is not reachable through them), but whether they can hold memory or confuse the session is unchecked (reviewer 2). |
| `known_hosts`, `known_hosts.confirmed` | Get its key written when the person picks "Trust always". | Written only after the handshake (`PendingTrust`, `apply_pending_trust`); host names pass `validate_host` inside `host_port`. | Accepted: a host trusted from quick connect lands in the synced `known_hosts` (its name travels with a synced folder). |
| The webview | Send any byte stream to xterm.js; show links; end the session with any exit status. | xterm renders text only, OSC 52 clipboard writes are not enabled (no clipboard addon); a clicked link goes through `open_external_url`, which allows only `http`/`https` and opens the parsed URL; output flow control is ack-based (`session_flow.rs`); a session counts as ended cleanly only with exit status 0 after at least 5 s (`classify_end`), so a failed login never closes unread. | Accepted: any `http(s)` link in output opens on a plain click. **Weak (known leftover):** a russh session task and its descriptor leak on a pre-key-exchange timeout. |

Not reachable: `connections.json`, RDP pins, sync key and hub secret, `open-tabs.json`, other device folder files.

*2. Hostile RDP server (or a machine in the path before its certificate is pinned)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vault secrets | The real server (or one that knows the account's NT hash) receives user, domain and password through CredSSP's credential delegation once its certificate is accepted. A machine in the path that is not the server and whose certificate the person accepted gets at most a NetNTLMv2 response (offline cracking, or relay to another NTLM service such as SMB without signing or LDAP without channel binding), not the password. Before M11 it could answer X.224 with TLS-only and receive the password in the Client Info PDU. | Order in `crates/fernjo-rdp/src/connect.rs`: X.224 offering CredSSP only, and a confirm that selects no CredSSP protocol (TLS-only, standard RDP security, RDSTLS, ...; or a negotiation failure that means no NLA) refused with `nla_required` before TLS (M11, "M11 RDP: NLA required, TLS-only servers refused"); then TLS with the handshake signature checked against the certificate's key (`tls::upgrade` with the own `PinVerifier` since M11 step 3; chain and name are not checked, the pin decides), then the certificate decision against `CertStore`, and only then the credential prompts and CredSSP. | Accepted: trust on first use (the NetNTLMv2 response of a weak password can be cracked offline, and the response can be relayed to another NTLM service in the network). Accepted API limit: IronRDP keeps the password in a plain `String`. Hosts whose certificate rustls cannot use fail with `certificate_unsupported` (M11 step 3, "M11 RDP TLS: own verifier, strict handshake signature"). |
| Local clipboard (via the webview) | With clipboard sharing on, read the local clipboard (the UI sends it whenever the remote view gets focus or a paste chord is used) and write text into it. | Clipboard off for quick connect; text capped at `MAX_CLIPBOARD_BYTES` (1 MiB) after reassembly, both ways. | Accepted by the owner (2026-11-24): on by default for saved connections; M11 step 3 adds a visible marker. **Missing (M11 step 3):** no cap of fernjo's own on a static or dynamic channel message before it is reassembled (`stage.process` in `crates/fernjo-rdp/src/session.rs`), so a server can announce a huge clipboard message. |
| The app process | Send bitmaps, desktop sizes and channel data; stop reading. | Desktop size checked (`desktop_too_large`); input queue capped by `InputBudget` (8 MiB). | **Missing (M11 step 3):** `writer.write_all` in the session loop has no timeout and the `ops` channel in `src-tauri/src/sessions/rdp.rs` is unbounded, so a server that stops reading keeps a closing session alive. **Weak (known leftover):** `Resize` ops weigh 0 and every op weighs only its payload. |
| RDP pins | Get its certificate pinned on "Trust always". | Pin is per host and port in the device folder. | Accepted: trust on first use. |

Not reachable: `connections.json`, `known_hosts`, sync key and hub secret, `open-tabs.json`.

*3. Hostile hub, or the network path to it*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sync key and hub secret | Learn the hub secret (it is the bearer token of every request). | The sync key never leaves the device; the hub stores only the SHA-256 of the secret (`config.json`, constant-time compare in `crates/fernjo-hub/src/server.rs`). The path: HTTPS only (plain `http` only to literal loopback), no redirects, system roots or the one pinned CA from the pairing code (`crates/fernjo-sync/src/client.rs`). | Accepted: the hub secret only grants hub access. |
| `connections.json`, vault file (content) | Nothing readable: the envelope is XChaCha20-Poly1305 under an HKDF key from the sync key, with magic, version, group and `seq` as associated data (`envelope.rs`). | `open_head` in `engine.rs` checks tag, group, `seq` against the ETag and the snapshot, at most 100 000 nodes, depth 60, and a vault part that parses within the KDF cap; responses read in bounded steps (64 MiB), 3 s connect and 20 s total timeout. | Accepted: metadata (sizes, times, addresses, group id, `seq`). |
| Freshness | Withhold the newest head, show an old one, empty itself, show different devices different histories, answer `412` or garbage forever. | A head below `lastSeq` or an empty hub after a sync is refused (`hub_rolled_back`, nothing written); vault heads below the device's generation mark are refused (`rolled_back`) when only the head changed the vault and the vault is unlocked; while it is locked the older file is written (the newer one stays in `vault.fernjo.bak`) and the next unlock reports `rolled_back` (corrected in M11, review #37); a both-changed merge refuses a head below the base file's generation (`rolled_back`, M11 review #29); at most 5 attempts per run. | Accepted (M9): withholding and forked histories are not detected; a garbage head stops sync until re-seed. |
| The hub host itself | — | Rootless Podman, read-only root file system, no capabilities, the unit publishes `127.0.0.1:8787` by default behind Caddy; auth is checked before a `PUT` body is read; 64 MiB cap per body. | **Weak:** the hub has no request timeout or connection limit of its own (it relies on Caddy and the home network); several authenticated 64 MiB uploads are buffered in memory at once. |

Not reachable: `known_hosts`, RDP pins, `open-tabs.json`, the webview (sync status carries codes and counts, not hub text).

*4. Hostile peer device in the sync group (a lost or compromised device; it holds the sync key and the hub secret)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| `connections.json` | Push any valid tree: rewrite a saved connection's host while its credential reference stays, switch the RDP clipboard on for a folder, delete nodes (C5), re-seed the hub. | Snapshot validation (schema, depth 60, 100 000 nodes); an edit beats a delete (C6); a redirected host is a new host to this device, so the host key or certificate prompt comes before any credential is sent; ten history envelopes on the hub, the local `.bak`, encrypted backups. | Accepted (assumption 2). **Weak:** nothing ties a credential to a host, so a swap between two hosts this device already trusts sends one host's password to the other without a prompt; field lengths in a snapshot are bounded only by the 64 MiB envelope. |
| Vault secrets | Hold the vault file (offline guessing against Argon2id); replace this device's vault with another vault's file when only one side changed it (V1, the replaced vault ends locked); change the master password (V3, notice `passwordChangeDropped`). | Master password and Argon2id; KDF cap; generation marks, `adopt_file` and `adopt_merged` never lower a mark; since M11 (review #29) a both-changed merge refuses a head vault older than the base file (`rolled_back`) instead of installing its header and dropping the entries added since; the vault `.bak`. | Accepted: a peer that knows the master password reads everything. **Weak:** the sync key is not rotated in version 1, so a lost device keeps the ability to decrypt any envelope it obtains later; `rotate-secret` only locks it out of the hub. |
| Device folder files | Its snapshot becomes `sync-base.json`. | Same validation as above; the file holds no secret beyond the encrypted vault bytes. | — |

Not reachable: `known_hosts`, `known_hosts.confirmed`, RDP pins, `open-tabs.json`, `settings.json`.

*5. Writer of the synced data folder (a sync tool, or another device through it)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| `connections.json` | Replace it with any valid tree (as a peer can); plant `connections.json.tmp` or `connections.json.bak` as a symlink. | Fingerprint check before every save (`changed_on_disk`); capped reads (64 MiB); depth 60; reload is a click. | **Weak (M11 step 2):** `persist::write_atomic` (`crates/fernjo-core/src/persist.rs`) opens a *fixed* `<file>.tmp` with create and truncate and copies the old file to `.bak` with `fs::copy`; both follow a planted symlink, so fernjo overwrites the link's target with the tree JSON (the same writer serves `settings.json`, which sits in the default data folder; `write_exact`, used for exports, keeps a fixed temp name too). |
| Vault secrets | Replace `vault.fernjo` with an older, foreign or oversized file; plant `vault.fernjo.bak` as a symlink. | Header guard on save (`changed_on_disk`), and since M11 (review #30) on the sync read too (`Vault::file_bytes`), so a planted file is never pushed to the other devices; rollback marks in the device folder (`rolled_back`), KDF cap, 64 MiB cap, an unreadable file makes the vault `Unusable` and untouched; random `create_new` temp names. | **Weak (M11 step 2):** the `.bak` is written with `fs::copy`, which follows a symlink at `vault.fernjo.bak` (same pattern in `known_hosts`, `CertStore` and the other writer copies). |
| `known_hosts` | Add, remove or change keys. | A key only in `known_hosts` is trusted silently only when `known_hosts.confirmed` (device folder) has it; otherwise `HostKeySynced` asks and shows the earlier fingerprints. | Accepted: the person decides that prompt. Same `.bak` symlink weakness as above. |

Not reachable: RDP pins, `open-tabs.json`, device folder files, sync key and hub secret (`choose_data_folder` refuses a data folder that contains the device folder or lies inside it, `folder_device_dir`; a scratch `FERNJO_DATA_DIR` run keeps its `.device` folder inside, by design for tests).

*6. Hostile import or backup file*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| The app process | Make parsing or key derivation expensive. | mRemoteNG: byte scan before `roxmltree` (attributes, `xmlns` names), 59 container levels, PBKDF2 budget, string caps (`mremoteng/file.rs`); backups: `open_with_password` validates the KDF cost against the cap before Argon2. | — |
| `connections.json` | Add nodes with any host and odd text. | JSON import drops credential references and gives fresh ids; depth 60; `sanitize_nodes` (JSON and backup imports) caps and cleans names, descriptions and hosts. | **Missing (M11 step 3):** `sanitize_nodes` does not cap or clean `settings.username`; `settings.domain` is not cleaned either (not in the M11 plan). |
| Vault secrets | A restored backup replaces the vault and the tree. | The backup password, a confirmation, plan-then-apply (`restore_incomplete`). | Accepted (assumption 8). With sync on, the restore is pushed to every device and the confirmation does not say so (known leftover, wording fix in M11 step 3). |

Not reachable: `known_hosts`, RDP pins, device folder files, sync key and hub secret.

*7. Script in the webview (only if one ever runs: a bundle or dependency compromise, or an injection bug)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vault secrets | Call vault reveal while the vault is unlocked; keep it unlocked forever by calling `note_activity`. | Secrets reach the webview only through reveal; reveal needs an unlocked vault. | Accepted (assumptions 4 and 5). **Weak (known leftover):** the idle-lock thread has no `catch_unwind`, so a panic there stops the auto-lock silently. |
| Sync key and hub secret | Call `sync_pairing_code`. | Nothing beyond assumption 4. | **Weak:** `sync_pairing_code` needs no unlocked vault and no other proof, so it hands over the sync key whenever sync is set up. |
| Local clipboard | Read it with `clipboard_read_text`, no gesture. | 1 MiB cap. | Accepted by design (M10). **Weak (known leftover):** the `arboard` read has no timeout. |
| `connections.json`, `known_hosts`, RDP pins, `open-tabs.json` | Change or delete anything; answer host key and certificate prompts; open sessions to any valid host (quick connect, with the agent); leave or re-seed sync. | Rust validates every input (`validate_quick`, `validate_host`, `TargetView`, `MAX_TABS`, tree rules); no file path from the webview reaches a writer (paths come from native dialogs; `FERNJO_DIALOG_FILE` only in debug builds). | Accepted (assumption 4). |
| Leaking what it read | Send it out. | CSP `default-src 'self'` with `connect-src` only for IPC, no `unsafe-eval`; nav guard; text-only rendering (no `{@html}` or `innerHTML` in `ui/src`); `localStorage` holds no secret. | **Weak:** `open_external_url` opens any `http(s)` URL in the default browser, and WebView2 sends one blind GET for a cancelled top-level navigation, so a script can put what it read into a URL. **Weak (M11 review #52, accepted for now):** `sync_leave` (when paired) and then `sync_setup` with the script's own `https://` hub and any CA PEM upload the whole tree and `vault.fernjo` at once, the background runner keeps pushing every later change there after the script is gone, and `sync_pairing_code` hands the script the sync key that opens those envelopes: a persistent bulk channel, not only URL-sized leaks. The vault inside stays under the master password. Mitigation pending the owner's decision: a native confirmation that names the hub host for `sync_setup` and `sync_join` (recommended by the review), so a script cannot point sync elsewhere silently. `tauri_plugin_opener::init()` injects an init script (known leftover). |

Not reachable directly: device folder files (only through commands).

*8. Another local user on the same machine (not an administrator)*

| Asset | What it can do | What stops it | Accepted, weak or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vault secrets, sync key and hub secret | — | Keychain items are per user (Windows item with `Local` persistence); the vault file is encrypted. | — |
| Data folder files | Read or plant files where the folder lets it. | Unix: files 0600, folders created 0700; Windows: the inherited ACL of the folder (`%APPDATA%` is per user). | **Weak:** on Windows fernjo sets no ACL of its own, and on Unix an already existing folder keeps its mode, so a data folder chosen in a shared place is as open as that place. |
| Device folder files, `open-tabs.json`, RDP pins | — | Per-user `%LOCALAPPDATA%` / XDG data dir; 0600 on Unix. | — |
| SSH agent | Squat the OpenSSH pipe name (`\\.\pipe\openssh-ssh-agent`) or answer as Pageant while the real agent is not running. | Nothing in fernjo's `agent::connect` (`crates/fernjo-ssh/src/agent.rs` connects by name); whether russh's pipe and Pageant clients check who serves them is unchecked. Such an agent never gets the user's keys; it sees the sign requests (user name, server session) and can offer its own keys. | Open question for reviewer 2. |
| The webview | Attach to the DevTools port. | The port exists only when `WEBVIEW2_ADDITIONAL_BROWSER_ARGUMENTS` sets it (the debugging recipe); it listens on loopback, which other local users can reach. | Accepted for debugging runs only. |
| The hub host | Read the volume. | Rootless Podman volume owned by the hub user; `config.json` holds a hash; envelopes are encrypted. | — |

**Rationale:** one table per adversary keeps each reviewer's question ("what can this attacker reach, and what is in the way?") on one screen, and naming the weak cells avoids reviewers re-deriving them or assuming protections that do not exist.
**Rejected alternatives:** one assets-by-adversaries grid (64 cells, too wide to read; the ids C5, C6, V1 and V3 used in the threat model are merge-rule ids in `Docs/sync-format.md`, not review ids); describing only the M9 and M10 additions (the reviewers review all of 0.3.0).

## 2026-11-24 – M11: one set of hardened file helpers (`fernjo-fs`)

**Context:** the atomic write (random `create_new` temp name, fsync, `.bak`, rename, directory fsync) and the capped read existed as private copies in `fernjo-core` (`persist`), `fernjo-vault`, `fernjo-ssh` (`known_hosts`), `fernjo-rdp` (`CertStore`), `fernjo-sync` (`files.rs`), `fernjo-hub` (`store.rs`) and `src-tauri` (`app_menu.rs`, the reader in `open_tabs.rs`), because those crates must not depend on one another (see "M9 follow-up candidate: one set of hardened file helpers"). The copies had drifted (`persist` still used a fixed `<file>.tmp` name), and every copy that keeps a `.bak` wrote it with `fs::copy(path, <file>.bak)`, which follows a symlink planted at the `.bak` name (a sync tool or another local user could point it at any file the user can write). Spec: `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-24-m11-hardening-security-design.md`, "Step 2: `fernjo-fs`".
**Decision:** (1) A new workspace crate `crates/fernjo-fs` that depends only on `std` and `getrandom`, never on Tauri, and may be used by every crate including `fernjo-hub` (so "hub never depends on sync" and the protocol crates' independence still hold). It exports `read_capped(path, max)` (length checked before reading, read bounded by `take(max + 1)`, oversize is `InvalidData` "the file is too large"; each caller keeps its own cap constant and maps the error to its existing rules), `write_atomic(path, bytes, WriteOptions)` and `with_suffix`. (2) `WriteOptions { parent, backup, file_mode }` holds exactly what differs between the callers today: `Parent::MustExist` (the hub store), `CreatePrivate` (missing folders 0700 on Unix: core, vault, `known_hosts`, RDP pins, sync) or `CreateDefault` (export files and `app_menu.rs`); an existing folder's mode is never changed. `backup` (yes for `connections.json`, `settings.json`, `vault.fernjo`, `known_hosts`, the RDP pin file). `FileMode::OwnerOnly` (0600 on Unix) or `Default` (`app_menu.rs`, export files). `WriteOptions::data_file()` is `CreatePrivate`, backup, `OwnerOnly`. (3) **The `.bak` name is never opened for writing:** the current file is copied into a second fresh `create_new` temp file in the same folder, synced, and renamed onto `<file>.bak`. A rename replaces a symlink at the target instead of following it, so a planted `x.bak -> victim` is replaced by a regular file and `victim` is untouched. (4) Every failure removes the temp files the call created and leaves the target as it was; the directory fsync after the rename is best effort and never fails the write. (5) The temp file creation takes its candidate names from a generator inside the crate, so the test `a_symlink_at_a_temp_name_is_never_followed` can force a planted name; the random names come from `getrandom` (8 bytes, 16 hex digits, up to 8 attempts). The callers move onto the crate in M11 Tasks 3 to 5 and keep their behaviour otherwise (trailing newline of `persist`, `.bak` presence, file modes, the vault's stale temp sweep). The open-tabs writer now creates a missing device folder private (0700 on Unix) like the other device-folder writers; before it used the default mode, so the folder's mode depended on which writer ran first.
**Rationale:** one implementation means one fix and one set of tests; the copies had already drifted once. Copying into a temp file and renaming keeps the `.bak` step as atomic as the main write and needs no platform-specific "open without following links" flag (`O_NOFOLLOW` covers only the last path component and does not exist on Windows).
**Rejected alternatives:** putting the helpers into `fernjo-core::persist` (the vault, SSH, RDP and hub crates would then depend on the model crate); keeping the copies and fixing the `.bak` step five times; `fs::copy` after checking `symlink_metadata` (a race between the check and the copy); removing the `.bak` name before copying to it (a second race, and a moment without any `.bak`).

**Update 2026-11-25 (M11 security review #21):** the steps above covered the `.tmp` and `.bak` names only; a symlink at the **target name itself** was still followed by `read_capped` (`File::open`) and by the `.bak` copy, so a writer of the synced data folder could point `known_hosts` (or `settings.json`) at `~/.ssh/id_ed25519` and the next write copied the key into a regular `known_hosts` and `known_hosts.bak` in the synced folder. Now (1) `read_capped` and `keep_backup` open through one function, `open_regular`: `symlink_metadata` must report a regular file (every platform; on Windows a symlink or junction is refused, other reparse points such as OneDrive placeholders are regular files as `std` reports them, so a data folder in OneDrive keeps working), then on Unix the open itself uses `O_NOFOLLOW | O_NONBLOCK` (a link swapped in after the check fails with `ELOOP`; a FIFO or device swapped in does not block the open; the flag has no effect on reading a regular file, so it is not cleared, which would need `unsafe` `fcntl`), and the opened handle's metadata must report a regular file again (the race-free part on Unix; on Windows the check-then-open stays a race, and creating a symlink there needs admin rights or developer mode). (2) The refusal is `io::Error::other("not a regular file (a symbolic link, a folder or a special file)")`: never `InvalidData` (the callers reserve that kind for oversize) and never `NotFound`, also for a dangling link, so no caller treats a link as a missing file. (3) **The writer refuses** a target that exists and is not a regular file (symlink, dangling symlink, folder, FIFO) with the same error before it creates anything, for every `WriteOptions` (also without `.bak`, also the export files of `write_exact`): a link is neither followed nor silently replaced by a regular file; the `.bak` step's own `open_regular` still covers a link swapped in after that check. A missing target or an error from that check (missing parent, no permission) is left to the steps after it, as before. (4) Effect per caller, none of them turns the refusal into data loss, because the reader and the writer refuse under the same condition (no "read as empty, then overwrite"): `connections.json` and `settings.json` load as unreadable (`load_failed`, `settings_unusable`, never saved over), `vault.fernjo` is `Unusable`, the sync state files are `state_unusable`, `known_hosts` fails the connect with `Io`, `known_hosts.confirmed` counts as "not confirmed" (asks again), `rdp_certs.confirmed` fails the RDP connect with `Io`, `vault-marks.json` and `open-tabs.json` read as empty and their (best effort) writes fail too, the hub's `head.env` read is an I/O error, an export onto a symlinked path fails with the error. A user who deliberately symlinked a single data file (for example `known_hosts` to `~/.ssh/known_hosts`) now gets that error; linking the whole data folder (a link in a folder above the file) still works, `O_NOFOLLOW` and the check cover the last component only. (5) `fernjo-fs` gains one dependency, `libc` (Unix only, the version already in `Cargo.lock` through `getrandom`), for the two open flags; `std` has no portable constant for them. Hard links are not covered (a hard link is a regular file; sync tools do not carry them between devices, and on Linux `protected_hardlinks` stops linking another user's file). The paragraph "Rationale" above ("needs no platform-specific open flag") is superseded for the read side. Tests: `read_capped_refuses_what_is_not_a_regular_file` and `write_refuses_a_target_that_is_not_a_regular_file` (a folder at the name; every platform) and the Unix tests `a_symlink_at_the_target_is_not_copied_into_bak`, `read_capped_does_not_follow_a_symlink_at_the_target` (also a dangling link and a link to `/dev/zero`), `read_capped_refuses_a_fifo_without_blocking` and `the_writer_refuses_a_fifo_at_the_target_without_blocking` (first run in CI on Linux).

**Update 2026-11-25 (M11 security review #21, #40): `known_hosts` reads.** `KnownHosts::read` (`known_hosts` and `known_hosts.confirmed`) now goes through `fernjo_fs::read_capped` with `MAX_KNOWN_HOSTS_LEN` (16 MiB: a line is about 100 to 750 bytes, so tens of thousands of hosts fit, more than any hand-kept or managed file, while the whole file is still read and scanned on every connect in a fraction of a second), so it inherits the no-follow and regular-file checks and closes the known item "`known_hosts` read uncapped". A larger, symlinked or special file is `Io`, which the handler already turns into a refused connect; nothing is written (`modify` reads first). The file I/O of a connect (`stored_algorithms` before the TCP connect, `check` in `decide` and `decide_synced`, the trust writes in `apply_pending_trust`, now `async`) runs through `known_hosts::blocking` (`tokio::task::spawn_blocking`), so a slow or hung file system holds a thread of tokio's blocking pool instead of one of the shared runtime's async workers; the `check` inside the handshake is still bounded by the handshake deadline, the others are not given an extra timeout (a share that hangs for ever keeps that one connect waiting, which is the "owner: accept?" part of review row 40; decided 2026-11-25: accepted by the owner, see "M11: owner decisions on the security review (2026-11-25)"). A FIFO is refused before it is opened (the regular-file check), so it no longer blocks at all. Tests: `an_oversized_known_hosts_is_refused_and_left_alone`, `a_folder_at_the_known_hosts_name_is_an_error_not_an_empty_file`, and the Unix tests `add_over_a_symlinked_known_hosts_does_not_copy_the_link_target` and `a_fifo_at_the_known_hosts_name_is_refused_without_blocking` (first run in CI on Linux).

**Update 2026-11-25 (M11 security review #49): hub `head.env`.** `HubStore::read_head_unlocked` reads `head.env` through `fernjo_fs::read_capped` with the hub's own `MAX_ENVELOPE_LEN` (64 MiB, the limit `put` and the HTTP body reader already enforce, so a larger head was never written by the hub; `fernjo-hub` still does not depend on `fernjo-sync`). A larger file is an `InvalidData` I/O error for `head` and `put` (nothing is written, the file is left alone for the hub admin), and the no-follow and regular-file checks apply. Test: `an_oversized_head_file_is_refused_and_left_alone`.

**Update 2026-11-25 (M11 security review #80): the data-folder copy and the vault header check.** `copy_atomic` in `src-tauri/src/data_folder.rs` (choosing a new data folder copies `connections.json` and `vault.fernjo` there) was the last raw file path: `fs::copy` followed a symlink at the source name (a link to a private key in the current data folder would be copied into the new folder as a regular file) and wrote through the fixed temp name `<file>.fernjo-copy.tmp` with create and truncate (a link or hard link planted there in a synced target folder had any user-writable file overwritten with connections or vault bytes). It now reads the source with `fernjo_fs::read_capped` (cap `fernjo_core::MAX_DATA_FILE_LEN` for `connections.json`, `fernjo_vault::MAX_FILE_LEN` for the vault) and writes with `fernjo_fs::write_atomic` (`Parent::MustExist`, the target folder was checked to exist; no `.bak`, the target holds neither file; `FileMode::OwnerOnly`, the mode `fs::copy` carried over from the 0600 sources). The rest is unchanged: only files that exist (`try_exists`; a dangling link is skipped, nothing is disclosed) are copied, an error is `copy_failed` naming the file and rolls back what was placed, and "already existed" in the target is decided as before. `fernjo_fs::open_regular` is now public, and the vault's `check_unchanged_on_disk` opens the file through it for its 34-byte header read, so a FIFO swapped in at `vault.fernjo` no longer blocks a save (it fails with `save_failed`, "not a regular file …"). Tests: `a_file_planted_at_the_old_temp_name_is_never_written` (a hard link at the old temp name; before the fix the linked file was overwritten with the vault bytes), `a_failing_copy_rolls_back_the_files_placed_earlier` (now a folder at the vault's source name), `a_save_over_a_folder_at_the_vault_name_is_refused_as_not_a_regular_file`, and the Unix tests `a_symlink_at_a_source_name_is_not_copied`, `a_symlink_at_the_old_temp_name_is_not_followed` and `a_save_over_a_fifo_at_the_vault_name_fails_without_blocking` (first run in CI on Linux).

**Update 2026-11-25 (final M11 review, Minor 2): stale temp files.** The random temp names leave a `<file>.<16 hex>.tmp` behind when a save is cut off (a crash, a power loss), and before only the vault removed its own; in the synced data folder a sync tool would replicate the rest. `fernjo_fs::sweep_stale_temps(path, age)` removes the siblings named exactly `<file name>.<16 lowercase hex>.tmp` that are regular files (judged by `symlink_metadata`, so a symlink is never followed and a FIFO, a folder or any other name stays) and older than `age`; every error is ignored. The callers use `fernjo_fs::STALE_TEMP_AGE` (one day, far longer than any save): `Store::open` (`connections.json`), `settings::load`, `Vault::open` (its own sweep now calls the shared one), and the write paths of `KnownHosts` (`known_hosts`, `known_hosts.confirmed`; under `WRITE_LOCK` on the blocking pool) and `CertStore` (the RDP pin file), which have no open step of their own. Tests: `fernjo-fs` `sweep_removes_only_old_temp_files_of_the_named_file`, `sweep_keeps_an_old_symlink_and_an_old_fifo_with_a_temp_name` (Unix), `store::tests::open_removes_stale_temp_files_of_connections_json`, `settings::tests::load_removes_stale_temp_files_of_settings_json`, the vault test `open_removes_vault_temp_files_older_than_a_day_and_keeps_newer_and_foreign_files`.

## 2026-11-24 – M11 RDP channel message cap

**Context:** IronRDP reassembles a chunked static channel message (`ironrdp-svc` 0.10.0, `ChunkProcessor::dechunkify`) and a fragmented dynamic channel message (`ironrdp-dvc` 0.10.0, `CompleteData`) into a buffer that grows chunk by chunk up to the total the server announces: the `u32` `length` of the channel PDU header in the first chunk, or the `length` of a DVC "data first" PDU. Nothing caps that total, so a hostile server could make the client buffer up to 4 GiB. Worse, `dechunkify` skips every length check for a chunk flagged `PACKET_COMPRESSED`, so a sequence of compressed chunks is not bounded even by the announced total (`ironrdp-session` does not decompress static channel data either). Spec: `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-24-m11-hardening-security-design.md`, "RDP static channel cap".
**Decision:** a pre-check in the read loop of `crates/fernjo-rdp/src/session.rs` (`process_frame`, before `ActiveStage::process`; module `session/channel_cap.rs`, `check_frame`) ends the session with `CloseReason::ChannelTooLarge` (end text "The server sent a channel message that is too large.") when, on a static channel (the ids come from the connection's `StaticChannelSet`; the I/O and message channels are not static channels): (1) a first chunk announces more than `MAX_CHANNEL_MESSAGE` (4 MiB); (2) any chunk is flagged `PACKET_COMPRESSED`: fernjo negotiates no compression (`compression_type: None` in `connect.rs`, every channel `CompressionCondition::Never`), so a compliant server never sends one; (3) on the drdynvc channel, a chunk that starts a message carries a DVC "data first" whose total is above 4 MiB, or is shorter than the longest "data first" header (9 bytes) while more of the message follows, because a header split over chunks would hide its total from the check. Everything else, and anything the check cannot decode, passes; IronRDP reports its own errors. Fast-path frames pass this channel check (they are judged by the display update caps, see the extension below).
**Rationale:** 4 MiB is well above the largest legitimate message (1 MiB of clipboard text is about 2 MiB of UTF-16) and small enough that a hostile announcement cannot hurt. A check before the stage needs no change to IronRDP and refuses before any reassembly buffer exists.
**Rejected alternatives:** patching or forking IronRDP; a stateful per-channel byte counter (more state for the same result once compressed chunks are refused); passing compressed chunks on (their growth is unbounded).
**Extension (M11 review #25, #26, 2026-11-25): display update caps.** Two more IronRDP structures grow from server input without a bound of their own (`ironrdp-session` 0.13.0, `fast_path.rs`): `CompleteData` appends every `Fragmentation::Next` and `Last` of a fragmented fast-path update to one `Vec` (`append_data`) and never compares the total with the `MultifragmentUpdate` size the client confirmed; `PointerCache` is a `HashMap` that takes any `u16` cache index, and `decode_pointer` produces width x height x 4 bytes whatever the wire size (the review's probe: a 31,760-byte pointer PDU became a 1,015,808-byte bitmap; slow-path pointers reach the same `process_pointer_update`). A second module of the same pre-check, `session/update_cap.rs` (`UpdateCheck`, called from `process_frame` right after `check_frame`; it keeps state, so `drive` owns one and resets it with `reactivated` after a deactivation-reactivation, when IronRDP builds a new fast-path processor), ends the session with the new `CloseReason::UpdateOutOfLimits` (end text "The server sent a display update beyond the limits of this client.") when:
- a fast-path fragment takes the update being reassembled above `fragment_cap(width, height)` = the larger of `MIN_FRAGMENTED_UPDATE` (8 MiB) and twice a raw 32-bit frame of the current desktop (8 x width x height bytes). The state mirrors `CompleteData::process_data`: `First` and `Single` drop a pending update (`check_data_is_empty`), `Next` and `Last` add their data length, `Last` ends the count, a fragment IronRDP refuses (a `Next` without a sequence, another update code, a different compression presence, compressed data without a decompressor) is left to IronRDP, which ends the session, and an update that does not decode clears the count (IronRDP discards the pending update with the rest of the frame on a truncated visual update, or ends the session). The data counted is the wire data, which is what IronRDP buffers because fernjo negotiates no bulk compression (no decompressor, so a compressed update ends the session in IronRDP).
- a pointer update that carries a cache index or a size (color, new, large and cached pointer; fast path, and slow path on the I/O channel, where the check walks concatenated share control PDUs like `x224::Processor::process_io_channel_data_indication`) has a cache index at or above `POINTER_CACHE_SIZE` (32) or a width or height above `MAX_POINTER_SIDE` (384); the first fragment of a fragmented pointer update must hold the whole pointer header (the same rule as the split DVC header), a whole update too short for it passes (IronRDP ignores or refuses it).
**Why these numbers:** IronRDP confirms `MultifragmentUpdate { max_request_size: 8 MiB }` only when the server proposes none; otherwise it echoes the server's own value (`ironrdp-connector` 0.12.0 `create_client_confirm_active` keeps the server's set), which a hostile server controls, so the confirmed size is no bound and the cap is fernjo's own. A server that proposes a size sizes it for one whole screen update (MS-RDPRFX requires the client to accept at least the server's value; FreeRDP's server proposes 16 KiB per 64 x 64 tile plus 16 KiB), and twice the raw frame covers that for every desktop of at least 200 x 200 while the 8 MiB floor covers smaller ones. The cap equals the memory of the two copies of the desktop the session already holds (decoded image and framebuffer): 8 MiB up to about 1280 x 800, 16 MiB at 1920 x 1080, 63 MiB at 3840 x 2160, 270 MiB at the framebuffer's largest desktop (8192 x 4320). The cap bounds the bytes counted, not the allocation: IronRDP's reassembly buffer grows by `Vec` doubling, so its capacity can reach about twice the cap (about 512 MiB at the largest desktop, 16 MiB at the 8 MiB floor). The client advertises a pointer cache of 32 entries (`DEFAULT_POINTER_CACHE_SIZE`, both `color_pointer_cache_size` and `pointer_cache_size`) and large pointers up to 384 x 384 (`LargePointerSupportFlags::UP_TO_384X384_PIXELS`, MS-RDPBCGR 2.2.9.1.2.1.11). MS-RDPBCGR allows only 96 pixels for color and new pointers; 384 is applied to every kind so a lax server is not cut off, and 32 entries of 384 x 384 hold at most 18 MiB.
**Tests:** `session::update_cap::tests` (`a_fragmented_update_above_the_cap_is_refused`, `a_fragmented_update_below_the_cap_passes_and_the_count_starts_again_after_it`, `first_and_single_drop_the_pending_update_like_ironrdp`, `fragments_ironrdp_refuses_are_left_to_it`, `every_update_of_a_frame_is_counted`, `the_cap_follows_the_desktop_and_a_reactivation_ends_the_pending_update`, `the_cap_is_eight_mebibytes_or_twice_the_raw_desktop`, `a_fast_path_pointer_outside_the_cache_is_refused`, `a_fast_path_pointer_above_384_pixels_is_refused`, `a_fragmented_pointer_is_judged_by_its_first_fragment`, `a_slow_path_pointer_outside_the_limits_is_refused`, `every_concatenated_share_pdu_is_checked`, `a_compressed_update_is_left_to_ironrdp`) and, through a real `ActiveStage`, `session::tests::an_endless_fragmented_update_ends_the_session_at_the_cap` and `a_pointer_outside_the_cache_ends_the_session_and_one_inside_reaches_the_stage`. A real server is an owner check (`FERNJO_TEST_RDP_ADDR`). An upstream report to IronRDP is worth filing.
**Rejected alternatives:** the 8 MiB constant alone (the review's suggestion; it would end legitimate sessions of servers that propose more for a large desktop, which IronRDP then confirms); the server's confirmed size (attacker-controlled); 96 pixels for color and new pointers (spec-exact, but ends the session of a lax server for no memory gain worth it); dropping a refused pointer update instead of ending the session (needs rewriting frames before IronRDP sees them).

## 2026-11-24 – M11 RDP TLS: own verifier, strict handshake signature

**Context:** "M8 RDP TLS handshake signature check" kept the handshake signature check by running `ironrdp_tls::upgrade_with_certificate_validation_callback_for_endpoint` with an accept-all callback over rustls's strict `WebPkiServerVerifier`. `ironrdp-tls` 0.3.0 builds that verifier from the platform root store (`rustls_verifier::platform_root_certificates`, `rustls_native_certs`) and refuses to build it when the store holds no usable root, so a machine without root certificates got `tls_failed` for every host. `ironrdp-tls` has no way to pass an own `ServerCertVerifier` (its `rustls_client_config` takes only the policy and the callback), takes the crypto provider from the process default (`ClientConfig::builder()`) and sets a `KeyLogFile`. Spec: `Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-24-m11-hardening-security-design.md`, "RDP TLS" and owner decision 5. This entry supersedes the verifier part of the M8 entry; the order of the connect (TLS, pin, prompts, CredSSP) and the public key extraction for the pin and CredSSP are unchanged.
**Decision:** `crates/fernjo-rdp/src/tls.rs` builds the rustls client itself. `tls::upgrade(stream, host)` uses `ClientConfig::builder_with_provider(ring::default_provider())` (explicit ring provider, never the process default), TLS 1.2 and 1.3, no client certificate, no session resumption, no key log, and `PinVerifier` (`rustls::client::danger::ServerCertVerifier`). `verify_server_cert` returns `ServerCertVerified::assertion()` for any chain, name and validity (the pin decides after the handshake, as before). `verify_tls12_signature` and `verify_tls13_signature` delegate to `rustls::crypto::verify_tls12_signature` / `verify_tls13_signature` with the provider's `signature_verification_algorithms`; `supported_verify_schemes` comes from the same table. No other crypto code. When a signature check fails, `PinVerifier` sets a flag if the cause is the certificate: rustls's own `ParsedCertificate::try_from` fails, the error is `UnsupportedSignatureAlgorithmForPublicKeyContext` and the certificate's SPKI algorithm (curve included) matches no `public_key_alg_id` of the provider's algorithms (`key_type_supported`, the comparison webpki itself makes), or it is `BadSignature` for an RSA key outside what ring verifies (read from the certificate with `x509-cert`, only to name the error). `upgrade` then returns `RdpError::CertificateUnsupported` (code `certificate_unsupported`, text "the server certificate is not supported") instead of `TlsFailed`, before any prompt. The classification only names the error; it never lets a handshake through. The rustls error is logged with `log::debug!` (it names alerts and certificate errors, never key material or credentials; the app installs no logger today, so the line stays silent until one exists). `ironrdp-tls` is no longer a dependency; `rustls` 0.23.46, `tokio-rustls` 0.26.6 (features `ring`, `tls12`) and `log` 0.4.35 became direct dependencies of `fernjo-rdp` at the versions already in `Cargo.lock`, so no crate version was added (the lock only lost `ironrdp-tls`) and `cargo deny check` passes without a new ignore. Owner's choice of 2026-11-24: option A, a strict signature check through rustls's functions only; unusual old certificates fail with a distinct error.
**Confirmed by source read (rustls 0.23.46, rustls-webpki 0.103.17, ring 0.17.14):** both rustls functions parse the leaf with `webpki::EndEntityCert::try_from` (`Cert::from_der`, `UnknownExtensionPolicy::Strict`) and verify with `EndEntityCert::verify_signature` (`signed_data::verify_signature`) against the key in the certificate; `verify_tls12_signature` tries every algorithm the scheme maps to, `verify_tls13_signature` only the first; a scheme the client did not offer is `PeerMisbehaved::SignedHandshakeWithUnadvertisedSigScheme`. The parse does **not** check the certificate's own signature, issuer, validity, names or key usage (that is path building in webpki's `verify_cert.rs`, which `PinVerifier` never calls), so a SHA-1 (or MD5) signature *on* the certificate does not matter. Finding: with a non-empty root store the M8 path already let SHA-1-signed and Windows-like certificates through (the accept-all callback caught every chain error; both new fake-server tests pass against the old code on Windows); what really failed was the empty root store. Certificates that stay refused (the handshake fails before any prompt):
- not valid DER, trailing data or a bad time encoding: `BadDer` / `BadDerTime` / `TrailingData` in `Cert::from_input`, which rustls's `pki_error` maps to `CertificateError::BadEncoding`: `certificate_unsupported`;
- not version 3 (v1, v2): `version3` gives `UnsupportedCertVersion` (`CertificateError::Other`): `certificate_unsupported`;
- inner and outer signature algorithm differ: `SignatureAlgorithmMismatch` in `Cert::from_input`: `certificate_unsupported`;
- an extension marked critical that webpki does not know (any OID outside `2.5.29.x`, or a `2.5.29.x` other than key usage 15, subject alt name 17, basic constraints 19, name constraints 30, CRL distribution points 31, extended key usage 37): `remember_cert_extension` / `remember_extension` call `Extension::unsupported`, which gives `UnsupportedCriticalExtension`: `certificate_unsupported` (unknown non-critical extensions are fine);
- a known extension twice or with a malformed value: `set_extension_once` gives `ExtensionValueInvalid`, or `BadDer`: `certificate_unsupported`;
- an RSA key under 2048 or over 8192 bits, or a public exponent that is even or outside 3 to 2^33 - 1: ring's `RSA_PKCS1_2048_8192_*` / `RSA_PSS_2048_8192_*` (`verify_rsa_`, `PublicModulus::from_be_bytes`, `PublicExponent::from_be_bytes`) return `Unspecified`, which webpki reports as `InvalidSignatureForPublicKey` and rustls as `BadSignature`; fernjo's `rsa_key_out_of_range` names it `certificate_unsupported`;
- a key type the ring provider has no algorithm for (DSA, ECDSA P-521, Ed448, an `id-RSASSA-PSS` key): `signed_data::verify_signature` gives `UnsupportedSignatureAlgorithmForPublicKeyContext`, which is `certificate_unsupported` only because the certificate's key type matches none of the provider's algorithms (`key_type_supported`); usually the server finds no common scheme in the client's list and aborts, which the client sees as an alert, so `tls_failed`. The same webpki error for a key type the provider does support (an RSA certificate with an ECDSA signature) is what an impostor produces when it replays a public certificate and signs with its own key, so it stays `tls_failed` (fix round 1 of the task review; tests `a_signature_of_another_key_type_than_the_certificate_is_not_called_unsupported` and `an_impostor_signing_with_another_key_type_fails_with_tls_failed_before_any_prompt`).
Unchanged from before: TLS 1.0 and 1.1 and cipher suites without ECDHE and AEAD are not offered (rustls), so such hosts get `tls_failed`.
**Tests:** `crates/fernjo-rdp/tests/connect.rs` with the fake server `Behaviour::TlsWithCert(Fixture)` and the OpenSSL fixtures in `crates/fernjo-rdp/tests/fixtures/` (commands in its `README.md`): `a_windows_like_rsa_certificate_reaches_the_certificate_decision`, `a_sha1_signed_certificate_reaches_the_certificate_decision`, `a_certificate_webpki_cannot_parse_fails_with_certificate_unsupported_before_any_prompt`; `an_impostor_signing_with_another_key_type_fails_with_tls_failed_before_any_prompt` (fake server `Behaviour::ImpersonatedWithCert`: the RSA fixture certificate, an ECDSA key); `tests/no_root_store.rs` (own binary, empty `SSL_CERT_FILE`, which `rustls-native-certs` then reads instead of the platform store) `a_windows_like_certificate_reaches_the_certificate_decision_with_an_empty_root_store`, which fails on the M8 code with `tls_failed`; the M8 guard `a_server_that_cannot_sign_for_its_certificate_fails_the_handshake_before_any_prompt` is unchanged and fails when `PinVerifier` accepts every signature. Unit tests in `tls.rs`: `the_verifier_needs_no_root_store`, `a_genuine_handshake_signature_passes_for_tls12_and_tls13`, `a_forged_handshake_signature_fails_and_is_not_called_unsupported`, `a_certificate_webpki_cannot_parse_is_marked_unsupported`, `an_rsa_key_under_2048_bits_is_marked_unsupported`, `a_key_type_the_provider_cannot_verify_with_is_marked_unsupported` (ECDSA P-521 fixture), `a_signature_of_another_key_type_than_the_certificate_is_not_called_unsupported`, `key_types_are_compared_with_the_provider_algorithms` (a rustls server cannot sign with a 1024-bit key, so that case has no fake-server test). A real Windows host stays an owner check (M11 Task 22, `FERNJO_TEST_RDP_ADDR`).
**Rejected alternatives:** option B, accepting the certificates above by verifying the handshake signature through a second crypto library (more crypto surface in the most sensitive spot of the RDP client for rare hosts; rejected by the owner); keeping `ironrdp-tls` and its root-store verifier (the empty-store failure stays); patching `ironrdp-tls` to take a verifier.

## 2026-11-25 – M11 session queue: minimum weight and resize coalescing

**Context:** the ordered writer queue of a session (`Shared::ops` in `src-tauri/src/sessions.rs`, the same `Op` queue for SSH and RDP) is an unbounded `tokio` channel. Since M10 it was capped only by bytes (`InputBudget`, `MAX_QUEUED_INPUT` 8 MiB): a one-byte write weighed 1, so a flood of tiny writes towards a session whose writer is stuck could queue millions of ops (each a queue node and an allocation), and `Op::Resize` weighed 0 and was queued once per `session_resize`, so dragging a window edge against a stuck writer grew the queue without any bound. The spec asks for a "bounded `ops` channel" (`Docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-24-m11-hardening-security-design.md`, "Step 3", RDP write timeout, and the M4/M10 leftover "`Resize` messages unbounded and the session input cap weighing payload bytes only").
**Decision:** (1) Every input op (`Data`, `Rdp`, `Clipboard`) weighs its length but at least `session_flow::MIN_OP_WEIGHT` (64), so the budget bounds the number of queued input ops to `MAX_QUEUED_INPUT / MIN_OP_WEIGHT` (131 072) as well as their bytes; above it the call is refused with `input_queue_full` and the session stays alive, as before. (2) Resizes are coalesced: `Op::Resize` carries no size, `Shared::resize_queued` (an `AtomicBool`) says one is in the queue. `SessionManager::resize` stores the size and, under the same `size` lock, sends `Op::Resize` only when the flag was clear (`swap(true)`); the writer (`take_resize`, both protocols) clears the flag and reads the **latest** size, again under the `size` lock, and applies that. Because flag and size change only together under one lock, every new size is either stored before the writer's `take_resize` (it reads it) or after it (the flag is clear, so a new `Op::Resize` is queued): the final size always reaches the session and the queue holds at most one resize (across a reconnect an old writer can still clear the new connection's flag, so one extra resize op can be queued: bounded, and no size is lost). `install_ops` puts the new sender in place at connect under the `ops` and `size` locks (order `ops` before `size`, as in `resize`), resets a flag a stopped writer left set, and queues a resize when the UI reported a size other than the one the connect used; this also closes a small old window in which a resize between that check and the sender being installed was lost. (3) `Op::Close` stays unweighted; it is sent once, when the session is forgotten. So the queue is bounded (at most 131 072 input ops, one resize and one close) while it stays an unbounded channel, whose `send` never waits: the senders are Tauri commands that may run on the UI thread, and a bounded channel's `blocking_send` (or a full `try_send` path per op kind) would either block that thread behind a stuck session or need its own refusal rules per op. This is how the spec's "bounded `ops` channel" is met. Together with `write_bounded` in `fernjo-rdp` (every RDP write has a timeout) a server that stops reading cannot grow the queue or keep a closing session alive.
**Tests:** `sessions::tests::a_flood_of_one_byte_writes_hits_the_cap_by_weight`, `a_resize_burst_queues_one_op_and_applies_the_last_size`, `a_resize_without_a_shell_queues_nothing_and_does_not_block_later_resizes`, `a_resize_during_the_connect_is_queued_when_the_shell_starts`, `sessions::rdp::tests::rdp_ops_weigh_at_least_the_minimum_and_resizes_are_coalesced`, and against the OpenSSH container `a_resize_burst_reaches_the_shell_with_the_last_size` (301 resizes, then `stty size` in the shell reports the last one; it fails when the writer does not clear the flag).
**Rejected alternatives:** a bounded `mpsc` channel (blocks or refuses at the sender for every op kind, including resize and close, which must never be refused); dropping intermediate resizes in the writer by draining the queue (the queue would still grow while the writer is stuck); a resize that carries its size and replaces a queued one in place (an unbounded channel cannot replace an element).

## 2026-11-25 – M11 SSH: close the socket when the handshake is left before the key exchange

**Context:** russh 0.65.1 (`Cargo.lock`; the plan said 0.65.2) `client::connect_stream` writes the client id, reads the server's id line and then spawns `Session::run` (`russh_util::runtime::spawn(session.run(stream, handler, Some(kex_done_signal)))`), which takes the stream; the `JoinHandle` reaches the caller only inside the returned `Handle`, after `kex_done_signal_rx` fired. Dropping the `connect_stream` future in between (fernjo's `with_handshake_deadline` returning `SshError::Timeout`, or the app aborting the connect task because the tab was closed) drops the `JoinHandle` without aborting the task. In `Session::run_inner` the command receiver (`self.receiver.recv()`, whose `None` would end the task) is polled only `if can_receive_outbound`, which is false while a key exchange is active, the keepalive only counts in the `Authenticated` state and fernjo sets no inactivity timeout, so against a server that sent its banner and went silent the task, its socket and its file descriptor stayed until the process ended. The tests `a_pre_key_exchange_timeout_leaves_no_session_task` and `a_cancelled_handshake_leaves_no_session_task` (`crates/fernjo-ssh/tests/timeout.rs`, a listener that sends a banner and then only reads) failed on the old code: the peer never saw the connection closed.
**Decision:** `Session::connect` takes a clone of the socket before the handshake (`TcpStream::into_std`, `std::net::TcpStream::try_clone`, `TcpStream::from_std`) and keeps it in `HandshakeSocketGuard`. Its `Drop` shuts the socket down (`Shutdown::Both`) unless the handshake returned, after which `disarm` drops the clone without a shutdown and the task belongs to the `Handle` (dropping a `Handle` ends the task the normal way, since the receiver is polled after the key exchange). The shutdown makes the task's pending read end, so `run` returns and drops the stream. It covers every way out of the handshake: the deadline, a cancelled connect task and handshake errors (where russh has already ended the task and the shutdown is a no-op). Since review #22 the guard (renamed `SocketGuard`) is no longer disarmed but kept for the whole session; see "M11 SSH: a server that stops reading, and channels the server opens".
**Rejected alternatives:** aborting the spawned task (its `JoinHandle` is not reachable before the key exchange); a wrapper stream with a cancel flag (more code for the same effect); setting russh's `inactivity_timeout` (it applies to the whole session, not only the handshake, and a cancelled connect would still wait for it to elapse).

## 2026-11-25 – M11 RDP clipboard default: stays on, accepted by the owner
